**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, deterministic algorithm to simulate large open quantum systems governed by the Lindblad equation. By exploiting the tridiagonal structure of the system Hamiltonian and employing ensemble rank truncation, the method achieves near-linear scaling with system size. This breakthrough significantly accelerates simulations for models like NV-cavity systems, making previously intractable dynamics accessible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable numerical tool for simulating complex open quantum dynamics in systems like cavity QED or NV centers, overcoming the exponential scaling bottleneck inherent in density matrix methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central obstacle in simulating large open quantum systems is the quadratic growth of the density matrix ($ho$) with the Hilbert-space dimension ($D$).

**Main result.** The authors introduce a deterministic algorithm that achieves memory and computational cost linear in $D$ for fixed low rank $R$, significantly accelerating simulations compared to existing tools like QuTiP.

**Method.** The method combines exploiting the tridiagonal structure of the Hamiltonian for coherent evolution (using split-operator steps) with Ensemble Rank Truncation (ERT) for the dissipative Lindblad evolution.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on open quantum systems governed by the Lindblad equation, specifically using a driven nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center coupled to a cavity.

**Key observables.** Expectation values $\langle O angle = 	ext{Tr}(Oho)$, and the method reproduces the QuTiP quadrature-variance evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The retained ensemble rank ($R$) is crucial; the method is most efficient when the system structure allows for tridiagonalization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the Hamiltonian and collapse operators can be ordered such that they are diagonal or tridiagonal. The efficiency relies on the ability to truncate the ensemble rank $R$ while maintaining accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general ERT propagation algorithm for open systems with sparse structure, detailing the split-operator approach for unitary evolution and the Gram matrix compression for the dissipative map.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quadratic growth of the density matrix with Hilbert-space dimension D is the central obstacle to simulating large open quantum systems. We introduce a deterministic algorithm that eliminates it for Lindblad dynamics whose Hamiltonian consists of a time-dependent diagonal part plus terms that are tridiagonal after reordering the basis. The state is a low-rank ensemble of vectors, propagated by tridiagonal split-operator steps and ensemble rank truncation of short-time Kraus branches, so that memory and cost per step are linear in D at fixed rank. For a driven nitrogen-vacancy-cavity model, rank 16 reproduces full density-matrix observables to relative error below $10^{-5}$, the method is up to two orders of magnitude faster than QuTiP already at $D \approx 500$, and its runtime scales nearly linearly.

</details>

**Summary.** This work unifies the understanding of slow dynamics in U(1) Lattice Gauge Theories by mapping them onto constrained fermionic models. The authors show that non-thermal behavior can be explained either as prethermalization (no $\theta$ term) or freezing due to an emergent Stark potential ($\theta$ term). This suggests that subdiffusive transport is a generic feature arising from emergent conservation laws in these systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical link between gauge field dynamics and fermionic models exhibiting non-equilibrium behavior, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum simulation results in Rydberg arrays.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the poorly understood origin of slow, non-thermal dynamics (such as subdiffusion or freezing) observed in one-dimensional U(1) Lattice Gauge Theories (LGTs).

**Main result.** The authors establish an exact analytical mapping from U(1) LGTs to non-local kinetically constrained fermionic models, showing that non-thermal behavior arises from proximity to integrable models or emergent conservation laws.

**Method.** The core method involves deriving a dual fermionic description by integrating out gauge degrees of freedom and analyzing the resulting Hamiltonian structure.

**Model / system.** The system is the U(1) LGT, which is mapped to a fermionic model featuring an emergent linear Stark potential. The analysis is performed on 1D systems, drawing parallels to Rydberg atom arrays.

**Key observables.** Electric-field correlations, subsystem charge fluctuations, and the decay profile of spin transport ($	ilde{C}_s(x, t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The topological $\theta$ angle, the constraint radius $R$, and the system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of an exact analytical mapping between the gauge theory and the constrained fermionic model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mapping from the Rydberg Hamiltonian to the constrained Wannier-Stark model, and show the finite-size scaling of charge fluctuations ($O_d(t)$) for different constraint radii $R$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the physical problem (slow dynamics in LGTs), then presenting the exact mapping to the fermionic model, and finally analyzing the resulting dynamics using conservation laws and scaling arguments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The dynamics of gauge theories is of fundamental interest, with low-dimensional systems displaying a rich phenomenology beyond the rapid thermalization typical of QCD and non-Abelian theories. In particular, recent quantum simulation experiments have reported robust and unexpected signatures of slow relaxation in generic regimes and at large volumes in one-dimensional U(1) lattice gauge theories (LGTs), whose origin remains poorly understood. Here, we present a framework that provides a unified perspective on such slow dynamics and establishes a new route for exploring subdiffusive fractonic hydrodynamics in Rydberg experiments. The backbone of our theory is an exact analytical mapping from U(1) LGTs with a topological $θ$ angle to non-local kinetically constrained fermionic models with an emergent linear Stark potential. In the absence of the $θ$ term, this framework interprets the non-thermal dynamics reported in recent experiments as prethermal behavior arising from proximity to an integrable free-fermion model. For non-zero $θ$, the emergent Stark potential instead explains the freezing dynamics observed in other recent experiments. We argue that this correspondence could also lead to subdiffusive transport due to emergent conservation laws. We predict that these phenomena arise in a much broader class of models, and support this prediction with numerical simulations and proposals for future experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a field-theoretical approach using Keldysh path integrals to calculate the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for sensing in open quantum systems. By employing a semiclassical approximation, the authors derive a tractable formula for the QFI applicable to large, complex models like time crystals and atomic arrays. This provides a crucial tool for assessing quantum advantage in realistic, dissipative quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable theoretical tool (the semiclassical Keldysh path integral) to quantify quantum sensing advantages in realistic, open quantum systems relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of calculating the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for quantum enhanced sensing in complex, driven-dissipative many-body quantum systems.

**Main result.** They derive a tractable, semiclassical expression for the QFI by linking it to a Keldysh path-integral formalism, which is applicable to large systems where exact methods fail.

**Method.** The core methodology involves mapping the QFI calculation onto a Keldysh path integral and then employing a semiclassical approximation by expanding the action and evaluating the QFI via the variance of the classical action.

**Model / system.** The study benchmarks its method on three systems: a driven-dissipative harmonic oscillator, a boundary time crystal (BTC), and a two-dimensional array of collectively emitting atoms.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), $I_\vartheta$; the semiclassical QFI, $I_{	ext{sc},\vartheta}$; and the system's occupation number $n(t)$ for the oscillator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pumping rates ($\gamma_\uparrow/\gamma_\downarrow$), driving strengths ($\Omega$), system size ($N$), and the ratio of driving to decay rates ($\Omega/\Gamma_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the validity of the semiclassical approximation, which involves truncating the action expansion and is known to fail for weak driving in the atomic array case.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the semiclassical versus exact QFI rates for the harmonic oscillator, BTC, and 2D atomic array, demonstrating agreement under certain parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QFI for open systems, develops the Keldysh path-integral formulation, derives the semiclassical approximation, and then applies this framework to benchmark calculations on the harmonic oscillator, BTC, and 2D atomic array.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven-dissipative many-body quantum systems can be exploited in quantum enhanced sensing protocols. Here, non-classical correlations may allow one to reach a measurement precision that surpasses the standard quantum limit. The relevant figure of merit for assessing such quantum advantage is the quantum Fisher information (QFI). In a many-body setting its computation is challenging as it requires knowledge of the full system-environment state. Here we make use of a field-theoretical approach, which is particularly well suited for systems with many degrees of freedom. In particular, we show that the QFI can be linked to a Keldysh path-integral. This route leads to a semiclassical expression of the QFI, which is obtained through a controlled expansion in the so- called quantum fields. We benchmark the framework on three systems of increasing complexity: the driven-dissipative harmonic oscillator, the boundary time crystal - which becomes semiclassical at large system sizes - and a two-dimensional array of collectively emitting atoms. We assess in which parameter regimes the semiclassical description holds and gives access to system sizes beyond the reach of exact methods.

</details>

**Highlighted author(s):** John Preskill  
**Authors:** Senrui Chen, Antonio Anna Mele, Francesco Anna Mele, John Preskill  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26705v1>

Note that each measurement may be chosen adaptively based on outcomes of previous measurements.</figcaption>

The vertical axis denotes the sample complexity for learning to ε trace distance with 2/3 probability using single-copy (i.e. non-entangled) measurements. The horizontal axis denotes a minimal-temperature parameter ν such that the family of Gaussian states is promised to satisfy Σ ≥( 1</figcaption>

</figure>

**Summary.** This work determines the conditions under which the quantum state of a bosonic field can be estimated using classical learning resources. It finds a sharp crossover: states near vacuum (cold) are fundamentally hard to learn, requiring exponentially more samples than classical states, while states with significant thermal excitation (warm) behave classically in terms of sample complexity.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative link between the physical concept of thermal excitation (a key concept in open quantum systems and quantum optics) and the computational difficulty of state estimation, which is highly relevant for quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental question of when bosonic Gaussian states become 'classical to learn,' comparing their sample complexity to that of classical Gaussian distributions.

**Main result.** Bosonic Gaussian states are classical to learn if their thermal fluctuations are sufficiently large ($
u = \Omega(1)$), allowing the sample complexity to match the classical $\Theta(n^2)$ scaling.

**Method.** The analysis uses a learning-theoretic perspective, comparing the required sample complexity ($N$) for quantum state tomography (using single-copy measurements) against the known classical bounds.

**Model / system.** The system under study is the $n$-mode bosonic Gaussian state, characterized by its covariance matrix $\Sigma$ and governed by quantum field theory concepts.

**Key observables.** Sample complexity ($N$), covariance matrix ($\Sigma$), and the thermal fluctuation parameter ($
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The crossover is governed by the thermal fluctuation parameter $
u$: cold states ($\Sigma \approx I/2$) are hard to learn ($\Omega(n^3)$), while warm states ($
u > 0$) are easier ($\Theta(n^2)$ for $
u=\Omega(1)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes measurements are restricted to single-copy (non-entangled) measurements, which is a practical constraint in many sensing experiments.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference in sample complexity between entangled vs. non-entangled learning schemes. Figure 2 shows the sample complexity scaling $N$ as a function of the thermal parameter $
u$, demonstrating the crossover.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by defining the quantum-classical boundary in learnability. It then derives bounds showing $\Omega(n^3)$ complexity for cold states and $\Theta(n^2)$ complexity for warm states, culminating in a characterization of the crossover.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A fundamental question in physics is: When does classical behavior emerge from quantum systems? Bosonic Gaussian states provide a natural setting to explore this quantum-classical boundary, as they capture both the classical field behavior and the intrinsic quantum nature of light. Here, we address this problem from a learning-theoretic perspective by asking: When are bosonic Gaussian states classical to learn? That is, under what conditions (if any) can an n-mode bosonic Gaussian state be learned with as few samples, and with operations as simple, as are needed to learn a classical 2n-variate Gaussian distribution? We establish a smooth crossover in learnability governed by the state's thermal fluctuations:   - Cold Gaussian states are non-classical to learn: When the covariance matrix satisfies $Σ\le(\frac12+O(\frac1n))I$, i.e. close to the vacuum covariance, tomography under single-copy (i.e., non-entangled) measurements fundamentally requires $Ω(n^3)$ copies, strictly exceeding the sample complexity $Θ(n^2)$ of learning classical Gaussian distributions. We show that this hardness persists even when few-copy entangled measurements are allowed.   - Warm Gaussian states are classical to learn: When thermal fluctuations exceed the vacuum noise, parameterized by $Σ\ge(\frac12+ν)I$ for any parameter $ν>0$, we prove that single-copy tomography requires $N=Θ\left(n^2\min(n,1+ν^{-1})\right)$ copies. This bound is tight and is achieved by simple, non-adaptive, unentangled heterodyne measurements. Crucially, for $ν=Ω(1)$, the sample complexity drops to $Θ(n^2)$, matching the classical case.   Our results tightly characterize a quantum-to-classical crossover in the learnability of bosonic Gaussian states, reveal a novel connection between fundamental physics and statistical learning theory, and have implications for real-world sensing experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work calculates the universal scaling exponents for massless scalar field theories far from equilibrium by analyzing the non-perturbative infrared behavior. Using a large-N expansion, the authors find distinct scaling laws for energy versus particle number transport. The results highlight that relativistic and nonrelativistic theories belong to different universality classes in this non-equilibrium regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings on non-equilibrium scaling exponents and the distinction between relativistic and nonrelativistic universality classes are highly relevant for understanding far-from-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems, including those modeled by quantum gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the universal scaling exponents and scaling functions for massless scalar field theories when driven far from equilibrium.

**Main result.** It determines that the universal power-law tail exponent $\kappa$ for the distribution function is $d+1$ for energy transport and $d$ for effective particle number transport in $d=3$ dimensions.

**Method.** The analysis employs a large-$N$ expansion to next-to-leading order within the 2PI effective action framework, analyzing self-similar scaling solutions.

**Model / system.** The model is a relativistic scalar field theory with $O(N)$ symmetry, analyzed in $d$ spatial dimensions, considering the transport of energy or effective particle number.

**Key observables.** The universal power-law tail $f_S(ar p) \sim 1/ar{p}^\kappa$ of the nonthermal scaling distribution function, and the scaling exponents $\kappa$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial dimension $d$, the nature of transport (energy vs. particle number), and the massless dispersion relation ($\omega = ar{p}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes spatially homogeneous and isotropic systems, and relies on the existence of self-similar scaling solutions in the classical-statistical limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 diagrammatically shows the retarded propagator $G_R$ and statistical function $F$, along with the diagrammatic expression for the self-energy $\Sigma_R$.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by setting up the field theory, deriving equations of motion for correlation functions, applying scaling assumptions to find fixed-point equations, and finally solving for the universal exponents based on conservation laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compute universal scaling exponents and scaling functions for massless scalar field theories far from equilibrium. Using a large-$N$ expansion to next-to-leading order, we investigate the nonperturbative infrared behaviour associated with the transport of a) energy or b) effective particle number towards low frequencies $ω$ and momenta $\bar p$. For massless dispersion $ω= \bar{p}$, we determine the scaling form of the distribution function whose universal power-law tail $f_S(\bar p) \sim 1/\bar{p}^κ$ is found to be a) $κ= d+1$ and b) $κ= d$ in $d=3$ spatial dimensions. Our results establish that relativistic and nonrelativistic theories do not belong to the same universality class in this case, which opens up new applications to far-from-equilibrium phenomena in high-energy and (quantum) many-body physics with linear dispersions.

</details>

**Authors:** Matheus G. H. Santos, Hugo Sanchez, Italo M. de Araújo, Luis Felipe A. da Silva, Miled H. Y. Moussa, Norton G. de Almeida  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25359v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical study demonstrates a 'superradiant Mpemba relaxation' in a system of coupled two-level emitters. The authors show that the anomalous crossing of relaxation rates occurs when collective superradiant emission is active, a feature that vanishes if the emitters decay independently. This highlights the crucial role of collective quantum kinetics in non-equilibrium relaxation processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems, linking the non-trivial phenomenon of the Mpemba effect to the collective quantum dynamics characteristic of superradiance, which is highly relevant to cavity QED and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the anomalous Mpemba effect—where a state initially farther from equilibrium overtakes a closer one during relaxation—can coexist with superradiant emission in a quantum system.

**Main result.** A superradiant Mpemba relaxation is demonstrated in a Dicke ladder system, where the crossing occurs in both energy and trace distance, a phenomenon absent under independent decay.

**Method.** The dynamics are analyzed by solving the master equation for the population cascade of two-level emitters coupled to a reservoir, comparing specific sparse initial states.

**Model / system.** The system consists of $N$ identical two-level emitters coupled to a common zero-temperature reservoir, modeled within the symmetric Dicke manifold. The dynamics are governed by a master equation incorporating collective decay rates.

**Key observables.** Excitation Energy, Collective Emission Rate, and Trace Distance to Stationarity ($D(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$ (tested from 6 to 60), and the comparison between collective vs. independent decay kinetics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes permutation symmetry, zero temperature, and negligible individual decay or dephasing outside the symmetric Dicke sector.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crossing in energy and trace distance, the comparison between collective and independent emission peaks, and the system-size dependence of these crossing times and peak intensities.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and dynamics, defines the specific initial states ($ho_A$ and $ho_B$), calculates the time evolution of key observables (Energy, Distance), and finally demonstrates the persistent crossing and its dependence on collective coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Mpemba effect occurs when a state initially farther from stationarity overtakes a closer one during relaxation. We show that this anomalous ordering can coexist with superradiant emission in a collectively damped ensemble of two-level systems. In the symmetric Dicke manifold, zero-temperature decay is a population cascade with rates $Γn(N-n+1)$. We construct the sparse family $ρ_A=|\lceil2N/3\rceil\rangle\langle\lceil2N/3\rceil|$ and $ρ_B=(|0\rangle\langle0|+|N\rangle\langle N|)/2$. Although $A$ is initially more energetic and more distant from the stationary ground state, it crosses $B$ in both energy and trace distance because it starts in a more radiative region of the Dicke ladder. Both states develop emission peaks larger than their independent-emitter references. The crossing persists for all examined sizes from $N=6$ to $60$, while the peak intensities show an effective scaling close to $N^2$. The same pair has no crossing under independent decay, identifying collective radiative kinetics as the origin of the effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes spontaneous symmetry breaking in a dephasing quantum chain where the Hilbert space fragments. By using nonlinear correlation functions, the authors prove that most fragments lose long-range order, while only specific channels dictated by the periodicity of the conserved 'spin word' can sustain residual order. This highlights how local constraints can select specific, robust physical channels even when global order is lost.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated diagnostic tool (nonlinear correlators) to probe subtle forms of symmetry breaking in non-equilibrium, open quantum systems, linking microscopic constraints (spin words) to macroscopic order parameters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Strong-to-Weak Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking (SWSSB) in quantum systems whose Hilbert space fragments into dynamically disconnected subspaces beyond simple global charge conservation.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that while the full system can retain long-range order via transverse exchange, typical, isolated fragments fail to support conventional two-point nonlinear order, except for specific channels dictated by the fragment's underlying spin-word periodicity.

**Method.** The authors use nonlinear correlators, specifically fidelity and Rényi-1 order, to diagnose symmetry breaking, comparing results derived from the full-state dynamics versus those restricted to individual word fragments.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional t-Jz chain subjected to species-resolved dephasing, governed by a Lindblad master equation. The dynamics are analyzed based on the conservation of a 'spin word' when the system fragments.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F_O$), Rényi-1 correlators ($R_{1,O}$), and the Rényi-2 diagnostic ($g_2(t)$) are used to quantify the degree of spontaneous symmetry breaking and long-range order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the periodicity ($k$) of the conserved spin word, the gap size ($r$), and the coupling strength $J_\perp$ which connects the fragments.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between the order supported by the full sector (global charge) and the order supported by individual, fixed-composition word fragments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spin-word fragmentation pattern, compare the suppression of local nonlinear order in typical fragments versus the full sector, and show how transverse exchange restores sector correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the physical model and the concept of word fragmentation, then using nonlinear correlators to prove the absence of order in typical fragments, and finally identifying the specific, periodic channels that can support residual order.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong-to-weak spontaneous symmetry breaking (SWSSB) can occur in a maximally mixed charge sector even when ordinary charged correlations vanish. We study this order when a charge sector splits into dynamically disconnected fragments. In a one-dimensional $t$-$J_z$ chain with species-resolved dephasing, each fragment is labeled by a conserved spin word. A charged transfer between separated finite intervals preserves that word only if the intervening spins satisfy a periodicity constraint. In the full-state two-point sense, we use this constraint to prove the absence of long-range fidelity and Rényi-1 order in typical fixed-composition fragments, uniformly over all norm-bounded charged operators in any prescribed finite interval. A fragment with primitive spin-word period $k$ supports a $k$-particle channel with exact long-distance value $ν^k(1-ν)^k/k^2$, and no charged operator on fewer than $k$ sites can carry this order. A transverse exchange connects the fragments while preserving the global charges and restores the full-sector steady state at finite size. The conserved spin word therefore selects the local channels that can support mixed-state order.

</details>

**Summary.** This research demonstrates that localized decoherence is not merely a source of noise but can be harnessed as a functional resource in quantum interferometry. By simulating matter-wave interference using optical beams modulated by random phase patterns, the authors confirm that specific decoherence profiles yield predictable and controllable interference patterns. This establishes a new paradigm for quantum state engineering using controlled decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum foundations (decoherence) with experimental quantum optics, providing a concrete, controllable method to generate and test complex quantum states using optical tools.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper explores the counter-intuitive idea that spatially localized decoherence, usually seen as detrimental, can be actively exploited as a constructive tool for matter-wave interferometry.

**Main result.** The simulations and experiments confirm that localized decoherence can generate measurable interference patterns, providing a novel resource for engineering arbitrary spatial superpositions of massive particles.

**Method.** The study uses an optical simulation approach, mapping the physics of matter-wave interference onto the paraxial propagation of classical optical beams modulated by a Spatial Light Modulator (SLM).

**Model / system.** The system involves matter-wave interferometry simulated optically, comparing theoretical predictions for different decoherence profiles: Opaque Disk (OD), Gaussian Opacity (GO), and Periodic Opacity (PO).

**Key observables.** Interference intensity patterns (e.g., Arago spot, Talbot carpets), central intensity at the beam axis, and the visibility of interference fringes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decoherence parameters ($\sigma$, $a$), propagation distance ($z$), and experimental parameters like SLM efficiency ($\eta$) and phase deviation ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the validity of the correspondence between matter-wave evolution and optical beam propagation, though discrepancies in fitting suggest limitations in the model equations used.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare experimental measurements of interference patterns (OD, GO, PO) against theoretical predictions, often including fits that account for experimental imperfections like SLM efficiency.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework linking decoherence to interference, through detailed modeling of various decoherence geometries (OD, GO, PO), and culminates in comparing these models against quantitative experimental data and fitting observed deviations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Decoherence is conventionally regarded as a detrimental process that suppresses quantum coherence and limits the performance of quantum technologies. Contrary to this view, we show that spatially localized decoherence can instead be exploited as an interferometric tool. Beyond its conceptual implications, localized decoherence provides a novel approach to prepare arbitrary macroscopic spatial superpositions for testing quantum physics with massive particles. We demonstrate the proof of principle by making use of the correspondence between optics and matter-waves through the equivalence of the respective propagation functions. By employing stochastic phase fields to the optical counterpart to model the decoherence, our experiments confirm the expected interference. The results establish localized decoherence as a resource for quantum state engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents quantum algorithms to simulate the dynamics of dissipative quantum many-body systems described by Lindbladians. By exploiting the locality of the dissipation using novel 'patching' and 'merging' techniques, the authors achieve near-optimal gate counts for sparsely dissipative systems. This advances the field by providing scalable quantum tools for simulating open quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the simulation of open quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding decoherence and non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum computation and quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing quantum algorithms to simulate the time evolution of dissipative quantum many-body systems governed by local Lindbladians.

**Main result.** The authors establish near-optimal quantum algorithms for sparsely dissipative systems, achieving a gate count of O(Nt polylog(Nt/epsilon)), and present an algorithm for generic systems with a scaling of O((Nt)^(4/3) polylog(Nt/epsilon)).

**Method.** The algorithms exploit locality using two key techniques: 'patching' to decompose dynamics into subsystems and 'merging' to absorb reversed dissipative dynamics, generalizing ideas from Hamiltonian simulation.

**Model / system.** The system is a lattice ($\Lambda$) described by the Gorini-Kossakowski-Sudarshan-Lindblad (GKSL) master equation, $rac{d}{dt}ho(t) = Lho(t)$, where $L$ is the local Lindbladian.

**Key observables.** Gate Count (primary efficiency metric), time-evolved state $ho(t)$, and time-evolved observables $\langle Oangleho(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N$), evolution time ($t$), and allowable error ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the locality of the Lindbladians and the ability to decompose the evolution using patching and merging techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decomposition of the lattice into internal and external boundaries for high-dimensional systems, detailing the iterative steps of the patching and merging protocol.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the problem (Lindbladian simulation), then introducing the locality-exploiting techniques (patching/merging), and finally presenting the main theorems for sparsely dissipative and generic lattice Lindbladians, culminating in complexity analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating the dynamics of dissipative quantum many-body systems governed by local Lindbladians is a fundamental task in quantum computation. While the near-optimal gate count has been achieved for Hamiltonian simulation, comparable results for Lindbladian simulation remain elusive. In this work, we develop quantum algorithms for lattice Lindbladians by exploiting their locality. First, we consider sparsely dissipative systems, in which the dissipation is sparsely located, including boundary-driven systems. We establish a near-optimal quantum algorithm for their dynamics with gate count $O(Nt \, \mathrm{polylog}(Nt/\varepsilon))$, where $N$ is the system size, $t$ is the evolution time, and $\varepsilon$ is the allowable error. We then consider generic lattice Lindbladians with finite-range interactions and dissipation, and develop an algorithm for simulating time-evolved observables with gate count $O((Nt)^{4/3} \, \mathrm{polylog}(Nt/\varepsilon))$ per sample with the sampling complexity $Θ(\varepsilon^{-2})$. The gate count has the smallest known dependence on the system size among algorithms retaining polylogarithmic dependence on $1/\varepsilon$. Our algorithms are based on two techniques that exploit locality: patching and merging. Patching decomposes dissipative dynamics into dynamics on subsystems with exponentially small error, generalizing a key idea underlying the Haah-Hastings-Kothari-Low algorithm for near-optimal Hamiltonian simulation. Merging absorbs reversed dissipative dynamics into other parts of the evolution, substantially reducing the overhead associated with quasi-probabilistic sampling. These results demonstrate that locality can be fully exploited to achieve fast quantum simulation of dissipative many-body dynamics, opening the way to applications such as predicting nonequilibrium phenomena and preparing desirable quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes quantum criticality in driven-dissipative systems using the eLMG model. It shows that while an effective temperature governs critical fluctuations, the system's steady state selection near first-order transitions is governed by a non-Boltzmann rule. This highlights a crucial distinction between thermal and non-equilibrium state selection in driven quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how non-equilibrium driving fundamentally alters the statistical description of quantum phase transitions, moving beyond simple thermal equilibrium assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether non-equilibrium quantum-critical steady states in driven-dissipative systems can be described by a simple effective temperature.

**Main result.** While the critical fluctuations are governed by an effective temperature, the steady state selection near first-order transitions follows a non-Boltzmann rule, decoupling it from the deterministic potential.

**Method.** The authors employ the Keldysh path-integral formalism, large-N limit treatments, and renormalization-group scaling analysis to study the steady-state probability distribution.

**Model / system.** The model is an electronic Lipkin--Meshkov--Glick (eLMG) model, representing a quantum dot with a collective spin coupled to voltage-biased metallic leads, making it a minimal driven-dissipative system.

**Key observables.** Order parameter ($\phi$), critical fluctuations ($\langle\delta\phi^2angle$), and the stationary probability distribution $P_{ss}(\phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($h$), coupling strength ($J$), temperature ($T$), bias voltage ($V$), and hybridization strength ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-N limit, Keldysh formalism, and approximations like the Gaussian kernel expansion for fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing the open QCP, the suppression of the critical field by dissipation, and the stationary probability distributions in the bistable regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model and the role of $T_{	ext{eff}}$ in the open QCP, then analyzing the scaling of fluctuations, and finally contrasting the thermal (second-order) vs. non-thermal (first-order) state selection mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Whether a nonequilibrium quantum-critical steady state can be described by an effective temperature remains an open question. We address it in a voltage-biased electronic Lipkin--Meshkov--Glick model, a minimal driven-dissipative model of a collective spin coupled to metallic leads. A controlled large-$N$ treatment reveals an overdamped open quantum-critical regime distinct from the closed model. The surrounding quantum critical fan, organized by temperature and voltage, is explored and fluctuations are found to be governed by an effective temperature $T_{\rm eff}$. At strong bias, the transition becomes first-order through a tricritical point. Remarkably, the same $T_{\rm eff}$ governs the strongly driven regime, but now varies across the entire order-parameter landscape. The steady state is therefore selected by a non-Boltzmann rule, shifting the first-order transition away from the equal-depth point of a deterministic potential. Driven criticality thus remains organized by an effective temperature while revealing non-thermal state selection.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates strong coupling between a reconfigurable array of ${}^{171}	ext{Yb}$ atoms and a tunable nanofiber cavity operating at telecom wavelengths. By characterizing the coupling strength and the tunability of the cavity, the authors establish a robust interface for quantum networking. This paves the way for high-rate, multiplexed entanglement generation necessary for distributed quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes cavity QED into the telecom regime using neutral atoms, which is a critical step for building scalable quantum networks and quantum repeaters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to create a high-rate, high-fidelity quantum interconnect by coupling a reconfigurable array of ${}^{171}	ext{Yb}$ atoms to a tunable telecom-band nanofiber cavity.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated strong atom-photon coupling on the telecom-band transition, achieving single-atom cooperativity of $C_{	ext{in}}=5.6$ and projecting a time-multiplexed entanglement generation rate of $40 	ext{ kHz}$.

**Method.** The study uses reflection spectroscopy on a single-sided cavity, combined with optical tweezer trapping and transport, to characterize the atom-cavity interaction.

**Model / system.** The platform combines a nanofiber cavity (formed by two FBGs) operating at $1389 	ext{ nm}$ with an array of ${}^{171}	ext{Yb}$ atoms trapped in optical tweezers. The system is designed to interface neutral-atom quantum processors with telecom quantum networks.

**Key observables.** Single-atom internal cooperativity ($C_{	ext{in}}$), collective coupling scaling ($\sqrt{N}$), and the cavity outcoupling rate ($\kappa_{	ext{ex}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Telecom wavelength ($1389 	ext{ nm}$), single-atom coupling ($g/2\pi \approx 1.86 	ext{ MHz}$), and the ability to tune $\kappa_{	ext{ex}}$ over two orders of magnitude.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on single-sided cavity input-output models and assumes compatibility between the tweezer trapping light and the cavity coupling mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the platform overview, the tweezer array setup, and SEM images of the fabricated nanofiber, supporting the characterization of coupling strength and transport feasibility.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform and coupling strength via spectroscopy, to demonstrating tunability of the cavity coupling rate, and finally to analyzing the compatibility for high-rate, multiplexed quantum networking protocols.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nanophotonic cavities provide strong atom-photon coupling in a compact, directly fiber-coupled geometry, while a reconfigurable array of $^{171}$Yb atoms combines high-fidelity gates with a metastable qubit state coupled directly to a telecom-band transition, making the two a natural interface between neutral-atom quantum processors and telecom quantum networks. Here, we realize strong atom-cavity coupling on the ${}^{3}\mathrm{P}_0 \leftrightarrow {}^{3}\mathrm{D}_1$ transition of ${}^{171}\mathrm{Yb}$, establishing, to our knowledge, the first neutral-atom cavity-QED system to reach the single-atom strong-coupling regime on a telecom-band atomic transition. Reflection spectroscopy of a single-sided cavity yields a single-atom internal cooperativity of $C_{\mathrm{in}}=5.6(1.0)$ and collective coupling following $\sqrt{N}$ scaling up to $N=5$ atoms, with homogeneous coupling at antinodes over $\sim$200 $\mathrmμ$m along the nanofiber. Thermal tuning of a fiber Bragg grating provides in situ control of the cavity outcoupling rate over two orders of magnitude from the undercoupled to the overcoupled regime, relevant for various atom-cavity protocols. We confirm compatibility with reconfigurable atom arrays by demonstrating 99.95(6)\% imaging fidelity and trap lifetimes comparable to free-space array, at 1 $\mathrmμ$m from the nanofiber axis. Moreover, the diameter-engineered antireflection design of the nanofiber enables tweezer-based atom transport across the nanofiber with no measurable loss or heating at 4.5 $\mathrmμ$m above the nanofiber, supporting a two-layer architecture for the nanofiber-integrated atom array. From the demonstrated atom-cavity coupling, atom capacity, and cavity mode profile, we project time-multiplexed remote atom-atom entanglement generation at 40 kHz, with prospects for further scaling through technical enhancements and channel multiplexing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes measurement-induced phase transitions in periodically monitored quantum circuits, showing that entanglement and purification can evolve independently. By introducing the logical-support radius and Krylov dimension, the authors characterize the transition between delocalized (volume-law) and localized (area-law) information storage. This establishes a new universality class for MIPTs distinct from standard random circuit models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of MIPTs provides a powerful, non-equilibrium tool to probe quantum criticality, offering insights into how structured dissipation can drive phase transitions distinct from thermal or Hamiltonian-driven ones.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Measurement-Induced Phase Transitions (MIPTs) and the fundamental separation between entanglement dynamics and purification dynamics in quantum systems subjected to structured, repeated measurements.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that entanglement and purification can transition independently, identifying distinct universality classes for these transitions based on the spatial structure of the monitoring process.

**Method.** The analysis uses the plateau stabilizer group to characterize the surviving logical subspace and introduces the logical Krylov dimension to quantify the spatial spreading and localization of quantum information.

**Model / system.** The model consists of spatially local monitored Clifford circuits where a fixed, random pattern of gates and Pauli measurements is repeated periodically (Floquet monitoring).

**Key observables.** Entanglement (Volume-law vs. Area-law), logical-support radius, and the logical Krylov dimension.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the system (1D vs. 2D+), the measurement probability ($p$), and the time scale (late-time/thermodynamic limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving an effective Floquet unitary acting on the surviving logical degrees of freedom, assuming the dynamics are governed by the plateau stabilizer group.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram, showing the separation of dynamics in 1D and the volume-law to area-law transition in 2D+. Schematics detail the localized vs. delocalized logical subspaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the effective Floquet dynamics via the plateau stabilizer group, then analyzing the separation of entanglement/purification in 1D, followed by characterizing the volume-law to area-law transition in 2D+ using entanglement measures, and finally resolving the transition using the logical support radius and Krylov dimension.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An entanglement transition can occur without a corresponding transition in purification. We demonstrate this separation in spatially local monitored Clifford circuits with a fixed number of layers per Floquet period, where a spatially random pattern of Clifford gates and Pauli measurements is repeated exactly in time. Rare spatial structures persist under this repetition and break the correspondence between entanglement and purification found in conventional spacetime-random circuits. In one spatial dimension, the late-time entanglement produced with an initial product state remains area-law, while the system remains extensively mixed in the thermodynamic and long-time limits if the initial state is maximally mixed. In two or more dimensions, we find a transition between volume-law and area-law entanglement while the system remains mixed on both sides of this transition. An extensive amount of quantum information survives indefinitely in both phases and evolves unitarily despite the repeated measurements. We introduce the logical-support radius to characterize its spatial spreading and identify the volume-law and area-law phases with delocalized and localized logical dynamics, respectively. We further introduce the logical Krylov dimension, which counts the independent logical operators dynamically generated from an initially local operator. Its scaling resolves the transition and provides evidence for a new universality class of measurement-induced phase transitions.

</details>

### [Quantum survival in hybrid pursuit dynamics - a lion chasing a lamb problem](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25844v1)

**Authors:** P. Held, İ. Yalçınkaya, F. Pegoraro, J. Lammers, F. Schlue, K. Jiráková, S. Barkhofen, B. Brecht, M. Štefaňák, V. Potoček, A. Gábris, I. Jex, C. Silberhorn  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25844v1>

Counterintuitively, the survival probability of lamb initially increases despite the increasing randomness of the lion’s movement, demonstrating that mixing classical stochasticity and quantum evolution can enhance survival through modified in- terference, resembling the non-monotonic recurrence behavior of stochastic walks [10]. The blue area indicates one sigma uncertainty due to finite sampling of trajectories.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work explores quantum survival in a hybrid quantum-classical pursuit game using a photonic quantum walk setup. By simulating a quantum walker chased by a classical random walker, the authors demonstrate that increasing the predator's mobility can paradoxically enhance the prey's survival. This highlights a novel mechanism where classical stochastic control aids quantum coherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally realized platform for studying how controlled measurement back-action and classical stochasticity can actively enhance quantum coherence and survival, which is a key topic in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the interplay between quantum coherence and classical randomness by modeling a quantum walker (lamb) being pursued by a classical random walker (lion).

**Main result.** Counterintuitively, increasing the lion's mobility does not monotonically suppress the lamb's survival probability; for a broad range of hopping probabilities, the lamb survives better than against an immobile predator.

**Method.** The dynamics are studied by implementing a photonic time-multiplexed quantum walk, where the lion's action is simulated via measurements (projections) onto auxiliary modes.

**Model / system.** The system involves a quantum walker (lamb) evolving unitarily on a line, coupled to a classical lazy random walk (lion). The interaction is non-unitary due to measurement-induced projection at the lion's position.

**Key observables.** Survival probability, $S_t(p)$, which is the average of the instantaneous survival probability over all possible lion trajectories.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lion's hopping probability ($p$), time steps ($t=20$), and the scaling of auxiliary modes ($t^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on averaging over all possible lion trajectories, and experimental measurements account for detector saturation and polarization imbalance.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 compares the measured and theoretical quantum survival probability versus the lion's hopping probability ($p$), showing the non-monotonic enhancement.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the hybrid quantum-classical pursuit problem, details the photonic time-multiplexed experimental setup, presents the theoretical framework for averaging over classical paths, and finally reports the measured survival probability results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurement and classical randomness usually degrade coherence; controlling where and when a subsystem is monitored can instead make them a tunable resource. We introduce a photonic time-multiplexed quantum walk whose fast reconfigurability enables precise position measurements, allowing us to study this interplay in a hybrid quantum-classical pursuit problem: A quantum walker (lamb) evolves on a line while a classical pursuer (lion) performs a lazy random walk. Each lion trajectory drives a distinct coherent, norm-reducing evolution of the lamb, with capture implemented by measurements at the lion's positions; averaging over the recorded trajectories then synthesizes a decoherent process. Counterintuitively, increasing the lion's mobility does not monotonically suppress survival: over a broad range of hopping probabilities, the lamb survives better than against an immobile predator. Directly observing this local enhancement, we demonstrate a manifestly quantum feature aided rather than hindered by classical stochastic control.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents the design and characterization of a novel, high-performance ion-photon interface capable of operating over a long working distance (26.15 mm). By optimizing a custom objective lens system, the authors achieved a stable, fiber-coupled detection efficiency of $\approx 1.3\%$ for trapped-ion fluorescence. This practical implementation is crucial for scaling up trapped-ion quantum network experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a major engineering bottleneck in trapped-ion quantum networking: coupling high-quality quantum light sources to external fiber infrastructure while maintaining operational stability and spatial constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge was designing an efficient ion-photon quantum interface that could maintain high photon collection efficiency while providing a long working distance for practical integration with existing ion-trap hardware.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an interface with a working distance of 26.15 mm and an overall fiber-coupled detection efficiency of $(1.29 \pm 0.09)\%$, while confirming non-classical photon statistics via antibunching measurements.

**Method.** The interface was constructed using a custom objective lens system of six spherical lenses, which was characterized by optimizing lens spacing and performing detailed photon-correlation measurements.

**Model / system.** The experiment utilizes trapped $^{138}	ext{Ba}^+$ ions in a 4-rod Paul trap. The system involves collecting fluorescence photons emitted from the ion, which are then coupled into a Single-Mode Fiber (SMF).

**Key observables.** Overall fiber-coupled photon-detection efficiency ($\approx 1.3\%$), antibunching ($g^{(2)}(0) = 0.042$), and signal stability over several hours.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Working Distance (WD) > 26 mm, Numerical Aperture (NA) = 0.5, and the use of a six-lens objective assembly.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis notes that initial simulations overestimated the coupling efficiency, and the final measurements were limited by the practical assembly of the complex optical system.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the schematic cross-section of the objective lens system (Fig. 1), optimized through-focus images across an axial scan (Fig. 2), and the mechanical integration setup and stability monitoring (Fig. 4).

**Paper structure.** The paper details the design of the long-working-distance objective, presents the experimental setup integrated with the ion trap, characterizes the optical performance (efficiency and stability), and confirms the quantum nature of the emitted light via correlation measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Trapped-ion quantum networks rely on efficient ion-photon interfaces, while objective-based free-space collection systems must balance high photon collection with sufficient working distance for practical integration with ion-trap hardware. Here, we design and characterize an ion-photon interface with a long working distance of 26.15 mm and a high numerical aperture of 0.5. The core of the interface is a photon-collection objective consisting of six commercial spherical lenses with optimized spacing. The interface achieved an overall fiber-coupled photon-detection efficiency of (1.29$\pm$0.09)% from the ion to the detector and maintains fiber coupling over several hours without active realignment, supporting stable operation of the ion-to-fiber interface. Photon-correlation measurements show clear antibunching in the fiber-coupled fluorescence, confirming the preservation of non-classical photon statistics. These results demonstrate a practical implementation of a long-working-distance photon collection interface for trapped-ion quantum network experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that by employing post-selection on a two-path interferometer, the phase sensitivity can be significantly enhanced beyond the standard quantum limit. This enhancement is achieved by engineering the input state to entangle path and polarization, effectively mimicking the scaling of a four-photon NOON state. This shows a powerful way to access high-precision quantum metrology using single-photon resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental demonstration of how measurement selection (post-selection) can effectively simulate the resource scaling of multi-photon entangled states (like NOON states) using only single photons in a standard setup.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To enhance the phase sensitivity of a two-path interferometer beyond the standard quantum limit (SQL) using post-selection techniques.

**Main result.** The achieved phase gradient approaches that of a four-photon NOON state, demonstrating that single-photon interference can reach multi-photon entanglement scaling through post-selection.

**Method.** The enhancement is achieved by preparing an entangled state in the path and polarization degrees of freedom and post-selecting on a low-probability output port.

**Model / system.** The system is a conventional two-path interferometer, extended to include polarization states. The phase shift is governed by a path difference operator, and the enhancement relies on transferring phase information to the circular polarization degree of freedom.

**Key observables.** The conditional probability $P(R|-angle)$ and its derivative (slope) with respect to the phase shift $\phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The effective photon number difference $A_{eff}$, which quantifies the enhancement; the standard quantum limit (SQL).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement relies on introducing a slight entanglement between polarization and paths, and the method is limited by experimental imperfections.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the setup using HWP1 and HWP2 for polarization rotation. Figure 2 illustrates the measured conditional probability $P(R|-angle)$ vs. $\phi$, showing the enhanced slope near $\phi=0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework for post-selection Fisher information, applies this to the two-path interferometer by incorporating polarization, and presents experimental measurements comparing the measured slope to the theoretical NOON state prediction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Post-selected metrology can exceed the standard quantum limit of single photon interference by concentrating all of the available Fisher information in a subset of post-selected outcomes. Here we apply this principle to a standard two-path interferometer, activating the Fisher information concentrated in the low probability output port by transferring the phase sensitivity to the circular polarizations of the photon. We obtain a phase gradient of the post-selected interference fringe that corresponds to that of a four-photon NOON state, providing a direct comparison of the performance of multi-photon interference with single-photon post-selection.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the successful operation of a programmable quantum photonic processor on a nanosatellite in orbit. By generating and manipulating indistinguishable photon pairs, the researchers demonstrated two-photon quantum interference, proving the feasibility of using quantum light for onboard computation. This advances quantum technology from secure communication to active, space-based quantum data processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering and physical realization challenges of quantum computation in extreme environments, providing a roadmap for space-based quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is to transition quantum photonic states from mere communication resources to active computational resources onboard satellites, overcoming environmental hurdles like thermal drift and radiation.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the hardware primitive for quantum-enhanced onboard processing by observing two-photon quantum interference (HOM dip) in orbit.

**Method.** The experiment involved programming a quantum photonic processor to execute distinct unitary transformations on two indistinguishable photons.

**Model / system.** The platform is a programmable quantum photonic processor housed on a nanosatellite, featuring a source of indistinguishable photon pairs, a six-mode universal photonic integrated circuit (uPIC), and single-photon detectors.

**Key observables.** Hong–Ou–Mandel (HOM) dip (signature of two-photon quantum interference), coincidence probabilities, and fidelity of programmed unitaries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating at 810 nm, the system is compact (9.8 kg, 10 W average power) and designed for space environments.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that maintaining quantum interference conditions (spectral degeneracy, phase stability) is possible despite the harsh space environment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall photonic computing platform in space, the physical dimensions of the unit, the in-orbit data acquisition correlating counts with solar position, and the implementation of programmable unitary operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup, the methodology for in-orbit measurements (including noise mitigation and environmental monitoring), the observation of the key quantum interference signature, and the implications for future quantum-assisted computing missions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum technologies promise computational capabilities beyond the reach of classical systems. A forward-looking application lies in satellite missions, which increasingly depend on onboard computing under stringent constraints on size, weight and power. Quantum photonics is particularly attractive here: photon interference can enhance the machine-learning models needed to process large onboard data volumes, at fixed hardware resources. However, harnessing this interference requires more than generating single photons, as they must remain mutually indistinguishable: a fragile condition that is hard to maintain within the technically demanding framework of a space mission, which includes a rocket launch, strong thermal drifts, and radiation. This is why quantum states of light, though already generated and transmitted in orbit for secure communication and fundamental tests, have never been used as a computational resource. Here, we report a programmable quantum photonic platform operating on a nanosatellite, processing two photons in a six-mode universal integrated circuit. By programming distinct unitaries and tuning the photons into indistinguishability, we observe two-photon interference, establishing the on-board generation, manipulation and detection of non-classical light. This extends space-based technologies towards in-orbit quantum-assisted computing, for instance local encoding of Earth-observation data, or nodes in a distributed quantum network.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a quantum battery implemented via a three-level system undergoing continuous measurement. By employing post-selection techniques, the dynamics are restricted to an effective non-Hermitian two-level system. The study quantifies energy storage and extractable work (ergotropy), highlighting how measurement backaction significantly influences the battery's performance compared to simpler models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics (measurement backaction, non-Hermitian physics) with quantum thermodynamics (quantum batteries), providing advanced theoretical tools for analyzing energy storage in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the charging dynamics and energy extraction capabilities of a quantum battery realized through a post-selection process on a three-level system.

**Main result.** Post-selection effectively confines the dynamics to a non-Hermitian two-level manifold, and the analysis shows that measurement backaction and post-selection effects are crucial for accurately determining the charging rate and ergotropy.

**Method.** The authors employ stochastic master equations (SME) for trajectory analysis, comparing these results with ensemble-averaged dynamics derived from the Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-level quantum battery modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating coherent driving ($\Omega$) and detuning ($\Delta$), subjected to continuous measurement and post-selection based on avoiding a specific quantum jump.

**Key observables.** Charging rate, ergotropy (extractable work), and quantum coherence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\Delta$), coherent driving strength ($\Omega$), and decay rates ($\Gamma_e, \Gamma_g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the validity of post-selecting trajectories that avoid specific quantum jumps, effectively reducing the system dimensionality.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Liouvillian spectrum (eigenvalues) and compare the time evolution of Bloch vector components and ergotropy under different driving protocols ($\sigma_x$ vs $\sigma_y$) and detuning regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the three-level system and the measurement scheme, deriving the SME and the post-selected effective dynamics. It then analyzes the charging dynamics and compares the results against generic two-level systems and standard Lindblad approaches, focusing on the role of measurement backaction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum batteries are quantum systems that store useful energy, which can subsequently be extracted to perform work. In this work, we investigate the charging dynamics under continuous measurements and post-selection-based non-Hermitian quantum battery realized by a three-level system. By post-selecting the trajectories with no quantum jump $|1\rangle \rightarrow |0\rangle$, the dynamics is effectively confined to a two-level manifold. We analyze the resulting charging dynamics under both $σ_x$- and $σ_y$-driving protocols and investigate the roles of detuning, coherent driving, non-linearity, and measurement backaction. The generation and dynamics of quantum coherence, which plays a pivotal role in quantum battery, are also analyzed under post-selection. The advantage and comparison of our results over those of a generic two-level system and with Lindblad dynamics are also highlighted. Our work explores the interplay between the measurement axis and the driving protocols that govern the charging rate, ergotropy, and quantum coherence of the non-Hermitian, highly tunable, and controllable quantum battery.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Preselection-Encoded Weak-Value Amplification (PeWVA) as a novel readout strategy for optical-rotation metrology. By encoding the measurement parameter into the probe polarization before a weak-value interferometer, PeWVA mitigates detector saturation issues common in high-precision sensing. Experimental validation using an atomic magnetometer confirms that PeWVA significantly improves magnetic-field readout performance under realistic, detector-constrained conditions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical limitations in quantum sensing by modifying the measurement readout scheme. The concept of separating sensing and readout via preselection is highly relevant for designing robust quantum optical sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Optical-rotation measurements are limited by detector saturation and technical noise when increasing probe photon number, despite the benefit of high photon numbers for precision.

**Main result.** The proposed Preselection-Encoded Weak-Value Amplification (PeWVA) technique provides a practical advantage by allowing high-flux sensing while keeping detected power within detector limits, leading to substantially improved magnetic-field readout.

**Method.** The authors introduce PeWVA, which encodes the measured quantity into the probe polarization *before* a weak-value interferometer, separating sensing from readout.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses an ${}^{87}	ext{Rb } M_x$ atomic magnetometer platform, employing polarization interferometry and weak-value measurement principles.

**Key observables.** Magnetic-field noise floor ($	ext{pT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$), visibility ($V$), and Fisher Information ($F(	heta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector saturation limit, weak-coupling parameter ($D$), and incident photon number ($N_{ph}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to separate the sensing interaction from the postselected readout, and that the primary limitation is detector dynamic range rather than fundamental quantum limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the schematic of the PeWVA readout setup. Figure 7 compares the measured noise spectra (OR noise) for standard vs. PeWVA readout under conditions with and without a vapor cell.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of detector limitations in optical metrology. It then develops the PeWVA theory, comparing it mathematically to standard interferometry (SI) using Fisher Information. Finally, it presents experimental results using an atomic magnetometer, demonstrating noise reduction and improved robustness against stray light.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optical-rotation measurements benefit from increasing probe photon number, but their performance is often limited by detector saturation and technical noise in the optical readout. Here we introduce preselection-encoded weak-value amplification (PeWVA), in which the quantity of interest is encoded into the probe polarization before a downstream weak-value interferometer. This architecture separates the sensing interaction from the postselected readout, enabling tunable local amplification while reducing the optical power delivered to the detector. We show that PeWVA does not create additional Fisher information, but offers a practical advantage when detector dynamic range is limiting. We further analyze coherent stray light and phase fluctuations and show reduced conversion of these perturbations into equivalent rotation errors. Using an $^{87}$Rb $M_x$ atomic magnetometer, we experimentally demonstrate substantially improved magnetic-field readout under detector-constrained conditions using both transimpedance-amplified and avalanche photodiodes. The experiments also confirm reduced susceptibility to vapor-cell-induced stray light and interferometer phase modulation. These results establish PeWVA as a detector-constrained optical readout strategy with enhanced tolerance to representative interferometric perturbations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an adaptive measurement-based protocol for resetting superconducting qubits, moving beyond fixed-threshold or iterative methods. By using Bayesian inference on the entire measurement history, it dynamically adjusts the feedback threshold to maintain high fidelity. This approach provides a practical and scalable route for fast qubit initialization necessary for large quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical hardware bottleneck in quantum computing—fast, reliable state preparation—by merging advanced statistical inference (Bayesian methods) with superconducting circuit implementation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a fast, high-fidelity qubit initialization (reset) protocol that overcomes the limitations of conventional methods which are either slow or discard crucial measurement information.

**Main result.** The proposed Bayesian adaptive thresholding protocol achieves a high ground-state initialization fidelity (99.44%) with a deterministic and fast reset duration, enabling scalable multi-qubit operations.

**Method.** The protocol uses Bayesian inference to continuously update the qubit-state estimate based on the full analog measurement history, dynamically adjusting the feedback threshold at each step.

**Model / system.** The work is implemented on superconducting transmon qubits coupled to a microwave resonator, utilizing dispersive readout to measure the qubit state via continuous measurement outcomes.

**Key observables.** Ground-state initialization fidelity, five-qubit simultaneous-reset fidelity, prior/posterior probability ($\pi_i$), and the dynamically adjusted feedback threshold ($t_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR), reset fidelity ($99.44\%$), and the scaling of expected exit time ($E[T_N] \propto \ln N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The readout outcomes are approximated by Gaussian distributions, and the analysis often assumes equal measurement variances ($\sigma_g = \sigma_e$) for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the adaptive protocol sequence, the evolution of the threshold relative to confidence, and the convergence of infidelity versus the number of reset rounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of fixed-threshold and Repeat-Until-Success (RUS) protocols. It then details the Bayesian adaptive thresholding method, showing how the threshold updates based on accumulated measurement history. Finally, it presents experimental results demonstrating high fidelity and scalability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast, high-fidelity qubit initialization is a key primitive for scalable quantum information processing, but conventional reset protocols either require long relaxation times or discard information contained in continuous measurement records. We demonstrate an adaptive measurement-based reset protocol for superconducting qubits that uses Bayesian inference to update the qubit-state estimate after each readout and dynamically adjust the feedback threshold. Unlike fixed-threshold or repeat-until-success (RUS) protocols, the method utilizes the full analog measurement history, enabling the reset decision to become progressively more conservative as confidence in ground-state preparation increases. Implemented with real-time FPGA-based feedback on a dispersively readout transmon qubit, the protocol achieves a ground-state initialization fidelity of $99.44\pm0.04\%$ after a small number of reset rounds. We further compare adaptive reset with RUS strategies in multi-qubit experiments and show that adaptive thresholding provides deterministic reset duration while maintaining a five-qubit simultaneous-reset fidelity of $98.78 \pm 0.19\%$. These results establish Bayesian adaptive thresholding as a practical and scalable route to fast qubit reset in superconducting quantum processors.

</details>
