**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that using fluctuating pump fields, such as those from Amplified Spontaneous Emission (ASE), can nonlinearly amplify quantum photon statistics in spontaneous four-wave mixing within a SiN microring. The results show a significant boost in photon bunching compared to using a coherent pump, establishing that the statistics of the driving field is a crucial parameter for designing advanced quantum light sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it shows that the statistics of the driving field itself can be engineered to enhance non-classical correlations in the generated quantum light, providing a new design dimension for quantum sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how fluctuations in the pump field can drive nonlinear amplification of quantum statistics in light generation, moving beyond standard coherent pumping models.

**Main result.** Using ASE pumping in a SiN microring, the unconditional second-order correlation ($g^{(2)}$) was significantly increased (from 2.01 to 7.58), demonstrating that pump fluctuations can enhance photon bunching while maintaining heralded single-photon behavior.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling of nonlinear interactions with experimental measurements using spontaneous four-wave mixing (SFWM) in a microring resonator.

**Model / system.** The system is a SiN microring resonator undergoing SFWM, where the pump field is broadband Amplified Spontaneous Emission (ASE). The theoretical framework extends the coherent-pump model to include pump intensity fluctuations, leading to a nonlinear weighting of the generation rate ($\propto I_p^2(t)$).

**Key observables.** Unconditional second-order correlation ($g^{(2)}_u(0)$), Hanbury Brown–Twiss (HBT) correlation time, and time–energy entanglement (Franson visibility).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of pump-to-photon bandwidths ($r_
u$) and the effective mode number ($M_p$) are key parameters governing the degree of statistical amplification.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the generated photon field is single-mode in some calculations, and the method relies on spectral filtering to extend the measurable coherence time.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the enhancement of HBT correlations due to pump fluctuations compared to coherent pumping, and show the dependence of statistical amplification on pump bandwidth ratios and effective pump mode numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework for fluctuation-driven amplification, presenting experimental measurements comparing ASE vs. coherent pumping for $g^{(2)}$ and HBT correlations, and finally demonstrating entanglement generation using the fluctuating pump source.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon statistics have moved to the forefront of modern optics, as intensity fluctuations and correlations shape multiphoton interactions and reveal information beyond mean-intensity measurements. Developing high-quality photon sources with pronounced correlations is a fundamental necessity in these fields. Here we demonstrate fluctuation-driven nonlinear statistical amplification of quantum light in spontaneous four-wave mixing using filtered amplified spontaneous emission (ASE). Extending the coherent-pump framework to fluctuating fields, we show how nonlinear weighting of pump intensity combines with bosonic bunching to amplify quantum statistics and reshape temporal correlations. In a SiN microring, ASE pumping increases the zero-delay unconditional second-order correlation from 2.01 to 7.58 and extends the Hanbury Brown--Twiss correlation time by a factor of approximately 2.4. The super-bunched quantum source nevertheless retains heralded single-photon behaviour with $g_H^{(2)}(0)\simeq0.04$, while the same ASE pump supports time--energy entanglement in a silicon waveguide with a raw Franson visibility of 89.84\%. These results establish driving-field statistics as a design dimension for quantum light, broadening the horizons for research into higher-order correlations and nonlinear physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a tensor network framework to classically simulate complex dynamic quantum circuits that include mid-circuit measurements and classical feedback. It introduces and compares two simulation strategies—single-path stochastic sampling and multi-path ensemble tracking—to manage the exponential growth of computational branches. The resulting framework offers a scalable tool for analyzing quantum algorithms that rely on adaptive measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to simulate complex, adaptive quantum processes like those found in quantum error correction or state preparation, which are crucial for understanding open quantum systems dynamics, is highly valuable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a scalable classical simulation method for dynamic quantum circuits, which suffer from exponentially growing execution branches due to mid-circuit measurements.

**Main result.** The authors provide a practical and scalable framework by comparing single-path stochastic and multi-path ensemble simulation strategies, showing the trade-offs between accuracy and computational cost.

**Method.** The method extends a DMRG-based tensor network approach to incorporate dynamic circuit operations, handling branching via stochastic sampling or weighted ensemble propagation.

**Model / system.** The model is the dynamic quantum circuit, involving unitary operations, mid-circuit measurements yielding classical outcomes, and classically controlled operations acting on both a quantum and a classical register.

**Key observables.** Fidelity (global and partial), computational runtime, and peak memory usage.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), circuit depth, and the number of active measurement branches (for multi-path).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The multi-path approach assumes that truncating low-probability branches (top-M selection) is an acceptable approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare computational costs (runtime/memory) for single-path vs. multi-path simulations, and show fidelity decay versus circuit depth for both MPS and TTN representations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the tensor network extension of DMRG for dynamic circuits, compares the single-path and multi-path simulation strategies, and benchmarks these methods on standard and random circuits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamic circuits, which incorporate mid-circuit measurements and classically controlled operations, extend the expressive power of quantum programs and are central to applications such as quantum error correction, state preparation, and distributed quantum algorithms. However, their classical simulation is challenging due to the exponential growth of execution branches induced by mid-circuit measurements. In this work, we develop a tensor network approach for simulating dynamic circuits by extending a DMRG-based method for quantum circuit simulation to support dynamic circuit operations. To address the exponential proliferation of execution branches, we introduce and compare two strategies: a single-path stochastic approach that samples individual measurement outcomes, and a multi-path approach that maintains an ensemble of branches. We benchmark these methods on standard dynamic circuits, including the teleportation protocol and GHZ state preparation, as well as on random dynamic circuits. Our analysis explores the trade-offs between simulation accuracy and computational efficiency. These results provide a practical and scalable framework for the classical simulation of dynamic circuits.

</details>

### [Maximum-Likelihood Amplitude Estimation for Quantum Monte Carlo Integration on Trapped-Ion Hardware: Convergence, Noise-Floor Saturation, Depth-Dependent Bias](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25992v1)

**Authors:** Aditi Lal, Alex Khan, Rut Lineswala, Abhishek Chopra  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25992v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `QC/QI experiment` **4/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work experimentally benchmarks Maximum-Likelihood Amplitude Estimation (MLAE) for Monte Carlo integration on trapped-ion quantum computers. The study finds that while the algorithm theoretically achieves superior scaling, current hardware noise causes the error to saturate at a measurable floor, negating the expected quantum advantage. The practical takeaway is that shallow, carefully tuned circuits are necessary, rather than maximizing circuit depth.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial, empirical assessment of the gap between theoretical quantum speedups (like Heisenberg scaling) and the actual performance achievable on near-term quantum hardware platforms like trapped ions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper assesses the practical advantage of Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) over classical Monte Carlo integration for uncertainty quantification on current quantum hardware.

**Main result.** While the algorithm shows near-Heisenberg scaling in the noiseless limit, on current trapped-ion hardware, the error saturates at a noise floor, and the super-classical scaling advantage does not survive.

**Method.** A Maximum-Likelihood Amplitude Estimation (MLAE) pipeline, combining controlled-Ry state preparation, Grover amplification, and maximum-likelihood inference, was benchmarked.

**Model / system.** The experiments were conducted on IonQ trapped-ion processors (Forte-1 and Aria-1 noise models), using a benchmark integral estimation problem.

**Key observables.** Estimation error ($\epsilon$), error scaling exponent ($\alpha$), total oracle calls, and device time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Amplification depth ($m$), number of qubits (5, 9, 19), and the comparison between hardware performance and vendor noise models.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between the asymptotic theoretical correctness of MLAE and its practical performance on current noisy hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely show the estimation error versus the number of oracle calls, illustrating the saturation at a noise floor, and potentially comparing circuit costs across different depths.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically benchmarks the MLAE pipeline across multiple hardware settings and schedules, first establishing the noiseless limit, and then analyzing the performance degradation and saturation on real quantum hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Amplitude Estimation promises to accelerate Monte Carlo integration by replacing the classical $\varepsilon \propto N^{-1/2}$ sampling law with the Heisenberg-limited $\varepsilon \propto N^{-1}$ scaling, but whether any part of that advantage survives on present-day hardware is an empirical question. We report a systematic experimental benchmark of a Maximum-Likelihood Amplitude Estimation QMC pipeline on IonQ trapped-ion processors, comprising 60 hardware trials on IonQ Forte-1 across five settings(5 & 9 qubits, 100-200 shots per depth, linear & exponential amplification schedules, ~113 device-hours), together with 100 trials on IonQ Aria-1 & Forte-1 noise models at 5, 9 & 19 qubits. The pipeline combines controlled-$R_y$ rotational state preparation, Grover-based amplitude amplification and maximum-likelihood inference and is benchmarked against $b_{\max}^{-1}\!\int_0^{b_{\max}}\!\sin^2\!x\,\mathrm{d}x$ with $b_{\max}=π/5$ ,given results emerge. First, the noiseless limit it attains $\varepsilon \propto N^{-0.88}$, close to the Heisenberg-scaling reference, reducing the error 54-74x over a 133x increase in oracle calls. Second, scaling does not survive on hardware. Every noisy backend saturates at an error floor of (2-5)x 10^{-2}, with fitted exponents of 0.04-0.15 on Forte-1 linear schedules and $α\le\!0$ on the vendor noise models: beyond the optimal shallow depth, however, deeper amplification provides no sustained accuracy gain and instead returns to the noise floor. Forte-1 hardware also outperformed IonQ's own Aria-1 noise model at every depth $m \ge 2$, indicating that vendor noise models are bad predictors of MLAE accuracy. The practical implication for UQ is that on current devices the useful operating point is a shallow, schedule-tuned amplification depth chosen so that the deepest circuit lands near p~1/2 not the deepest schedule the coherence budget allows.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes realizing the Kitaev spin liquid model in a superconducting metamaterial composed of Cooper-pair boxes. By carefully controlling external parameters like magnetic flux and Fermi energy, the authors engineer bond-directional interactions necessary for the model. This establishes a concrete, circuit-based platform for studying exotic quantum spin liquid physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges the gap between abstract theoretical models of quantum magnetism (Kitaev model) and tangible solid-state superconducting circuit implementations, which is highly relevant for experimental quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a viable experimental route to realize the Kitaev spin liquid model, an archetypal quantum spin liquid, within controllable superconducting metamaterials.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the Kitaev Hamiltonian can be realized using only bosonic Cooper-pair degrees of freedom in engineered superconducting networks, supporting both gapless and gapped spin-liquid phases.

**Method.** The effective Hamiltonian is derived using perturbation theory (Schrieffer–Wolff transformation) by integrating out the superconducting elements connecting Cooper-pair boxes.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconducting metamaterial built from Cooper-pair boxes (CPBs) coupled via depleted semiconductor–superconductor heterostructures forming a honeycomb lattice. The effective spins are encoded in the CPBs.

**Key observables.** Gapless spin-liquid phase, gapped non-Abelian phase (with chiral Majorana edge modes), and the required bond-directional exchange interactions ($J_x, J_y, J_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Out-of-plane magnetic flux ($\Phi_A$) and the semiconductor Fermi energy ($E_F$), which control interference and enforce bond directionality via emergent Nambu-exchange symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes the CPBs operate in a regime where pairing energy exceeds Coulomb energy, and the bond-directional interactions rely on the equality of $	ext{ECT}_2$ and $	ext{CAR}_2$ contributions in the low-density limit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical architecture, showing CPBs and the honeycomb network with labels for x, y, and z bonds, and depicting Peierls phases. Figure 2 details the four classes of processes contributing to the effective theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical architecture and the target Hamiltonian. It then derives the effective link Hamiltonians using perturbation theory, showing how bond-directional interactions emerge from the balance between cotunneling ($	ext{ECT}_2$) and anomalous pair transfer ($	ext{CAR}_2$). Finally, it discusses the resulting quantum phases and experimental feasibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a realization of the Kitaev honeycomb Hamiltonian -- an archetypal spin-liquid model -- in a superconducting metamaterial. The architecture consists of Cooper-pair boxes coupled through depleted semiconductor--superconductor heterostructures that do not require spin--orbit coupling. The Cooper-pair boxes encode effective spin degrees of freedom, while normal and anomalous virtual propagation through the heterostructures mediate bond-directional interactions. Two key control parameters are an out-of-plane magnetic flux and the semiconductor Fermi energy. The former controls interference and distinguishes the bond directions, while tuning the latter close to the bottom of the band gives rise to an emergent Nambu-exchange symmetry that enforces the required bond directionality. Through numerical calculations, we identify an operating regime with controlled corrections, with associated energy and length scales within experimental reach. These results establish a route toward equilibrium quantum spin liquids in engineered superconducting networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how isolated, coupled quantum systems, such as qubits, can exhibit thermalization and dephasing despite evolving unitarily. By deriving and solving a weak-coupling master equation, the authors show that non-linear coupling terms are necessary to drive the system towards a thermal state, while linear coupling alone is insufficient. The findings have implications for understanding energy relaxation in quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in isolated quantum systems, providing crucial insights into how thermalization can occur purely through internal interactions without coupling to an external bath, which is vital for understanding quantum thermalization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the phenomenon of thermalization and dephasing within an isolated quantum system, contrasting this behavior with the standard definition of unitary evolution.

**Main result.** The system's ability to thermalize depends critically on the coupling mechanism: linear coupling fails to achieve thermal equilibrium, whereas non-linearity (three-wave mixing) ensures a thermal distribution determined by the initial total energy.

**Method.** The analysis employs a weak-coupling master equation approach, deriving time evolution equations for the density matrix elements of a subsystem coupled to the rest of the system.

**Model / system.** The model consists of an isolated set of $N$ coupled qubits (or weakly anharmonic oscillators). The dynamics are analyzed by treating one qubit as the system and the remaining qubits as an effective bath, considering both linear and cubic (three-wave mixing) coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Populations ($ho_{gg}, ho_{ee}$), coherence ($ho_{ge}$), and the final steady-state temperature ($eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak coupling limit, initial total energy ($\Delta E$), and the presence/absence of non-linearity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving a weak-coupling master equation and making approximations regarding the coupling strength and the nature of the bath interaction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup comparing global unitary evolution to subsystem analysis, show time traces of populations for WAOs under three-wave mixing, and display the decay of off-diagonal elements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using a weak-coupling master equation for a subsystem. It then analyzes the results for linear coupling (showing no thermalization) and contrasts this with the non-linear case (showing thermalization). Finally, it presents potential experimental realizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A system evolving unitarily does not dephase or thermalize by definition. Yet, if one considers a part of a unitary system, situation is different. Here we analyze an isolated set of coupled qubits taking one qubit at a time as a subsystem, and writing self-consistently a weak-coupling master equation for it. Weakly anharmonic oscillators and ideal qubits (two-level systems) demonstrate dephasing, i.e. the diagonal elements of the density matrix reach a steady-state in the long-time limit, and the off-diagonal elements of ideal qubits decay exponentially. For the linear coupling between the qubits, only the degenerate ones interact, and the system does not reach thermal distribution. However, non-linearity, in our analysis in form of three-wave mixing, ensures a thermal distribution in the long time limit, where the temperature is determined uniquely by the energy in the initial non-thermal state. Finally, we present a potential experimental scheme based on a superconducting quantum circuit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive analytical description of how systems approach a non-equilibrium steady state in nested stochastic resetting processes. It derives key diagnostics like accumulation times and shows that relaxation is bottlenecked by the slowest resetting component. These results have potential applications in modeling relaxation and transport in biological systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of non-equilibrium steady states and relaxation dynamics in complex, coupled stochastic systems is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum transport models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the approach to a non-equilibrium steady state (NESS) in nested stochastic resetting processes.

**Main result.** Relaxation dynamics are shown to be governed by the minimum resetting rate in the system, and the accumulation time for steady state establishment grows linearly along the resetting chain.

**Method.** The authors derive analytical solutions using renewal equation approaches, Fokker-Planck equations, and Laplace transforms to analyze the time evolution.

**Model / system.** The model involves $N$ Brownian particles on $\mathbb{R}$ undergoing nested stochastic resetting, where each particle resets to the position of the preceding one. The analysis is extended to include heterogeneous resetting rates.

**Key observables.** Accumulation time ($T_{r,n}(x)$), steady-state probability distributions ($P_{r,n}(x)$), and spatial moments (e.g., variance, excess kurtosis).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Resetting rates ($r$), diffusion coefficient ($D$), and the number of particles ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to one spatial dimension ($d=1$) and assumes Poissonian resetting protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic approach to steady state, notably showing the linear increase of the accumulation time along the resetting chain.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from single-particle resetting analysis to the nested extension, deriving time-dependent PDFs and characterizing the relaxation dynamics for both equal and heterogeneous resetting rates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterise the approach to nonequilibrium steady state in nested resetting processes by deriving accumulation times for the system, which quantify the effective first-passage time for the local establishment of steady state. For equal resetting rates, we obtain closed-form expressions showing that relaxation propagates as a wavefront with a delay that increases linearly along the resetting chain. We then extend this analysis to heterogeneous resetting rates, deriving exact steady-state distributions, spatial moments and accumulation times for both degenerate and non-degenerate resetting rates. We show that relaxation to steady state is ultimately governed by the minimum resetting rate in the system and, in degenerate systems, its multiplicity. These results provide a comprehensive analytical description of first-passage to nonequilibrium steady state in hierarchically coupled stochastic resetting systems, with potential applications to relaxation and transport processes in biological systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a thermodynamic efficiency measure for communication channels by bounding the entropy production cost. It shows that optimizing this efficiency favors biased inputs that minimize costly state transitions, rather than maximizing raw information capacity. This has implications for understanding energy dissipation in natural and artificial information processing systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on non-equilibrium thermodynamics, entropy production bounds, and the interplay between information capacity and thermodynamic cost is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum thermodynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives bounds on the thermodynamic cost (entropy production) associated with communication channels and defines a thermodynamic efficiency metric.

**Main result.** The thermodynamic efficiency is bounded by unity, and maximizing it favors a biased input that switches rarely, leading to spontaneous symmetry breaking in simple models.

**Method.** The analysis uses information theory concepts (mutual information, capacity) combined with non-equilibrium thermodynamics, decomposing entropy production into adiabatic and non-adiabatic parts.

**Model / system.** The study applies to a general class of communication channels relevant to both artificial and natural systems, exemplified by the Binary Symmetric Channel (BSC) and a minimal cellular sensing model.

**Key observables.** Mutual Information ($I$), Channel Capacity ($C$), Entropy Production ($\Sigma$), and Thermodynamic Efficiency ($\eta = I/\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Input bias parameter ($b$), channel error rate ($\epsilon$), and the ratio of transition to idle entropy production ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes specific time-scale separations and that the cost must account for transitions between successive symbols, not just the input symbols themselves.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of efficiency ($\eta$) on input bias ($b$) and show the non-trivial symmetry breaking transition in the BSC case.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the fundamental thermodynamic bound ($k_b I \le \Sigma_s$), defines efficiency, and then applies this framework to specific models like the BSC, demonstrating how optimization leads to biased, symmetry-breaking inputs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We identify a broad class of communication channels that captures common physical constraints in both artificial and natural systems and derive bounds on their thermodynamic cost. We find that the entropy production per channel use is bounded from below by the input-output mutual information, and their ratio -mutual information divided by entropy production- defines the thermodynamic efficiency. Unlike previous studies of energy-constrained communication channels, our analysis shows that thermodynamic costs must be assigned not only to the input symbols themselves, but also to transitions between successive symbols. As a result, maximizing thermodynamic efficiency favors a biased input that switches only rarely, rather than the capacity-achieving input. For the binary symmetric channel, this preference emerges through a pitchfork bifurcation that spontaneously breaks the symmetry of the channel. A minimal model of cellular sensing exhibits the same phenomenon.

</details>

**Authors:** Jinjie Liu, Zhongcan Ouyang, Hao Wu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26734v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive theoretical model to describe the mechanics of endocytosis, integrating non-specific entropic depletion forces with specific binding and the viscoelastic resistance of the cell cortex. By using an Onsager variational approach, the authors predict how these competing forces govern the rate and feasibility of membrane wrapping. The findings highlight the crucial role of non-specific interactions in initiating cellular uptake processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biophysics, the use of the Onsager variational principle to couple dissipation (viscoelasticity) with free energy minimization (depletion/binding) provides a general framework applicable to non-equilibrium dynamics in complex soft matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the physical mechanisms driving the initial approach and subsequent engulfment during receptor-mediated endocytosis, focusing on non-specific forces.

**Main result.** The model predicts that entropic depletion attraction provides a possible non-specific force contributing to membrane proximity, and the wrapping time is sensitive to cell stiffness and binding energy density.

**Method.** It combines depletion attraction (derived from excluded volume theory), ligand-receptor binding, membrane deformation (using Helfrich-Canham), and cytoskeletal viscoelastic dissipation (using a hereditary integral) within an Onsager variational framework.

**Model / system.** The system models a spherical particle (virus) approaching and wrapping around a cell membrane, influenced by crowders (depletants) in the extracellular space and the viscoelastic properties of the underlying cytoskeleton.

**Key observables.** Wrapping time, conditional minimum ligand density, finite particle-size window, and the depletion potential/force.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Crowder radius ($r$), virus radius ($R$), membrane stiffness, cytoskeletal viscosity ($\eta_c$), and binding energy density.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The extension of the planar depletion volume calculation to the curved wrapping phase is an approximation, and the viscoelastic response is modeled using a standard linear solid.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the entropy-induced engulfment model, showing the virus, depletion zones, and the overlap region representing increased accessible volume for crowders.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the depletion potential for planar geometry, then extends this to a reduced continuum model incorporating viscoelasticity and binding energy, culminating in kinetic predictions for the wrapping time and size window.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Receptor-mediated endocytosis requires a particle to approach the cell membrane to within a few nanometers before ligand--receptor binding can occur. Existing continuum models often start from an already established contact and do not explicitly describe how crowding particles on the extracellular side influence the distribution of the particle near the membrane. We examine entropic depletion forces as one possible nonspecific contribution to this initial approach. For ideal depletants, the Asakura--Oosawa excluded-volume construction gives an exact depletion potential for the planar geometry before contact. The potential and force vanish continuously at the onset of excluded-volume overlap. This interaction provides a possible contribution to membrane proximity before specific binding, while its extension to curved wrapping geometries requires additional approximation. Within a reduced continuum model, we combine depletion attraction, ligand--receptor binding, membrane deformation, and cytoskeletal viscoelastic dissipation. The viscoelastic contact is formulated through a hereditary integral and a standard linear solid. The kinetic model gives a conditional minimum ligand density for complete engulfment, a finite particle-size window, and a stiffness-dependent upper limit. When the stationary radius lies inside the domain of finite positive wrapping times, the estimated wrapping time has a minimum at a radius that decreases with increasing binding energy density. At fixed viscosity and other independent parameters, the same time approximation predicts slower wrapping as cell stiffness increases. The two positive roots defining the size window merge at a limiting parameter value, which characterizes closure of the admissible size interval. Depletion attraction is interpreted as one possible contribution to particle-membrane association

</details>

In the light brown region, the correct ligand cannot reach Na &gt; 10 before 275 s. The light green region is where 275 s &gt; ta &gt; 150 s, and the light red region is where ta &lt; 150 s. Data are collected at the locations marked by symbols. The dashed curve is the fitted function c(Jb) = (0.0721 + 0.0248e4Jb)t0/ta with ta = 150 s.</figcaption>
</figure>

</figure>

**Summary.** This work proposes a minimal model for T cell receptor activation to explain how a system achieves high specificity using only a few correct ligands. It shows that the coupling between neighboring receptors allows a single correct ligand to trigger a large, rapid signal, while incorrect ligands alone fail to do so. The analysis uses kinetic modeling and phase diagrams to map out the optimal parameter regimes for signal detection.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of coupled, non-equilibrium dynamics and the mapping to an Ising-like model to describe biological signaling pathways is relevant to understanding collective behavior in open quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mechanism by which incorrect ligands can assist in the specific detection of a correct ligand, mimicking T cell receptor (TCR) activation.

**Main result.** The system exhibits high specificity because incorrect ligands alone cannot activate the receptors, but the binding of a single correct ligand can trigger a rapid, amplified signal through coupling effects.

**Method.** The authors developed and analyzed a minimal kinetic model simulating receptor binding and activation on a lattice, mapping the dynamics to an Ising-like Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a small cluster of immobile receptors interacting with two types of ligands (correct/incorrect) and governed by enzyme-mediated activation/deactivation rates. The interactions include nearest-neighbor coupling ($J_b$) and reversible binding.

**Key observables.** Detector output (number of fully activated receptors), signal amplification, and the activation time ($t_a$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Binding-state coupling strength ($J_b$), incorrect ligand concentration ($c$), and kinetic rates ($r_a, r_d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model is a simplification of TCRs; the coupling term for boundary receptors is set to zero; the derivation of the cluster formation time neglects merging processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of activation states with and without a correct ligand, and phase diagrams map out parameter regimes satisfying criteria for high specificity, sensitivity, and rapid response.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the minimal model, details the kinetic and energetic framework (Hamiltonian), analyzes the performance criteria (specificity, sensitivity, speed), and presents results showing the role of the correct ligand as a nucleation seed.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Intrigued by the response of T cell receptors to the presence of a few agonist ligands, we propose a minimal model that can achieve similar performance. The model consists of a small cluster of immobile receptors that bind reversibly to two types (correct/incorrect) of ligands in the environment, with slightly weaker binding strength for the incorrect one. It features binding-state coupling between nearest-neighbor receptors, and receptors in the bound/free states are activated/deactivated by specific enzymes, with rates that allow kinetic proofreading. It is found that, for a range of binding-state coupling strength, incorrect ligands alone cannot activate the receptors, but the binding of merely one correct ligand to a receptor is sufficient to promote the activation of other receptors via induced binding to incorrect ligands. Both response time and signal amplification increase as the receptor binding-state coupling strength increases until it reaches an optimal range to achieve the most rapid and sensitive response. These results suggest a possible mechanism for a speedy and specific response of receptors to very few correct ligands in biological and artificial systems at the subcellular scale.

</details>

### [Kinetic Interference in Translational Control: A Path-Measure Framework for Collision-Triggered Transcript Decay](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25536v1)

**Authors:** Shlomo Segal  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25536v1>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a theoretical framework to model collision-triggered transcript decay during translation by applying advanced path-measure techniques from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics. It aims to show that perturbing the *timing* of translation, rather than just the overall rate, generates unique physical predictions. The work is presented as a research program, requiring rigorous testing against established models to validate its novel physical insights.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of path-measure decomposition to distinguish between rate-level effects and abundance-level effects is a sophisticated non-equilibrium statistical mechanics tool applicable to complex, driven biological systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a rigorous framework to study how perturbing the *timing* (rate) of molecular processes, rather than just the *abundance*, can trigger specific biological outcomes, namely collision-triggered transcript decay.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a conceptual framework based on path-measure decomposition, proposing specific, falsifiable tests to determine if the predicted dynamics are unique to ribosomal queueing and collision-triggered decay, rather than being derivable from standard exclusion process models.

**Method.** The methodology connects the path-measure formulation of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (decomposing the action into entropic and frenetic sectors) with stochastic modeling of ribosomal traffic.

**Model / system.** The system is ribosomal traffic on messenger RNA (mRNA) during translation elongation, modeled as a driven flow involving queue formation and collision-triggered removal. The mathematical tools involve path-measure analysis and comparison against established lattice exclusion models (SSEP/TASEP).

**Key observables.** Collision count statistics ($N_{	ext{coll}}(T)$), selectivity metrics (AUC for desirable vs. toxic transcript loss), and the difference in the frenetic term ($\Delta K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initiation rate ($\alpha$), codon-pair burden, and the specific perturbation applied via antisense oligonucleotides (ASOs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on the Markov property assumption when projecting the multi-state elongation cycle onto a scalar coordinate, and the core claims of selectivity and non-redundancy are currently untested hypotheses.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured as a research program proposal, first introducing the path-measure formalism, then comparing its predictions against existing exclusion process models to test for non-redundancy, and finally outlining a sequence of falsifiable tests (Milestones 1, 2, and 3) that must be passed for the theory to hold.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

I connect two literatures developed independently: the path-measure formulation of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, where a trajectory action decomposes into a time-antisymmetric (entropic) and time-symmetric (frenetic) sector, and the stochastic modelling of ribosomal traffic on messenger RNA. The biological target is a proposed intervention -- antisense oligonucleotide (ASO) interference with wobble-uridine (U34) modification of transfer RNA -- whose intended effect is not to abolish translation but to perturb its timing, driving ribosome collisions and collision-triggered transcript decay preferentially on high-flux, codon-biased transcripts.   Dynamical-activity and large-deviation analyses of generic lattice exclusion models -- notably the symmetric and totally asymmetric simple exclusion processes -- are well established. To my knowledge their formalization specifically for ribosomal traffic queues, translation elongation, and collision-triggered no-go decay remains unoccupied; this paper addresses that narrower gap, not the general one.   Two claims here are load-bearing and untested. First, selectivity: transcripts whose loss is therapeutically desirable are separable, by vulnerable-codon-pair burden, from transcripts whose loss is toxic. Second, non-redundancy: the frenetic decomposition yields predictions, specific to ribosomal queueing and collision-triggered decay, not already obtainable from rate-level exclusion-process models or existing activity/large-deviation analyses of exclusion processes. This paper establishes neither. It specifies both as falsifiable tests with pre-registered decision rules, including outcomes under which the framework should be abandoned or narrowed. It is a research programme proposal, not a result. No new experimental, computational, or bioinformatic results are reported.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report the successful construction and operation of a dual-region apparatus designed to trap single fermionic ytterbium atoms near an in-vacuum nanofiber cavity. They overcome challenges in atom delivery by implementing a pulsed transport sequence, enabling single-atom loading into an optical tweezer array. The key achievement is demonstrating stable, multi-second trapping of single atoms in this complex, integrated quantum system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines state-of-the-art techniques in cold atom trapping (moving molasses, MOTs) with advanced quantum optics elements (in-vacuum nanofiber cavities) for fundamental quantum physics experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to develop and demonstrate a dual-region apparatus capable of trapping single fermionic ytterbium atoms near an in-vacuum nanofiber cavity.

**Main result.** The system successfully demonstrated single-atom loading into a tweezer array near the cavity and achieved a measurable single-atom trapping lifetime of 9.5(7) seconds.

**Method.** The experiment utilized a pulsed transport sequence based on longitudinal moving molasses to move atoms from a source region to a science chamber containing the trapping optics.

**Model / system.** The platform involves a dual-region setup using fermionic ytterbium-171 atoms. Atoms are cooled and transported using $399	ext{ nm}$ light, captured in a $3	ext{D-MOT}$ ($556	ext{ nm}$), and finally loaded into optical tweezers positioned near an in-vacuum nanofiber cavity.

**Key observables.** Single-atom filling fraction (up to 74.9%), $1/e$ trap lifetime (9.5(7) s), and the number of atoms delivered during transport.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Source-to-science chamber distance ($\approx 240	ext{ mm}$), tweezer array distance from nanofiber ($\approx 50\ \mu	ext{m}$), and optimal transport parameters (e.g., $t_{	ext{launch}} \simeq 1.2 	ext{ ms}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The design assumes that the dual-region architecture can provide sufficient atom flux while maintaining optical access and protecting the nanofiber cavity from untrapped atoms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of the dual-region apparatus and single-atom tweezer operation, while Figure 2 characterizes the pulsed transport sequence by showing atom number versus time of flight and launch duration.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the development of the dual-region apparatus, characterizes the pulsed atom transport sequence from the source to the science chamber, demonstrates single-atom loading into the tweezer array near the cavity, and finally reports the measured trapping lifetime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a dual-region experimental apparatus for trapping single atoms of the fermionic isotope ytterbium-171 (${}^{171}\mathrm{Yb}$) with optical tweezers in a science chamber containing an in-vacuum nanofiber cavity. A source region is located approximately $240\,\mathrm{mm}$ from the science chamber, preserving optical access around the chamber for high-numerical-aperture tweezer optics. A pulsed transport sequence based on longitudinal moving molasses launches atomic pulses toward the science chamber, where they are decelerated and recaptured in a magneto-optical trap. We subsequently load single atoms into a $2\times2$ tweezer array located approximately $50\,\mathrm{μm}$ from the nanofiber and measure a $1/e$ lifetime of $9.5(7)\,\mathrm{s}$. These results demonstrate that the dual-region architecture provides sufficient ${}^{171}\mathrm{Yb}$ atoms for single-atom tweezer operation near an in-vacuum nanofiber cavity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced theoretical tools to study superconducting circuits, particularly focusing on the Josephson junction nonlinearity. By employing Momentous Quantum Mechanics with a Gaussian closure, the authors derive accurate, non-perturbative effective Hamiltonians. They rigorously compare this approach against standard approximations and various models of dissipation, offering a robust framework for analyzing quantum dynamics in superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the non-perturbative dynamics of superconducting circuits, a cornerstone of modern quantum optics and quantum information research, by providing advanced techniques to handle strong nonlinearities and dissipation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the non-perturbative study of superconducting circuits, overcoming the limitations of standard approximations (like Taylor expansions) when modeling the Josephson junction potential.

**Main result.** The authors derive a closed-form effective Hamiltonian using a Gaussian closure method, and show that the Bateman dynamics provides a faithful semiclassical description of dissipation that reproduces the Lindblad benchmark for weak damping.

**Method.** The study employs Momentous Quantum Mechanics (MQM) combined with a Gaussian closure technique to resum infinite moment hierarchies, alongside comparisons between various dissipation models (Lindblad, Bateman, Caldirola-Kanai).

**Model / system.** The physical system is superconducting circuits, modeled by Josephson junctions (JJ) and coupled to cavities or resonators. The dynamics are governed by Hamiltonians derived from Lagrangian formulations, including the standard JJ Hamiltonian and coupled cavity-JJ systems.

**Key observables.** Quantum-dressed frequency ($\omega_{	ext{eff}}$), the quantum width $G_{2,0}(t)$, and the comparison of physical uncertainty ($U_{	ext{phys}}(t)$) versus canonical uncertainty ($U_{	ext{can}}(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless ratio $E_J/E_C$ controls the regime, with the transmon regime ($E_J/E_C \gg 1$) being a focus. The validity of approximations is tested by the damping parameter $\lambda/ \omega_1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is the Gaussian closure assumption, which is monitored by the quantum width $G_{2,0}(t)$. Standard treatments are limited by double truncation errors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circuit diagram, the coherent quantum-state-transfer in coupled cavity dynamics, and the decay of physical uncertainty in damped oscillators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the fundamental circuit models (JJ, cavity-JJ) to introducing the advanced theoretical framework (MQM, Gaussian closure). It then benchmarks this method against known results for dissipation (Lindblad vs. Bateman) and analyzes the resulting dynamics and error control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We apply the momentous quantum mechanics formalism to the non-perturbative study of superconducting circuits in the transmon regime, deriving effective equations of motion for the bare Josephson junction (JJ), the cavity--JJ system, and a dissipative resonator. A Gaussian closure on the hierarchy of quantum moments resums the full cosine nonlinearity into the closed-form effective Hamiltonian $\tfrac{1}{2}[V(φ+φ_s)+V(φ-φ_s)]$, which is non-perturbative and preserves the periodicity and boundedness of the Josephson potential at all phase amplitudes; the standard Kerr (Duffing) approximation is recovered as a special case. We derive the quantum-dressed frequency $ω_{\rm eff}=Ω_p\sqrt{\cos(φ_{\rm zpf}/φ_0)}$, and benchmark the effective dynamics against exact Mathieu-function diagonalization, with the quantum width $G^{2,0}(t)$ providing the most sensitive diagnostic of the closure's validity and marks the boundary of the Gaussian approximation more sharply than $\langle\hatφ\rangle(t)$ does. We compare the Caldirola--Kanai, Bateman, and Lindblad descriptions: the Bateman dynamics, quantized with a switched symplectic structure, preserves the Heisenberg bound, admits exact closed-form moment solutions, and reproduces the Lindblad benchmark for weak damping, i.e., within the analytic error bound $(λ/ω_1)^2\,φ_{\rm zpf}^2$.

</details>

For n = 0 (2), the oscillator’s equilibrium position shifts to ±X0 = ±α/(Mω2 ph) while the potential energy minimum reduces to Vn(±X0) ≡V0 = −α2/(2Mω2 ph). (b), (c) The change in the atomic position ∆ˆ Xc and momentum ∆ˆPc for the HH and HSSH models, respectively, for an L = 12 site chain with U = 8th, g = 0.2th, ωph = th. **Summary.** This work uses time-dependent DMRG simulations to compare the non-equilibrium response of two electron-phonon coupling models: Holstein and Su-Schrieffer-Heeger. The authors find that while electronic measurements fail to distinguish the couplings, the resulting coherent lattice dynamics provide a robust signature, showing distinct phonon oscillations for the Holstein model versus none for the SSH model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear distinction in lattice dynamics provides a novel, measurable diagnostic tool for probing the microscopic nature of electron-phonon coupling in strongly correlated materials, which is highly relevant to understanding high-Tc superconductivity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if coherent lattice dynamics can distinguish between different microscopic electron-phonon (e-ph) coupling mechanisms (Holstein vs. Su-Schrieffer-Heeger) when the system is driven out of equilibrium.

**Main result.** While electronic responses are similar, the lattice dynamics differ substantially: the Holstein model shows long-lived coherent phonon oscillations, whereas the SSH model does not.

**Method.** Time-dependent density matrix renormalization group (DMRG) simulations were used to calculate the time evolution of electronic and lattice observables following an ultrafast pump pulse.

**Model / system.** The study investigates a one-dimensional Mott insulator coupled to phonons, comparing the Hubbard-Holstein (HH) model (local potential modulation) and the Hubbard-SSH (HSSH) model (hopping modulation).

**Key observables.** Lattice dynamics (e.g., $\Delta E_{	ext{ph}}$, $\Delta \hat{X}_{c	ext{disp}}$) and electronic observables ($\Delta E_{	ext{total}}$, $\Delta E_U$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is analyzed in the Mott-insulating regime, with key parameters including the coupling strength ($\alpha$), phonon frequency ($\omega_{	ext{ph}}$), and pump frequency ($\omega_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The contrast in lattice dynamics is robust against changes in phonon frequency and pump parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pump-induced electronic response ($\Delta E_{	ext{total}}$) showing qualitative similarity, contrasted by figures showing the lattice response, where HH exhibits clear coherent oscillations while HSSH does not.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem by comparing the Hamiltonians of HH and HSSH models. It then simulates the non-equilibrium dynamics using DMRG, showing that while electronic properties are insensitive to the coupling type, the resulting coherent lattice dynamics provide a clear, distinguishing signature.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy provides a powerful means to investigate electron-phonon (e-ph) interactions in strongly correlated systems. Nevertheless, the question of how different microscopic e-ph coupling mechanisms influence the lattice's nonequilibrium response has not been widely addressed. We perform time-dependent density matrix renormalization group simulations on a pumped one-dimensional Mott insulator coupled to phonons through either Holstein or Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) interactions. The electronic responses of the two models are qualitatively similar in the Mott-insulating regime and are governed by the same excitations and resonance conditions of the Hubbard model. In contrast, the lattice dynamics of the two models differ substantially. While the Hubbard-Holstein model exhibits long-lived coherent phonon oscillations when the pump resonates with the bare phonon frequency, no such oscillations are observed for the Hubbard-SSH model. These contrasting responses remain robust against changes in phonon frequency and pump parameters, suggesting that coherent lattice dynamics can provide a direct nonequilibrium probe of the microscopic form of e-ph coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that photon statistics provide a new control knob for strong-field atomic physics, specifically controlling Rydberg-state excitation. Using advanced quantum-optical models, the authors show that field fluctuations enhance excitation in the multiphoton regime but suppress it in the tunneling regime. This highlights that field statistics can independently control both the probability and the symmetry of the final bound electronic state.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses how nonclassical light properties (photon statistics) can be harnessed to control quantum dynamics, offering a powerful tool for manipulating final-state symmetries and excitation probabilities in atomic physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the statistical properties of the driving laser field (photon statistics) can be used as a novel control mechanism to govern the formation of bound states, specifically Rydberg-state excitation (RSE), in strong-field atomic interactions.

**Main result.** Field fluctuations enhance multiphoton RSE by broadening resonance access, but they suppress the RSE mediated by tunneling, a suppression attributed to the ensemble-induced dispersion of relative path phases.

**Method.** The research employs a combination of a quantum-optical phase-space formulation coupled to time-dependent Schrödinger equation (TDSE) calculations and a Quantum-Statistical Recapture (QSR) model.

**Model / system.** The system models an atom (like Hydrogen) interacting with intense quantum light fields, comparing coherent, Bright Squeezed Vacuum (BSV), and Phase-Squeezed Coherent (PSC) states. The dynamics are governed by the joint atom-field density operator.

**Key observables.** Rydberg-state excitation (RSE) yield, Rydberg-manifold parity, and the decomposition of RSE into individual-path and interference components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon statistics (field fluctuations), mean peak intensity, and the distinction between the multiphoton and tunneling ionization regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes the electric-dipole approximation and relies on the QSR model, which treats the field statistics via ensemble averaging.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate RSE dynamics comparing coherent vs. BSV driving, showing yield dependence on intensity, and demonstrating how photon statistics reshape the parity composition of the final Rydberg manifold.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the effects of different field statistics (coherent vs. squeezed) on RSE across different intensity regimes, using the QSR model to quantify the enhancement in the multiphoton regime and the suppression in the tunneling regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photon statistics provides a new control dimension for strong-field bound-state formation. Using a quantum-optical phase-space formulation coupled to time-dependent Schrödinger-equation calculations, we show that field fluctuations enhance multiphoton Rydberg excitation by broadening AC-Stark-shifted resonances, but suppress tunneling-mediated recapture even as ionization increases. A quantum-statistical recapture model attributes the latter to ensemble-induced dispersion of relative path phases. Photon statistics also reshapes Rydberg-manifold parity, linking field statistics to recapture coherence and final-state symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel acousto-optic modulator design on a CMOS platform that overcomes the spectral limitations of single-frequency modulation. By engineering the structure to support multiple, harmonically spaced resonances, the authors can synthesize complex, non-sinusoidal light waveforms. This breakthrough significantly enhances frequency control for visible light, making it highly relevant for scaling quantum gate operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical hardware bottleneck in quantum computing: generating high-fidelity, high-frequency optical control signals. The demonstrated high efficiency and manufacturability are crucial for integrating quantum light sources with classical photonic infrastructure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Scaling quantum control for atomic, ionic, and solid-state emitters requires high-power, gigahertz-frequency spectral control of visible light in a manufacturable platform.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a method using harmonically spaced resonances to achieve programmable, non-sinusoidal modulation waveforms, significantly improving frequency-shifting efficiency and suppression levels compared to single-tone systems.

**Method.** They engineered a silicon nitride microstructure to support multiple, harmonically spaced mechanical resonances, which are then piezoelectrically controlled to synthesize complex modulation signals.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an acousto-optic modulator fabricated on a 200 mm CMOS platform using silicon nitride photonics integrated with an aluminum nitride piezoelectric actuator. The modulation relies on coupling multiple mechanical resonances to a guided visible-wavelength optical mode.

**Key observables.** Conversion efficiency (up to 50% sideband conversion), frequency shift suppression (60 dB carrier, 53 dB image suppression), and the ability to generate flat multi-line frequency combs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Harmonically spaced resonance frequencies (e.g., 1.14 GHz and 2.28 GHz), modulation depths ($eta_n$), and relative phase control.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique overcomes the spectral limitation imposed by single-tone modulation by utilizing multiple, harmonically spaced resonances, which is shown to be scalable to more harmonics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, the principle of two-tone harmonic phase modulation, and the resulting spectral comb lines. Supplementary figures detail the theoretical modeling of sideband generation and efficiency optimization.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the need for high-speed, programmable light control for quantum systems. It then proposes the use of multi-harmonic acousto-optics on a CMOS platform. The core work involves demonstrating the synthesis of tailored waveforms and achieving record suppression levels, concluding with discussions on scalability and quantum applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scaling quantum control for atoms, ions, and solid-state emitters requires gigahertz-frequency spectral control of high-power visible light in a volume-manufacturable platform. Silicon nitride photonics provides high power handling and CMOS-foundry compatibility but has no intrinsic mechanism for high-speed modulation. Integration with piezoelectric materials enables acousto-optic phase modulation, and mechanical resonant enhancement has made it efficient at gigahertz frequencies. However, a single resonance restricts the modulation waveform to a single tone, imposing Bessel-function sideband amplitudes and limiting frequency-shifting efficiency to 33.9%. Here we engineer a silicon nitride acousto-optic microstructure to support harmonically spaced resonances at 1.14 GHz and 2.28 GHz, each strongly optomechanically coupled to a 730 nm guided optical mode, so that tailored non-sinusoidal modulation waveforms can be resonantly synthesized. By piezoelectrically controlling the two mechanical amplitudes and their relative phase, we demonstrate 50% conversion to one sideband (1.5x the single-tone theoretical maximum), a flat seven-line comb, and a frequency shift with 60 dB carrier and 53 dB image suppression - to our knowledge the highest reported for an integrated modulator. The devices are fabricated in a 200 mm CMOS foundry, and we measure 91.7% yield without post-fabrication tuning across 36 devices from three wafers. We also show how the technique can be straightforwardly scaled to three or more harmonics. This result overcomes the trade-off between resonant enhancement and spectral programmability, with important consequences including improved single-qubit gate efficiency for hyperfine qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents an experimental realization of Device-Independent Quantum Key Distribution (DIQKD) over 25 km using an asymmetric architecture combining a trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion and a transmitted photon. By maintaining quantum correlations across the fiber link using frequency conversion and polarization stabilization, the team demonstrated a high CHSH parameter, proving a viable route for quantum networking.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of scaling quantum communication, specifically by integrating trapped-ion quantum memories with long-haul fiber links in a device-independent framework.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to demonstrate Device-Independent Quantum Key Distribution (DIQKD) over a practical, long-distance fiber network of 25 km.

**Main result.** The authors successfully achieved a CHSH parameter $|S|=2.75$, exceeding the required threshold, and estimated a secure key rate of 69 bits out of 10908 detected states.

**Method.** The protocol uses an event-ready, asymmetric atom-photon architecture where a trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion acts as one party and a transmitted photon acts as the other.

**Model / system.** The system couples a single trapped $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion to a photon transmitted over 25 km of telecom fiber. Key components include double quantum frequency conversion, active polarization stabilization, and SNSPD detection.

**Key observables.** Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt parameter ($|S|$), secret-key bits (SKB), and readout-failure probability ($	ext{p}_{	ext{fail}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Link distance of 25 km; DIQKD threshold $|S|=2.362$; Signal-to-Background Ratio (SBR) $\approx 10$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the protocol is event-ready and that the architecture is compatible with heterogeneous, repeater-compatible quantum networks.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a schematic of the entire atom-photon QKD link, while Figure 2 details the relevant energy levels and transitions of the $^{40}	ext{Ca}^+$ ion used for entanglement generation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the physical setup, including the complex optical and atomic control sequences (QFC, APC, laser cooling/readout), followed by the measurement statistics analysis (testing for setting dependence) and finally the calculation of the security parameters ($|S|$ and SKB).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Device-independent quantum key distribution (DIQKD) can guarantee security without trusting the internal workings of the measurement devices, but extending it to fiber networks demands high-quality entanglement, reliable heralding, and faithful photon transmission simultaneously. Here, we address these requirements in an event-ready asymmetric atom-photon architecture implemented over $25\,\mathrm{km}$ of spooled telecom fiber. A single trapped $^{40}\mathrm{Ca}^{+}$ ion forms one party of the protocol while a single transmitted photon measured at the remote station forms the other. Double quantum frequency conversion and active polarization stabilization preserve the atom-photon quantum correlation across the full link. We obtain a Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt parameter of $|S|=2.75^{+0.16}_{-0.15}$, exceeding the threshold $|S|=2.362$ required for a positive asymptotic secret-key fraction under the DIQKD model used. Within the same asymptotic model, this corresponds to a conservative estimate of 69 secret-key bits out of 10908 detected Bell states. The demonstrated architecture establishes a route towards DIQKD in heterogeneous, repeater-compatible quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work uses the AdS/CFT correspondence via the D3-D7 system to study entanglement entropy in a current-carrying, strongly coupled conductor. The authors find that the entanglement anisotropy undergoes a smooth sign reversal as the charge density changes. This reversal is fundamentally linked to the stress tensor anisotropy of the holographic source, providing insights into non-equilibrium quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of entanglement anisotropy in non-equilibrium systems is highly relevant to understanding quantum correlations in driven quantum materials, providing a holographic tool to diagnose directional dependence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how a conserved charge density modifies the orientation dependence of equal-time spatial entanglement entropy in a non-equilibrium steady state (NESS) within a strongly coupled conductor.

**Main result.** The anisotropy in the entanglement entropy exhibits a density-driven sign reversal, which is traced to a radial sign competition in the full Dirac-Born-Infeld stress anisotropy. This reversal is a smooth crossover, not a phase transition.

**Method.** Holographic entanglement entropy is calculated using the Ryu–Takayanagi/HRT prescription, focusing on the leading probe-flavour correction derived from the linearized closed-string response.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the D3–D7 holographic conductor, representing a strongly coupled $3+1$ dimensional conductor at finite temperature and charge density, driven by a DC electric field.

**Key observables.** Leading probe-flavour contribution to equal-time spatial entanglement entropy, mutual information anisotropy, and the difference in entropy corrections for parallel vs. transverse subregions ($\Delta S_{\parallel} - \Delta S_{\perp}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric field magnitude ($E$), charge density ($\mu$), and the width ($\ell$) of the subregions being compared.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation is performed at leading order in the probe expansion, and the analysis isolates the homogeneous, spatially traceless response by taking the difference between two orientations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the geometry used for calculating the entropy difference for parallel and transverse orientations relative to the electric field.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the holographic framework for calculating entanglement entropy in a driven, non-equilibrium state, focusing on isolating the anisotropy via the difference between parallel and transverse cuts, leading to the identification of the density-dependent crossover mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the leading probe-flavour contribution to equal-time spatial entanglement entropy in a homogeneous 3+1-dimensional strongly coupled conductor at finite temperature and charge density, realised holographically by the D3-D7 system and driven by a constant DC electric field. We compare slab-shaped subregions that are extended in two spatial directions and have finite width parallel or transverse to the field. The difference between the corresponding entropy corrections isolates a homogeneous, spatially traceless response and exhibits a density-driven sign reversal. Over the investigated parameter range, the crossover density increases with the electric-field magnitude and decreases with the strip width. Mutual information shows the same qualitative reversal and provides an ultraviolet-finite check. We trace the effect to a radial sign competition in the full Dirac-Born-Infeld stress anisotropy. The reversal is a smooth response crossover rather than a thermodynamic phase transition.

</details>

**Authors:** Junyu Tang, Hongquan Lv, Gang v. Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25922v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</figure>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework to measure the topological properties of fractionalized spinons in a Mott insulator using optical spectroscopy. By analyzing the physical optical response as a combination of charge and spinon effects, the authors propose two protocols: one to extract the quantized spinon Chern number at low frequencies, and another to reconstruct the underlying quantum geometry (metric and Berry curvature) at resonance. This offers a pathway to experimentally verify topological order in strongly correlated quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology provides a powerful, experimentally relevant framework for probing topological invariants (like the Chern number) in strongly correlated systems using measurable optical responses, which is highly relevant for condensed matter theorists studying correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop quantitative optical protocols to extract the spinon Chern number and reconstruct the hidden quantum geometry of gapped spinon bands in the Topological Mott Regime.

**Main result.** The low-frequency ratio of longitudinal and Hall conductivities yields a self-calibrated estimator for the spinon Chern number, while an inverse-response Kramers-Kronig protocol allows reconstruction of the quantum metric and Berry curvature spectral densities.

**Method.** The authors use a slave-rotor formulation and analyze the physical optical response as an Ioffe-Larkin composition of charge and spinon responses, applying both low-frequency (off-shell) and resonant (on-shell) measurement techniques.

**Model / system.** The study models a two-dimensional topological Mott insulator, specifically using the half-filled triangular-lattice Hofstadter–Hubbard (TLHH) model, which simulates physics in moiré heterostructures under an orbital magnetic flux.

**Key observables.** Spinon Chern number ($C_s$), physical optical conductivity ($\sigma_{	ext{phys}}(\omega)$), longitudinal ($\sigma_L$) and Hall ($\sigma_H$) conductivities, quantum metric ($g_{ij}$), and Berry curvature ($\Omega_{ij}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Orbital flux ($\Phi_\Delta$), spinon gap ($\Delta_s$), and the ratio of hopping to repulsion ($U/t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the TLHH model as a representative system and assumes that the physical response is an Ioffe-Larkin composition, allowing for the separation of spinon contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the optical probe mechanism, showing the finite-frequency Chern-number estimator approaching quantized values at low frequency, and comparing direct integration versus reconstruction of the quantum metric and Berry curvature.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by deriving the Quantum Geometric Tensor for a two-band model. It then details the measurement protocols: the off-shell limit for the Chern number and the on-shell limit using inverse-response Kramers-Kronig relations to reconstruct spectral densities. Finally, it analyzes the robustness of these measurements against theoretical errors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inspired by recent interests in quantum geometry in electronic bands, we consider a two-dimensional topological Mott insulator, i.e. chiral spin liquid, whose spinon bands develop nontrivial band topological and quantum geometry. The virtual polarization of the gapped charge sector transfers the electromagnetic drive to the charge-neutral spinons through the emergent U(1) gauge field, producing a tensor Ioffe-Larkin response and enabling two complementary optical protocols for measurements. In the off-shell (low-frequency) regime, a self-calibrated ratio of the physical longitudinal and Hall conductivities yields the spinon Chern number without the microscopic knowledge of the charge response. In the on-shell (resonant) regime, combining the inverse Ioffe-Larkin response with the Kramers-Kronig relation enables reconstruction of the quantum-metric and Berry-curvature spectral densities. Our approach directly applies to the triangular lattice Hofstadter-Hubbard model in Moiré systems, can be well adapted to other triangular lattice antiferromagnets such as Nb$_3$Br$_8$ with appropriate modifications. We establish a unified framework for quantitatively probing the quantum geometry of fractionalized quasiparticles in topological Mott regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel variational approach to simulate strongly correlated fermions, specifically using the 2D Hubbard model. It integrates a neural network backflow transformation with a low-rank multi-determinant ansatz within a VMC framework. This combination allows for scalable and accurate calculations, achieving benchmark energies while capturing complex physical phenomena like charge/spin ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on Hubbard models, the use of deep learning to parameterize complex many-body wavefunctions provides a powerful, generalizable tool for simulating strongly correlated quantum matter beyond traditional tensor network limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating strongly correlated fermions is challenging due to the exponential Hilbert space complexity and intricate wavefunction sign structures.

**Main result.** The method achieves variational energies for the 2D Hubbard model within 0.45% of AFQMC at half-filling and successfully captures charge/spin stripe patterns at 1/8 doping.

**Method.** It introduces a variational framework combining deep learning with Variational Monte Carlo, using a neural network backflow transformation applied to a low-rank multi-determinant ansatz.

**Model / system.** The primary system studied is the two-dimensional Hubbard model, characterized by nearest-neighbor hopping and on-site Coulomb repulsion U. The method is applied at both half-filling and 1/8 doping.

**Key observables.** Energy expectation value per site, relative error compared to AFQMC, and visualization of charge/spin density stripe patterns.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio U/t (e.g., U/t=2 to U/t=8) and the doping level (half-filling vs. 1/8 doping).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of low-rank factorization for determinant updates is crucial for maintaining computational efficiency and scalability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall workflow, from input embedding through the backflow network to the multi-determinant expansion. Figure 2 likely shows energy benchmarks on lattices of varying sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the architecture, starting with the general variational framework, elaborating on the deep backflow transformation and its application to the multi-determinant wavefunction, and concluding with benchmarking results and discussions on computational scaling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating strongly correlated fermions remains a long-standing challenge due to the exponential complexity of the Hilbert space and the intricate sign structure of many-body wavefunctions. We introduce a variational framework centered on a neural network backflow transformation that combines deep learning with variational Monte Carlo. The proposed ansatz employs a multi-determinant expansion with low-rank shifts to capture non-local correlations and complex sign structures. Applied to the two-dimensional Hubbard model at both half-filling and $1/8$ doping, the method achieves energies within $0.45\%$ of auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo at half-filling and captures intertwined charge- and spin-density stripe patterns at $1/8$ doping. These results demonstrate the potential of this framework as a scalable and interpretable approach to variational simulations of strongly correlated fermionic systems.

</details>
