**Summary.** This work analyzes the statistical mechanics of classical fractons interacting via pair-inertia kernels, distinguishing between compact and non-compact interaction supports. Using advanced simulation techniques like ECMC and theoretical bounds, the authors establish that non-compact interactions lead to a stable, liquid-like equilibrium state, contrasting sharply with the ordered phases predicted by compact kernels.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of non-equilibrium steady states and the rigorous derivation of equilibrium ensembles for complex interacting particle systems, especially those exhibiting exotic symmetries or constraints, is highly relevant to understanding quantum many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the equilibrium statistical mechanics of classical fractons, focusing specifically on the behavior when the interaction kernels are non-compact.

**Main result.** For non-compact kernels, the system reaches an equilibrium liquid state characterized by short-range clustering and non-Gaussian momentum tails, unlike the ordered states found with compact kernels.

**Method.** The authors employ theoretical bounds (Graph-Laplacian, matrix-tree) to determine free energy scaling and use a nonreversible event-chain Monte Carlo (ECMC) algorithm to sample the coupled position-momentum phase space.

**Model / system.** The model consists of one-dimensional classical Machian fractons interacting via a pair-inertia kernel K(x), conserving a global dipole moment and total momentum. The Hamiltonian couples momentum differences through this kernel.

**Key observables.** Structure factor S(k), single-particle momentum distribution P(p), and the density-wave order parameter.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of the interaction kernel K(x) (compact vs. exponentially decaying vs. uniformly non-decaying) and the system density $ho$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a normalizable Gibbs ensemble for non-compact kernels, and the ECMC sampling is shown to match long-time averages of Hamiltonian dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate representative kernels (Fig. 1) and show the structure factor S(k) and momentum distribution P(p) for the equilibrium liquid state (Fig. 7).

**Paper structure.** The paper first contrasts compact vs. non-compact kernels, establishing the theoretical framework for free energy scaling. It then develops the ECMC algorithm to sample the equilibrium ensemble, culminating in the characterization of the resulting liquid state properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the equilibrium statistical mechanics of one-dimensional classical Machian fractons: particles whose dynamics conserves a global dipole moment and whose Hamiltonian couples momentum differences through a position-dependent pair-inertia kernel. Compactly supported interaction kernels have a divergence in the Gibbs partition function, and have been shown to break ergodicity and symmetry by forming non-equilibrium steady states with particle clusters, evading the Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner-Coleman theorem. In this paper, we consider kernels with non-compact support and study their ergodic properties. For exponentially decaying kernels, graph-Laplacian and matrix-tree bounds provide an extensive free energy suggesting that a putative statistical mechanical description is valid. Similarly, uniform non-local kernels have a super-extensive free energy and require a Kac rescaling. A generalized Hohenberg--Mermin--Wagner--Coleman argument, supported by finite-size scaling, implies symmetry-breaking density order parameter vanishes at all wave vectors melting the long-range translation-breaking density order of compact kernels. To study the resulting equilibrium ensemble, we construct a nonreversible event-chain Monte Carlo (ECMC) algorithm that samples the coupled position-momentum phase space while preserving the dipole moment and total momentum. The ECMC sampling is shown to quantitatively match long time-averaged quantites in Hamiltonian dynamics. The equilibrium liquid exhibits preferred short-range clustering and strongly non-Gaussian single-particle momentum tails associated with the correlated nature of positions and momenta. This paper provides a detailed investigation into the equilibrium liquid properties of the non-compact regime, whilst the companion paper investigates the mechanisms that relax the liquid.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study calculates the maximum possible renormalization of an electron's effective mass when confined in a cavity due to coupling with polarizable material mirrors. By using advanced methods like Quantum Monte Carlo and variational calculations, the authors establish a definitive upper bound for this effect. This work bridges the gap between fundamental cavity QED theory and practical dielectric engineering of electronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses cavity QED effects on material properties, providing quantitative limits on how vacuum field modification can engineer electronic parameters like effective mass, which is central to AMO physics applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the ultimate theoretical limit for electron mass renormalization induced by coupling to the vacuum electromagnetic field within a planar cavity.

**Main result.** The upper limit for mass renormalization is found to be of the same order as the bulk Landau-Pekar polaron result, and the calculated dependence on system parameters is continuous, resolving previous debates about phase transitions.

**Method.** The authors employ a combination of Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) evaluation of the Feynman path integral and variational minimization techniques applied to the polaron energy functional.

**Model / system.** The system models a two-dimensional electron monolayer placed inside a planar cavity whose mirrors are described by a Lorentz oscillator dielectric function, leading to field-mediated electron-phonon interactions.

**Key observables.** Electron mass renormalization ($\Delta m/m$) and the polaron binding energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless coupling strength ($\alpha_s$) and the normalized distance to the mirror ($\zeta = z_0 / \sqrt{2m\omega_s}$), which define the coupling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations assume the electrostatic (Coulomb) limit dominates at short distances, and the weak-coupling analysis assumes in-plane translation invariance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (planar cavity) and show comparisons of the calculated mass renormalization ($\Delta m/m$) versus the mirror distance ($\zeta$) using perturbative, QMC, and Landau-Pekar results.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem and model (2D electron in a cavity) to detailing the advanced computational methods (QMC, variational minimization) and finally presenting the results that establish the upper bound for mass renormalization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cavity-induced electron mass renormalization is an important ingredient of many proposals to control material properties through vacuum field modification. What is its ultimate limit? Here this question is answered by (i)~noting that this mass renormalization is essentially due to the field-mediated interaction between the electron and the polarizable degrees of freedom in the cavity mirrors, and (ii)~directly evaluating the corresponding Feynman path integral for a 2D electron by numerically exact quantum Monte Carlo. The sought upper limit is provided by the Landau-Pekar polaron in the planar geometry. The presented calculation bridges the gap between cavity control and dielectric engineering of material properties.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the phase diagram of an anyon gas, predicting a quantum phase transition between a crystalline state and a superfluid state. By studying the effective Hamiltonian in the large $k$ limit, the authors determine a critical value $k_c$ that contradicts common assumptions about anyon superfluidity. The findings are significant for understanding strongly correlated quantum matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of anyons and their phase transitions provides a theoretical framework relevant to strongly correlated electron systems, such as fractional quantum Hall states, which are key topics in condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits the long-standing problem of the anyon gas, specifically investigating the quantum phase transition between crystalline and superfluid states.

**Main result.** The system undergoes a quantum phase transition at a critical parameter $k_c \simeq 2-5$, implying that an anyon gas with only statistical interactions is not necessarily a superfluid.

**Method.** The analysis employs a double-scaling limit, semiclassical saddle-point methods, and Renormalization Group (RG) analysis of the effective Hamiltonian derived from a Chern-Simons action.

**Model / system.** The system models anyons (particles with fractional statistics $	heta = \pi/k$) at fixed density $\lambda = N/k$, described by an effective Hamiltonian involving density and phase variables.

**Key observables.** Unit-cell size, ground-state energy, acoustic excitations, and the critical value $k_c$ separating the two phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The statistical parameter $k$, the density parameter $\lambda = N/k$, and the critical value $k_c$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large $k$ limit (semiclassical expansion) and assumes the system is at the infrared-stable fixed point of the RG flow.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 (mentioned) shows the sequence of classical saddles found by minimizing the energy functional as a function of the density parameter $\lambda$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by deriving the effective Hamiltonian, analyzing the energy minimization in different density regimes ($\lambda$), and finally establishing the quantum phase transition boundary using RG techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the long-standing problem of the anyon gas. We study the solvable near-bosonic limit of a large number of anyons $N$ at fixed $N/k$, where $1/k$ characterizes the statistical interaction. In this limit, the ground state is a crystal with order $N/k$ lattice sites. The statistical interactions, mediated by a long-range gauge field, are completely screened within the crystalline phase. The resulting state is a conventional crystal without superfluidity. We characterize this anyon crystal by determining its unit-cell size, ground-state energy, acoustic excitations, angular momentum crossings, and other properties.   Our results indicate a quantum phase transition between the crystal at $k>k_c$ and a superfluid at $k<k_c$, with a critical value $k_c\simeq2\text{--}5$. These results imply that an anyon gas with only statistical interactions is not necessarily a superfluid, contrary to common lore.   We also present a detailed analysis of the renormalization-group evolution of anyon--anyon interactions, identifying many new potential fixed points and elucidating finite-size effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates Gaussian-augmented Matrix Product States (GAMPS) to understand how adding free-fermionic structure enhances the entanglement capacity of tensor networks. Using a replica tensor-network method, the authors show that random GAMPS rapidly achieve volume-law entanglement, requiring exponentially smaller bond dimensions than standard MPS to reach the same level of quantum resource saturation. This demonstrates a significant expansion in the expressive power of these tensor network descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The enhanced entanglement scaling and approach to Haar-random limits suggest that incorporating fermionic structure (Gaussian unitaries) provides a powerful, resource-efficient way to model highly entangled quantum states relevant to strongly correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify how Gaussian augmentation expands the expressive power of Matrix Product States (MPS) by analyzing Gaussian-augmented Matrix Product States (GAMPS).

**Main result.** Random GAMPS exhibit nearly maximal volume-law entanglement entropy even at modest bond dimension, suggesting an exponential reduction in bond dimension needed compared to unaugmented MPS.

**Method.** The authors developed a replica tensor-network method to compute ensemble averages over random GAMPS, utilizing a two-copy density operator approach.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by random GAMPS, defined as $|\Psi_Gangle = U_G |\Phi_\chiangle$, where $U_G$ is a fermionic Gaussian unitary and $|\Phi_\chiangle$ is a random MPS.

**Key observables.** Annealed Rényi-2 entanglement entropy density, Subsystem purity, second-order inverse participation ratio ($I_2$), and state two-design distance ($D_H$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), system size ($N$), and the crossover bond dimension ($\chi_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to compute averages using the quadratic dependence of the quantifiers on the density matrix $ho_G$, and relies on the structure of the fermionic commutant for the Gaussian average.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the GAMPS construction, the contraction method for calculating averages, and show the Page curve demonstrating near-Haar entanglement at modest bond dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the replica tensor-network framework to calculate ensemble averages, analyzes the scaling of entanglement and resource quantifiers across three regimes based on bond dimension, and confirms the enhanced expressive power of GAMPS.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong entanglement limits the reach of tensor-network descriptions of quantum many-body systems. We study Gaussian-augmented matrix product states (GAMPS), formed by applying a free-fermionic (Gaussian) unitary to a matrix product state. To explore the capabilities of this family, we consider random GAMPS}, a minimally structured ensemble obtained by independently sampling random matrix product states and Gaussian unitaries. Exploiting the algebraic structure of Gaussian unitaries, we develop a replica tensor-network method that computes averages over the random GAMPS ensemble for hundreds of qubits. Random GAMPS exhibit nearly maximal volume-law entanglement already at modest bond dimension, while other quantum resource quantifiers rapidly approach Haar-random state values as the bond dimension increases. The entanglement scaling suggests an exponential reduction in the bond dimension needed to approach the volume-law entropy compared with unaugmented matrix product states. We find a state two-design behavior of random GAMPS at fixed accuracy with a bond dimension independent of system size. Our results reveal how Gaussian augmentation expands the expressive power of tensor networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives a universal fidelity law for linear-optical entangling gates that accounts for noise from imperfect photon sources. By analyzing contributions from single, pair, and three-photon emissions, the authors show that the gate fidelity can be predicted using only measurable quantities like HOM visibility and second-order coherence. This provides a unified benchmark for advancing photonic quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, platform-agnostic benchmark for characterizing and optimizing photonic quantum computing hardware, directly linking fundamental source statistics to gate performance.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The performance of interference-based linear-optical entangling gates is degraded by imperfect photon sources, necessitating a universal fidelity law.

**Main result.** The authors derive a universal fidelity law for these gates expressed solely in terms of measurable source metrics (HOM visibility and second-order coherence), independent of the specific physical platform.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the two-qubit density matrix using coincidence operators and analyzing contributions from single, pair, and three-photon emission events.

**Model / system.** The system is an interference-based CNOT gate implemented using linear-optical elements and single-photon sources (e.g., SPDC). The analysis models the gate operation using creation operators incorporating temporal wavepackets.

**Key observables.** Gate Fidelity (F), Hong-Ou-Mandel (HOM) visibility (V), and second-order coherence ($g^{(2)}(0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon indistinguishability ($\eta$), signal-noise overlap parameter ($M_{sn}$), and emission-event weights ($w_x, r_x$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial derivation uses an approximation valid for at most two photons, though the framework extends to three-photon events. The universal law relies on expressing fidelity via measured quantities to absorb platform-specific noise origins.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the CNOT gate components and defines parameters like $\eta$ and $g^{(2)}$. Other figures/tables detail the coincidence matrices ($\sigma$) for different noise types and the derived visibility relations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the source of error (imperfect sources) to deriving the general fidelity expression by analyzing multi-photon contributions, culminating in the universal law that relates fidelity to measurable source parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Imperfect photon sources degrade the performance of interference-based linear-optical entangling gates. We derive a universal fidelity law for these gates in terms of two standard source metrics: Hong-Ou-Mandel visibility and second-order coherence. We model the multiphoton component as an arbitrary overlap between noise and signal photons. While different values of the signal-noise overlap produce different error structures, associated with different physical platforms, all result in the same fidelity law. We test the model on our actively synchronized CNOT-gate experiment and against published results across different platforms, with no free parameters. This framework turns source characterization into a unified benchmark for photonic quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes the Quantum Phase-based Comparator (QPC) to solve the quantum comparison problem by encoding inputs into relative phases rather than bit strings. It uses a phase interferometer structure, yielding a statistical readout that requires a number of shots scaling inversely with the separation. This method is advantageous for its ability to handle superpositions of pairs and its low depth scaling.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core mechanism is quantum circuit design, the reliance on phase estimation and interference patterns connects to quantum optics and measurement theory, which are relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental quantum computational task of determining the order ($x < y$) of two operands.

**Main result.** It introduces the Quantum Phase-based Comparator (QPC), which achieves comparison by analyzing the interference pattern of phases rather than basis states.

**Method.** The QPC utilizes a phase interferometer structure, where the probability of measuring zero is used to infer the relative order of the input phases.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum circuit implementing a phase interferometer on a single ancillary qubit, which is driven by two input phases ($\varphi_x$ and $-\varphi_y$). It can be implemented for hard-coded values or via a register-driven cascade.

**Key observables.** The probability of measuring $|0angle$, $P(|0angle)$, which serves as a statistical readout (a biased coin) indicating the order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required number of shots ($N$) scales inversely with the square of the phase separation ($\propto 1/\sin^2 d$), and the depth scales logarithmically with the inverse separation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The output is statistical, requiring multiple shots for a reliable decision, and the comparison is only guaranteed for phase separations within a certain margin.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the circuit for hard-coded value comparison, while Figure 2 shows the register-driven cascade implementation allowing for superposition inputs.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of quantum comparison, then details the physical model using the phase interferometer (Theorem 1), presents two implementations (hard-coded vs. cascade), and finally analyzes the statistical readout cost and scaling properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum comparators decide the order of two operands. They rely on reversible logic acting on basis-encoded integers, so their width grows with the precision. We introduce the Quantum Phase-based Comparator (QPC), which compares two values carried in relative phases instead. The circuit places the two phases, entered with opposite signs, between a pair of Hadamard gates, and a fixed offset centers the interference, so that the probability of measuring zero falls below one half exactly when the first phase is the smaller. We give two implementations. With hard-coded values, the two values are written into the parameters of two phase gates, and the comparison costs one qubit and five gates. With the register-driven cascade, the values are drawn from two $t$-qubit registers through binary-weighted controlled-phase gates, at a cost of one qubit beyond the registers and depth linear in $t$; since the cascade induces each phase linearly from the register content, one circuit handles a superposition of operand pairs. The readout yields a biased coin rather than a definite bit, and we quantify the shots that a decision takes at a given phase separation. Scaling both phases by an integer widens the decision margin at no cost in width or depth, and an adaptive doubling schedule turns this amplification into a shot count logarithmic in the inverse separation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to significantly boost the sensitivity of atomic magnetometers by utilizing a specialized quantum state called the 'pinched spin state.' Theoretically, this state offers an $(F+1)$-fold improvement in magnetic-field response over conventional states. Experimentally, the authors successfully implemented this in a compact, room-temperature magnetometer, achieving state-of-the-art sensitivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental limits in quantum sensing by proposing a specific quantum state manipulation (pinched state) to surpass standard noise limitations, which is a core topic in quantum optics and AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the sensitivity of atomic spin sensors used for magnetic field measurement.

**Main result.** The use of a 'pinched spin state' ($\left|F, 0ightangle$) theoretically provides an $(F+1)$-fold enhancement in magnetic-field response compared to the standard stretched state, which was experimentally demonstrated for $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ with a 2.7-fold enhancement.

**Method.** The authors used theoretical modeling based on the density matrix master equation and performed experiments using a single-beam, dual-axis, zero-field RF magnetometer setup.

**Model / system.** The system is an atomic spin sensor using $^{87}	ext{Rb}$ atoms, modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating Zeeman splitting, a tensor light shift ($\propto F_z^2$), and a transverse RF drive. The dynamics are governed by the density matrix master equation.

**Key observables.** RF-field response ($\langle F_x angle$), magnetic-field sensitivity ($	ext{fT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$), and the ratio of field responses between the pinched and stretched states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters include the spin quantum number $F$, the tensor light shift $\Omega_{TS}$, the relaxation rate $\Gamma$, and the operating frequency range ($3-100 	ext{ Hz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ideal enhancement relies on engineering an RF spectrum symmetric about $m=0$, and the analysis often uses the weak-drive approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the experimental setup for both pinched and stretched state magnetometry. Figure 2 compares the RF-field response, showing the enhancement factor. Other figures detail the frequency dependence and noise spectra.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical advantage of the pinched state via QFI. It then derives the steady-state response equations for both states. The latter part details the experimental implementation in a compact cell, measuring the field response and noise floor.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The sensitivity of an atomic spin sensor is fundamentally constrained by the quantum Fisher information (QFI) of the probe state. The pinched state, $|{F,0}\rangle$, has an $(F+1)$-fold larger single-particle QFI for transverse-field sensing than the stretched state, $|{F,F}\rangle$, corresponding to a predicted $\sqrt{F+1}$ reduction in the spin-projection-noise-limited field uncertainty. We show that this advantage can be accessed in an rf sensing scheme by engineering a spectrum symmetric about $m=0$, yielding an ideal $(F+1)$-fold enhancement of magnetic-field response. For $^{87}\mathrm{Rb}$ with $F=2$, we observe up to 2.7-fold response enhancement. Implementing this approach in a compact $0.8~\mathrm{cm}^3$ anti-relaxation-coated multipass cell, we realize a single-beam, dual-axis, zero-field magnetometer, achieving $13$--$23$~$\mathrm{fT}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ over $3$--$100$~Hz at room temperature.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a novel spectroscopic interrogation protocol designed to suppress systematic frequency shifts caused by residual atomic motion in optical ion clocks. By engineering the interrogation sequence, the authors achieved a substantial reduction in the motion-induced uncertainty budget for a $	ext{Yb}^+$ clock. This improved precision allows for setting tighter limits on fundamental physical constants like the fine-structure constant.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant advancement in precision measurement techniques for quantum systems, directly addressing systematic noise sources (motion) in trapped-ion clocks using advanced quantum control sequences.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Residual motion-induced frequency shifts significantly limit the uncertainty budgets of state-of-the-art optical ion clocks.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally reduced the motion-induced systematic uncertainty contribution from $1.3	imes10^{-18}$ to $0.3	imes10^{-18}$ using a novel interrogation protocol.

**Method.** They introduced a spectroscopic interrogation protocol, based on a Ramsey-style sequence with detuned pulses, to achieve first-order auto-suppression of motion-induced shifts.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses a single laser-cooled ytterbium ion ($	ext{Yb}^+$) confined in an endcap radio-frequency Paul trap, probing the 467 nm electric octupole (E3) transition.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the residual clock frequency shift ($\Delta
u_{	ext{ENGS}}$) and the measured E3/E2 frequency ratio.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The uncertainty reduction factor is key, moving from $1.3	imes10^{-18}$ to $0.3	imes10^{-18}$, and the resulting limit on the fine-structure constant variation is $2.4(2.7) 	imes 10^{-19}/$yr.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The auto-cancellation scheme only provides first-order cancellation and does not correct for imperfect micromotion compensation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates Ramsey interrogation sequences, while Figure 2 shows calculated residual clock frequency shifts versus the final motional state ($ar{n}_{	ext{final}}$) for various pulse detunings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem of motion-induced shifts, then details the theoretical basis for the auto-suppression protocol using pulse engineering, presents the experimental implementation on $	ext{Yb}^+$, and concludes with the measured uncertainty reduction and fundamental constant limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spectroscopic frequency shifts due to residual motion of the probed atoms significantly contribute to the uncertainty budgets of state-of-the-art optical ion clocks. For clock transitions with second-order Doppler and quadratic Stark shifts of opposite signs, it is possible to configure electrodynamic ion confinement such that these two shift effects become anticorrelated causing zero net shift. Here, we introduce a spectroscopic interrogation protocol which leads, for systems with unknown and varying motional energy gain rates, to first-order auto-suppression of corresponding frequency shifts without requiring the opposite-sign configuration. We experimentally demonstrate the proposed method on a new ytterbium ion optical clock probing the 467 nm electric octupole (E3) transition, where the combined fractional uncertainty contribution from motion-induced frequency shifts is reduced from $1.3\times10^{-18}$ to $0.3\times10^{-18}$. An interleaved optical frequency ratio measurement against ytterbium's electric quadrupole transition (E2) at 435 nm delivers an E3/E2 frequency ratio of $0.932 \,829 \,404 \,530 \,965 \, 340 (39)$. Combined with previously published ratio data this leads to a limit for a potential fractional temporal variation of the fine-structure constant of $2.4 (2.7) \times 10^{-19}/$yr, in agreement with existing bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that transient spin-orbit coupling (SOC) can act as a catalyst to enable magnetization transfer in the Fermi-Hubbard model, overcoming limitations imposed by symmetry conservation. By applying quantum optimal control techniques like GRAPE, the researchers successfully accelerate this otherwise slow process in both 1D and 2D lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of SOC as a tunable catalyst to bypass symmetry-protected dynamical bottlenecks is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses overcoming symmetry-imposed dynamical constraints that prevent the transfer of magnetization from an antiferromagnetically correlated state to a polarized ferromagnetic sector during a Zeeman ramp.

**Main result.** Transient spin-orbit coupling (SOC) acts as a controllable catalyst, opening finite-gap pathways for this magnetization-sector transfer, which can be rapidly accelerated using quantum optimal control techniques.

**Method.** The research employs quantum optimal control methods, specifically Gradient-Ascent Pulse Engineering (GRAPE) and Variational Quantum Circuits (VQC), to design time-dependent Hamiltonian controls.

**Model / system.** The system is the spinful Fermi-Hubbard (FH) model, studied in 1D and 2D lattices at half-filling. The total Hamiltonian includes hopping, interaction, a Zeeman term, and a time-dependent SOC term.

**Key observables.** Total magnetization ($\hat{M}$), final connected spin-spin correlations ($S(i, j)$), and the time required for magnetization transfer ($\mu(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The SOC coupling ($\lambda(t)$) is the key control parameter, alongside the Zeeman ramp $f(t)$. Optimal control significantly reduces the transfer time from adiabatic timescales to much shorter, engineered timescales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SOC control is treated as a normalized pulse envelope inherited from the Zeeman ramp, and the dynamics are analyzed using both time-evolution simulation and mapping to quantum circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the control architecture, the sector-transfer mechanism showing gap closing (no SOC) versus avoided crossing (with SOC), and the resulting magnetization dynamics and final spin correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the symmetry constraint in the FH model. It then introduces SOC as a catalyst. The core methodology involves applying quantum optimal control (GRAPE/VQC) to accelerate the transfer, demonstrating success in both 1D and 2D lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate spin--orbit-coupling (SOC) catalysis of magnetization-sector transfer in the half-filled Fermi--Hubbard model. Without SOC, conservation of total magnetization confines the dynamics to the initial sector, preventing an antiferromagnetically correlated state from reaching the polarized ferromagnetic sector under a Zeeman ramp. A transient SOC control lifts this obstruction by coupling otherwise disconnected magnetization sectors and converting symmetry-protected crossings into avoided crossings, thereby opening a finite-gap pathway for magnetic-state conversion. We further accelerate the SOC-enabled dynamics beyond the adiabatic timescale using gradient-ascent pulse engineering (GRAPE) and a variational quantum circuit. Both approaches achieve rapid magnetization transfer in one- and two-dimensional Fermi--Hubbard lattices. Our results establish transient SOC as a controllable catalyst for overcoming symmetry-imposed dynamical constraints, while optimal control sets the timescale for the resulting magnetization-sector transfer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel method to exactly represent the Hamiltonian of tight-binding models on complex lattices (like fractals and hyperbolic spaces) as a Matrix Product Operator (MPO). By encoding the lattice geometry using a finite-state automaton, the method ensures the computational cost scales only logarithmically with system size. This allows for large-scale, analytical calculations of electronic properties across a vast range of structured materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general computational framework for simulating electronic structure on geometrically complex systems that are difficult for standard methods, bridging geometry and tensor network efficiency.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a systematic, exact, and analytical method to represent the Matrix Product Operator (MPO) for single-particle Hamiltonians on complex, recursively structured lattices.

**Main result.** They achieve this by encoding the lattice geometry within a finite-state automaton, resulting in an MPO whose bond dimension grows only logarithmically with system size.

**Method.** The core method combines finite-state automata to encode lattice structure with tensor network techniques (MPO) and utilizes the Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM) for spectral calculations.

**Model / system.** The model is the tight-binding Hamiltonian, applied to a broad class of lattices including regular Bravais lattices, fractals (e.g., Sierpinski Carpet), quasiperiodic chains (e.g., Fibonacci), Cayley trees, and hyperbolic lattices.

**Key observables.** Spectral properties, Density of States (DOS), and Local Density of States (LDOS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bond dimension ($\chi$), which is determined by the number of automaton states and remains finite for these structured lattices.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework critically relies on the recursive structure of the lattices to ensure the automaton state space, and thus the bond dimension, remains finite and manageable.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 illustrates the evolution of the MPO bond dimensions versus the number of iterations for various lattice types, showing differences in growth rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured to first introduce the general formalism (Section II), followed by detailed constructions for specific lattice types: regular, fractal, quasiperiodic, and hyperbolic lattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Inspired by the recent progress in the simulation of tight-binding Hamiltonians on large lattices using tensor networks, we introduce a systematic matrix product operator (MPO) construction for single-particle Hamiltonians on recursively structured lattices. Taking advantage of this recursive structure, we encode the lattice geometry in a finite-state automaton and, adapting ideas from Abelian-symmetric tensor networks, obtain an exact and analytical MPO representation of the Hamiltonian, where the number of tensors grows logarithmically with the system size and the bond dimension is set by the number of automaton states. Several examples demonstrate the generality of the framework: regular lattices, fractals, Cayley trees, hyperbolic lattices, and one- and two-dimensional Fibonacci quasicrystals. Combined with the kernel polynomial method, these MPO representations enable large-scale calculations of spectral properties without explicitly constructing the full Hamiltonian. This provides a unified route to the exploration of electronic properties across a broad class of lattices at exponentially large system sizes.

</details>

### [Hamiltonian Learning and Certification via Eigenphase Engineering](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26596v1)

**Authors:** Myeongjin Shin, Yu Tong  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26596v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">

<img src="2609.26596_figures/2609.26596_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: The quantum circuit of the energy gap estimation subroutine.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'eigenphase engineering,' a novel technique for learning unknown quantum Hamiltonians from time evolution data. It achieves state-of-the-art, Heisenberg-limited scaling for recovering Hamiltonian coefficients while maintaining a large, near-optimal time step size. This method is highly significant for quantum simulation as it allows for efficient characterization of complex quantum dynamics with minimal experimental overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, resource-efficient protocol for characterizing the underlying physics (Hamiltonian) of a quantum system using only time evolution measurements, which is crucial for quantum simulation and control.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of Hamiltonian learning: recovering the unknown Hamiltonian coefficients of a quantum system from limited measurements of its time evolution.

**Main result.** The proposed 'eigenphase engineering' technique achieves Heisenberg-limited learning scaling while allowing for a near-maximal time step size, significantly improving efficiency over previous methods.

**Method.** The method encodes energy expectation differences into the perturbative response of eigenphases, which are then extracted using phase estimation and high-order extrapolation.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum many-body system governed by a $k$-local, $s$-sparse Hamiltonian $H$, which is learned by observing the forward time evolution $U(t) = e^{-iHt}$ at fixed, discrete time steps.

**Key observables.** Energy expectation differences ($\Delta H(x, y)$), Pauli coefficients ($\lambda_a$), and the fidelity of the recovered Hamiltonian ($\lVert \hat{oldsymbol{\lambda}} - oldsymbol{\lambda} Vert_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sparsity ($s$), locality ($k$), operator norm bound ($\Lambda$), and desired estimation error ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Hamiltonian is assumed to be $k$-local and $s$-sparse. The analysis relies on the fixed-grid model using only forward time evolution at integer multiples of a fixed time interval $	au$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the technique by first establishing the core measurement principle (eigenphase engineering) and then applying it to solve the Hamiltonian learning problem, culminating in complexity bounds for coefficient recovery and certification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study Hamiltonian learning for $k$-local Hamiltonians at large step size, using only forward time evolution at integer multiples of a fixed time interval. For this task, our new technique, which we call eigenphase engineering, achieves Heisenberg-limited learning with a step size that is optimal up to logarithmic factors. For an unknown $s$-sparse Hamiltonian with fixed locality $k$ and $\|H\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leqΛ$, our algorithm recovers its Pauli coefficients to $\ell_2$ error $\varepsilon$ in total evolution time $\widetilde{O}(s/\varepsilon)$, matching the best known scaling while allowing a near-maximal step size $\widetildeΘ(1/Λ)$. As an independent contribution, we strengthen the evolution-time lower bound to $Ω_k(s^{1-1/(2k)}/\varepsilon)$, placing our algorithm within a factor $\widetilde{O}(s^{1/(2k)})$ of being optimal. Eigenphase engineering departs from approaches based on dynamical approximation or iteratively canceling the unknown Hamiltonian. It encodes energy expectation differences in the perturbative response of eigenphases of the actual controlled evolution, then extracts them through phase estimation and high-order extrapolation. This avoids the need for polynomially shorter evolution steps as the target precision improves. A version using only single-qubit operations retains the same step size with a $\sqrt n$ overhead in $\ell_2$ learning time for $n$ qubits. The method also enables tolerant Hamiltonian certification and estimation of a specified Pauli coefficient, the latter without locality or sparsity assumptions. These results establish eigenphase engineering as a versatile approach to extracting Hamiltonian information with infrequent control.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Operator Score Matching, a novel, partition-function-free technique to determine the Hamiltonian of a quantum system from its thermal state measurements. By adapting classical score matching to the language of operator algebras, it provides a robust and scalable framework for quantum Hamiltonian learning. This is particularly valuable for studying strongly correlated electron models where exact calculations are intractable.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a powerful, computationally tractable alternative to traditional methods (like those relying on partition function calculations) for characterizing complex many-body Hamiltonians from limited experimental data.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is learning the microscopic Hamiltonian (H) that governs a quantum system solely from measurements of its low-temperature thermal state ($ho \propto e^{-H}$).

**Main result.** The authors introduce Operator Score Matching, a partition-function-free framework that efficiently recovers Hamiltonian parameters using simple gradient descent.

**Method.** They adapt classical score matching by developing a quantum analog using generalized derivatives and an integration-by-parts identity in operator algebras to construct a computable loss function.

**Model / system.** The method is tested on various models, including the transverse-field Ising model, XXZ spin chain, Fermi-Hubbard model, and lattice $\phi^4$ theory, applicable to both spin and fermionic systems.

**Key observables.** The operator score loss function, $L_{	ext{osm}}(	heta)$, which measures the difference between predicted and target operator scores.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The inverse temperature ($eta$) and the depth ($d$) of the nested commutator expansion used for approximation are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of direction operators $\{F_k\}$ that generate the full algebra for unique Hamiltonian determination, and convergence relies on establishing a local Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PL) inequality.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the fundamental problem of Hamiltonian learning, develops the Operator Score Matching loss function using algebraic identities, demonstrates its applicability across multiple physical models (Ising, Fermi-Hubbard), and concludes with convergence guarantees via the PL inequality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning quantum Hamiltonians from low-temperature thermal state measurements is a fundamental problem in quantum physics. Scalability of existing methods is limited by the complexity of semidefinite optimization problems or partition function computation. Here, we develop a quantum analog of classical score matching method that exploits generalized notions of derivatives and integration by parts in operator algebras. We introduce a new \emph{Operator Score Matching} loss function that recovers Hamiltonian parameters by a simple gradient descent without the need to compute intractable normalization constants. Numerical experiments on the Gibbs states of the transverse-field Ising model, XXZ spin chain, Fermi-Hubbard model, and lattice $φ^4$ theory demonstrate efficient parameter recovery using finite-depth truncations of nested commutator expansions. Our results establish Operator Score Matching as a practical, partition-function-free framework for quantum Hamiltonian learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes superconductivity in a Chern Dartboard Insulator coupled with strong electron-electron interactions (HK coupling). The authors demonstrate that these interactions can induce superconductivity even in insulating regimes and predict a unique re-entrant superconducting phase. The mechanism hinges on the non-monotonic temperature dependence of the pairing susceptibility, a signature of the combined effects of correlation and pairing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of re-entrance arising from the non-monotonic temperature dependence of the pairing susceptibility, driven by both correlation (HK) and pairing terms, offers insights into complex superconducting phase diagrams beyond simple BCS predictions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of re-entrant p-wave superconductivity in a Chern Dartboard Insulator (CDI) when strong electron-electron interactions are included.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a novel insulator-superconductor-insulator re-entrant transition, which is attributed to the confluence of Hund's coupling (HK) interactions and inter-orbital pairing terms.

**Method.** The authors employ an exact theoretical formulation using a 2-band generalization of the Hatsugai-Kohmoto model, calculating the pair susceptibility via Dyson equations and mean-field theory.

**Model / system.** The model is a 2-band superconductor built upon the Chern Dartboard Insulator (CDI) platform, incorporating both inter-orbital pairing and band Hatsugai-Kohmoto (HK) interactions.

**Key observables.** Pair susceptibility ($\chi$), superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), and the existence of re-entrant transitions in the phase diagram.

**Important parameters / regimes.** HK interaction strength ($U$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and pairing strength ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis utilizes a $p$-wave pairing structure and relies on a 2-band generalization of the HK model Green function.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the band structure changes due to HK interaction, phase diagrams showing superconducting regions, and plots of the pairing susceptibility ($\chi_0(T)$) demonstrating non-monotonic behavior leading to re-entrance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model Hamiltonian and calculating the bare pairing susceptibility ($\chi_0$). It then uses mean-field theory to find the gap self-consistency, culminating in the detailed analysis of $\chi_0(T)$ to pinpoint the re-entrant transition mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate an exact theory for a 2-band superconductor with inter-orbital pairing and band Hatsugai-Kohmoto (HK) interactions. We apply this theory to a Chern dartboard insulator and compute the pair susceptibility and pair correlations analytically. We find that in the insulating phase previously predicted by BCS theory (small pairing strength $g$, chemical potential $μ$ in the gap), the HK interaction can induce superconductivity, and possibly a Chern dartboard superconductor, by lifting the doubly-occupied band and making it cross the chemical potential. From this HK-induced superconductor, we notice that in a significant parameter range (HK interaction strength $U = 1$, $μ= 0.5$), an insulator-superconductor-insulator re-entrant transition exists. We isolate the re-entrance to a conflation between HK interactions and inter-orbital pairings.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel theoretical framework to calculate spectral properties in many-body physics directly on the real frequency axis, circumventing the numerical instability of analytic continuation. It achieves this by representing Green's functions using systematically improvable pole expansions and defining algebraic rules for complex diagrammatic operations. This provides a robust, controlled tool for advanced calculations in correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the rigorous mathematical framework for handling spectral functions via pole expansions is a powerful, general technique for any field requiring accurate spectral analysis, including those involving quantum optics or open systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is extracting reliable spectral properties from finite-temperature many-body theories, as standard methods relying on analytic continuation from imaginary time are ill-conditioned.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a practical, controlled, and systematically improvable framework to calculate spectral quantities directly on the real axis, bypassing the need for problematic analytic continuation.

**Method.** The approach represents Green's functions using systematically improvable pole and moment expansions, developing algebraic rules for diagrammatic operations directly on these pole data tuples.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to finite-temperature many-body systems, utilizing concepts from diagrammatic perturbation theory (like GW and GF2 self-energies) and the Keldysh formalism.

**Key observables.** Spectral function ($A_X(\omega)$), retarded/advanced Green's functions ($G_R, G_A$), and self-energies ($\Sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite temperature ($eta$), frequency ($\omega$), and the number of poles ($M_X$) used in the approximation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method approximates continuous spectral features (branch cuts) by shifting poles into the complex plane, and it relies on a controlled recompression procedure to manage pole proliferation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Keldysh contour, diagrammatic building blocks, and the analytic structure of the spectral function showing poles and branch cuts. A key figure compares the convergence of convolution integrals calculated via pole representation versus numerical quadrature.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of real-axis spectral calculation, introduces the pole/moment expansion framework, details the algebraic rules for diagrammatic operations (convolution, product), and benchmarks the method on impurity models and the uniform electron gas.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finite-temperature many-body theories are typically formulated in imaginary time, where they are amenable to efficient numerical treatment. However, the extraction of spectral properties from the imaginary axis is difficult and requires numerical analytic continuation, while a direct formulation on the real axis requires prohibitively expensive numerical quadrature. In this paper, we represent the objects of many-body perturbation theory using systematically improvable pole and moment expansions and develop the corresponding framework for evaluating diagrammatic contributions directly on the real axis. We introduce a controlled recompression procedure that prevents the proliferation of poles under algebraic and diagrammatic operations. We benchmark the approach on paradigmatic quantum impurity problems and apply it to self-consistent calculations of the uniform electron gas. The resulting spectral quantities converge systematically towards numerical accuracy. This work provides a practical framework for controlled and systematically improvable calculations of spectral quantities within diagrammatic many-body theories, including self-consistent conserving approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the synergy between Quantum Machine Learning and AI for quantum systems. It introduces Neural Quantum Embedding (NQE) to enhance quantum classification by improving class separation in the embedding space. Additionally, it applies advanced ML models like Mamba to improve the decoding efficiency of quantum error correction codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep integration of ML theory (generalization bounds, representation learning) with fundamental quantum information tasks (classification, error correction) provides a powerful new toolkit for analyzing quantum hardware limitations and potential.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the bidirectional intersection between quantum computing and artificial intelligence, developing quantum methods for machine learning and applying ML techniques to quantum systems.

**Main result.** The proposed Neural Quantum Embedding (NQE) framework improves quantum classification performance and provides tighter generalization bounds compared to conventional quantum feature maps. Furthermore, Mamba decoders show promise for improving error correction decoding efficiency.

**Method.** The work employs NQE, which trains quantum embeddings by optimizing an implicit fidelity loss, and applies advanced ML architectures like Mamba for quantum error correction decoding.

**Model / system.** The study utilizes parameterized quantum circuits (QNNs), Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes like Surface Codes, and various quantum feature maps (e.g., ZZ, XYZ). The work is demonstrated on platforms like NMR quantum processors.

**Key observables.** Trace distance between class ensembles, classification accuracy, effective model complexity (ef-dimension), and logical error rates (LER) in QEC.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise levels, code distance, learning rates (e.g., Adam LR 0.001), and the structure of the quantum Hamiltonian.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on generalization bounds derived from Rademacher complexity and assumes the applicability of specific noise models for QEC decoding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QNNs vs. Quantum Kernels, show NQE improving class separation (trace distance) on MNIST, and illustrate the Mamba decoder's performance relative to Transformers in QEC.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around developing NQE for quantum classification, analyzing its generalization bounds using complexity theory, applying it to various feature maps, and separately addressing ML for QEC decoding and state estimation via Stochastic Reconfiguration.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This thesis studies the intersection of quantum computing and artificial intelligence in two directions: quantum methods for machine learning and machine learning methods for quantum systems. For quantum machine learning, Neural Quantum Embedding learns data representations that increase the trace distance between embedded class ensembles, lowering an embedding-dependent bound on empirical risk and improving classification on noisy quantum hardware. A training objective based on the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product extends this approach to deterministic quantum computation with one qubit (DQC1) and is demonstrated on an NMR quantum processor. A margin-based generalization analysis then connects quantum neural network performance to quantum state discrimination. In the studied benchmarks, margin distributions predict generalization more reliably than parameter-count metrics. For quantum systems, a Mamba-based neural decoder for surface codes matches a reproduced Transformer baseline in memory experiments while reducing inference-cost scaling from quartic to quadratic in code distance. Under an explicit decoder-induced-noise model, it achieves lower logical error rates and a higher effective threshold. For neural quantum states, stochastic reconfiguration is interpreted as tangent-space ridge regression, with its diagonal shift controlling the bias-variance trade-off under finite Monte Carlo sampling. Multi-shift stochastic reconfiguration reduces checkpoint-local validation residuals and update variance relative to fixed-shift SR, at additional computational cost. Together, these contributions show how learned representations, statistical control, and hardware constraints shape the exchange between quantum computing and machine learning.

</details>

### [Beyond expressiveness in pairwise and higher-order models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26031v1)

**Authors:** Cyril Rommens, Pietro Traversa, Guilherme Ferraz de Arruda, Yamir Moreno  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26031v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper critiques the common debate pitting pairwise (graph) versus higher-order (hypergraph) models in complex systems. It argues that functional equivalence does not imply structural necessity, proposing instead an operational Minimum-Description-Length criterion. The work establishes that the choice of model must be guided by explicit assumptions and data, not just the ability to reproduce observed dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not explicitly quantum, the rigorous framework for distinguishing necessary structural complexity (hypergraphs) from merely emulated complexity (graphs) is highly relevant for modeling many-body quantum systems where interactions can be complex and non-pairwise.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental debate over whether complex system dynamics are best described by pairwise (graph) or higher-order (hypergraph) interactions.

**Main result.** The authors argue that 'expressiveness' alone is misleading; the choice of model depends on an operational Minimum-Description-Length criterion combining structural cost, rule cost, and model fit.

**Method.** The comparison is formalized using algorithmic information theory, treating the problem as a description-length minimization, and analyzing the invariance of interaction order.

**Model / system.** The models are general dynamical systems whose behavior is governed by interactions among components, represented either by pairwise graphs or higher-order hypergraphs.

**Key observables.** Interaction order (pairwise vs. higher-order), structural projection cost (edge list length), and model fit/prediction error.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model class constraints, regularity assumptions, and the specific data used for fitting.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the debate must be reframed away from mere expressive power toward an operational, information-theoretic criterion.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention schematic representations showing the transfer of grouping information from hypergraph structure to graph rules, and quantitative comparisons of structural size (e.g., edge list length).

**Paper structure.** The paper distinguishes four conflated notions (structural projection, functional representability, statistical identifiability, mechanistic adequacy) before recasting the comparison into a minimum-description-length framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The debate over pairwise and higher-order models is often cast as a contest of expressive power, but this framing is misleading. A graph with arbitrary multivariate node functions can emulate the node-level dynamics of many hypergraph models, yet this does not erase the grouping information encoded by the hypergraph: it may simply be shifted from structure to dynamics. We distinguish four notions that are often conflated: structural projection, functional representability, statistical identifiability, and mechanistic adequacy. We show that the interaction order of a finite-state map is an invariant of the map itself, so an exact change of representation cannot reduce the underlying order of dependence. We then recast the comparison in terms of description length. At the unrestricted algorithmic level, a fixed compiler can move information between structure and rule with only constant overhead, so expressiveness alone does not privilege graphs or hypergraphs. Preferences arise only relative to explicit model classes, coding schemes, regularity assumptions, and data. This motivates an operational minimum-description-length criterion combining structural cost, rule cost conditional on structure, and model fit. For $M$ disjoint groups of size $k$, we show that the clique projection requires an edge list asymptotically $k-1$ times longer than the corresponding hyperedge list, making projection a more expensive encoding of the same grouping. Examples spanning diffusion, Boolean dynamics, ecological interactions, and ambiguous projections illustrate graph-preferred, hypergraph-preferred, and unresolved cases. The resulting position is symmetric: higher-order structure should not be inferred from phenomenology alone, but neither should graph-based emulation be taken as evidence that a graph is the most parsimonious or scientifically adequate description.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes the fundamental limits for simultaneous quantum parameter estimation in magnetometry by analyzing three distinct physical regimes: unitary evolution, thermal equilibrium, and ground states. It derives tight lower bounds for the estimation error in these settings and identifies optimal control Hamiltonians that achieve these theoretical limits. This work advances the theory of quantum sensing by providing concrete, achievable benchmarks for quantum sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides rigorous, quantitative bounds on measurement precision in complex quantum systems, which is crucial for designing high-precision quantum sensors relevant to AMO physics and quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental limits for simultaneously estimating multiple unknown parameters in quantum systems, generalizing established single-parameter metrology bounds.

**Main result.** Tight lower bounds for the Mean Square Error Matrix (MSEM) are derived for vector magnetometry across three regimes: unitary dynamics, thermal equilibrium, and ground states, and optimal control Hamiltonians are identified to saturate these bounds.

**Method.** The analysis relies on generalizing the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM) for multi-parameter estimation in three distinct physical settings (unitary, thermal, ground state).

**Model / system.** The system involves a quantum probe whose Hamiltonian is parameterized as $H_{\vec{	heta}} = H_{P\vec{	heta}} + H^C$. The analysis is specifically applied to vector magnetometry using angular momentum operators.

**Key observables.** Mean Square Error Matrix (MSEM), Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), and the weighted mean square error (WMSE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parameters (2 or 3), the temperature/inverse temperature ($eta$), the time ($t$), and the spectral gap ($\Delta$) characterize the different metrological regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumptions include the diagonal nature of the weighting matrix $W$ and the use of specific approximations (e.g., large $M$ limit for thermal states).

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically generalizes metrological bounds for the three paradigms (dynamical, thermal, ground state) before focusing on deriving and comparing tight bounds for vector magnetometry in each regime.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the simultaneous estimation of multiple parameters in Hamiltonians of the form $H_{\vecθ}= H_{\vecθ}^P + H^C$, where $H_{\vecθ}^P$ encodes the unknown parameters and $H^C$ is a control term. We consider three basic paradigmatic frameworks for metrology: (i) dynamical, where the parameters are encoded unitarily, (ii) thermal, and (iii) ground state. We generalize previous bounds for single-parameter estimation to simultaneous multiparameter estimation for all three metrological settings and establish the conditions required for their saturability. We then focus on vector magnetometry, deriving tight bounds for the simultaneous estimation of magnetic-field components and identifying optimal many-body control Hamiltonians $H^C$ that saturate them. These results establish the fundamental limits of vector magnetometry for unitary dynamics, thermal equilibrium states, and ground states.

</details>

### [Hyperbolic Restricted Boltzmann Machine Neural Quantum State](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26032v1)

**Authors:** H. L. Dao  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26032v1>  
**Analysis basis:** abstract only; PDF text extraction failed or PDF unavailable
**Topic relevance:** `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work introduces the hyperbolic Restricted Boltzmann Machine (HRBM) as a novel neural quantum state (NQS) ansatz for simulating quantum systems. By testing it on the QSK model, the authors show that the hyperbolic geometry allows the NQS to much more accurately capture the volume-law entanglement spectrum than conventional Euclidean NQS. This suggests that hyperbolic structures are inherently better suited for modeling such highly entangled quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, mathematically motivated improvement to the ansatz structure used in variational quantum simulations, suggesting that geometric considerations (hyperbolic vs. Euclidean) can dictate the representational power for specific physical entanglement regimes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of representing quantum states, particularly those exhibiting volume-law entanglement, using neural quantum state (NQS) ansatzes.

**Main result.** The hyperbolic Restricted Boltzmann Machine (HRBM) NQS significantly outperforms its Euclidean counterpart (RBM NQS) in optimizing ground state energy and accurately reconstructing the entanglement spectrum of the Quantum Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (QSK) model.

**Method.** The authors construct and compare two types of non-autoregressive NQS: HRBM (hyperbolic) and RBM (Euclidean), using the Variational Monte-Carlo (VMC) method.

**Model / system.** The study is applied to the Quantum Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (QSK) model, whose ground state is known to exhibit volume-law entanglement. The comparison is made across increasing system sizes ($N=14$ to $N=24$).

**Key observables.** Ground state energy, Renyi-2 ($S_2$) and von Neumann ($S_{vN}$) absolute entanglement entropy reconstruction errors, and the fidelity of reproducing the entire entanglement spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N$), Hilbert space dimension (increasing by 512-fold), and the comparison between hyperbolic and Euclidean geometries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the hyperbolic geometry underlying HRBM provides a more natural representation for volume-law entangled quantum systems compared to Euclidean geometries.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the HRBM as a novel non-Euclidean NQS ansatz for the QSK model. It then compares its performance against the standard RBM NQS across varying system sizes, demonstrating superior performance in energy optimization and entanglement spectrum reconstruction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct the first type of non-Euclidean non-autoregressive neural quantum state (NQS) in the form of the hyperbolic Restricted Boltzmann Machine (HRBM), which is studied in the variational Monte-Carlo (VMC) setting of the Quantum Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (QSK) model whose ground state exhibits volume-law entanglement. Across a 512-fold increase in the Hilbert space dimension corresponding to a system size increase from $N=14$ to $N=24$, HRBM NQS robustly outperforms its Euclidean version, the RBM NQS, in terms of better ground state energy optimization as well as lower Renyi-2 $S_2$ and von Neumann $S_{vN}$ absolute entanglement entropy reconstruction errors. More importantly, for all QSK system sizes, HRBM NQS demonstrates a superior expressivity in faithfully reproducing the entire entanglement spectrum of the QSK model from the top eigenvalues down to the tail end across 15 orders of magnitude, while RBM NQS consistently overestimates the sub-dominant modes. This work furnishes a proof-of-concept demonstrating that hyperbolic non-autoregressive NQS ansatzë, thanks to the exponential volume of the hyperbolic geometry underlying their constructions, might be more natural at representing volume-law quantum systems than conventional Euclidean NQS. Furthermore, an interesting byproduct of this work is the polynomial scaling result of RBM-type NQS ansatzë in the QSK volume-law system as the Hilbert space increases exponentially.

</details>

**Summary.** This work rigorously examines the quantum-classical correspondence by analyzing the Heisenberg equations of motion for various quantum systems, including fields and particles, in curved spacetime. It demonstrates that the quantum evolution equations naturally reproduce the corresponding classical field equations and geodesic motion at the operator level. This establishes a powerful, systematic framework for understanding semiclassical physics in curved backgrounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on QFT and gravity, the rigorous demonstration of operator-level correspondence provides a powerful, systematic tool for analyzing quantum dynamics in complex backgrounds, which is foundational for understanding quantum gravity effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the extent to which the quantum-classical correspondence, established for non-relativistic particles, holds true when extended to quantum field theory and curved spacetime.

**Main result.** The Heisenberg equations of motion for quantum fields and particles reproduce the corresponding classical field equations (like Maxwell's or Einstein's) and geodesic motion at the operator level, even without taking the $\hbar 	o 0$ limit for the core dynamics.

**Method.** The primary method involves using the Heisenberg picture formalism to derive equations of motion directly from the quantum operators, comparing these results systematically against known classical equations.

**Model / system.** The analysis covers several systems: free and interacting scalar and Dirac fields in flat and curved spacetime, electromagnetic fields, and the dynamics of a quantum test particle in a curved background.

**Key observables.** Heisenberg equations of motion for field operators ($\hat{\phi}, \hat{\psi}$), metric perturbations ($\hat{h}_{\mu
u}$), and the position/momentum operators ($\hat{x}_\mu, \hat{p}_\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Curvature-coupling parameter ($\xi$), Planck's constant ($\hbar$), and the background metric tensor ($g_{\mu
u}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Heisenberg picture formalism and employs the semiclassical approximation when linking quantum matter fields to the classical background metric in gravity.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds systematically: first establishing the correspondence for non-relativistic particles, then extending it to free and interacting quantum fields in flat spacetime (QFT), and finally generalizing the framework to curved spacetime for fields and test particles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a recent work \cite{Shaikh:2026nsi}, it was shown that for a non-relativistic particle, the operator equations of motion can be cast in the same differential form as Newton's equation of motion. This establishes a correspondence between quantum operator dynamics and classical mechanics at the level of the equations of motion. In this work, we investigate whether this correspondence extends to quantum field theory and linearized gravity. We explicitly show that the Heisenberg equations of motion for quantum field operators reproduce the corresponding classical equations of motion for a broad class of field theories. The Klein-Gordon equation, Maxwell's equations, Yang-Mills equations, and linearized Einstein equations are shown to arise at the operator level without taking the limit $\hbar\rightarrow0$. We then extend the analysis to curved spacetime and show that the quantum field operators satisfy the corresponding covariant field equations at the operator level. In the weak-field approximation, we show that the Heisenberg evolution of the metric-perturbations reproduces the linearized Einstein equations at the operator level. For a quantum particle in curved spacetime, the Heisenberg equations for position and momentum reproduce classical geodesic motion in the semiclassical limit, thereby extending the operator-level quantum-classical correspondence from fields to particle dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the performance of photonic CZ gates realized through two-photon scattering from arrays of coupled emitter-cavity systems. By deriving scattering matrices using advanced quantum optics formalisms, the authors quantify the gate fidelity. They conclude that optimizing the required two-photon interaction leads to a fundamental trade-off, limiting the average gate fidelity to about 60%.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles a core physical challenge in quantum computing—generating controlled non-linear interactions using solid-state emitters—providing quantitative fidelity estimates for a promising platform.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of implementing a deterministic two-photon Controlled-Z (CZ) gate in photonic quantum computing, which requires non-linear photon-photon interactions.

**Main result.** The trade-off between optimizing the single-photon and two-photon wave packet shapes limits the average CZ gate fidelity to approximately 60% when averaged over all input states.

**Method.** The authors derive single- and two-photon scattering matrices using the input-output formalism for single cavities and the SLH formalism for multiple coupled cavities.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes photonic CZ gates based on few-photon scattering from N waveguide-coupled cavities containing two-level emitters, analogous to the KLM scheme.

**Key observables.** CZ gate fidelity ($F_{CZ}$) and average CZ gate fidelity ($ar{F}_{CZ}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling rates, cavity decay rates ($\Gamma$), and the number of cavities ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a simple passive emitter-cavity system without active control over emitter or cavity parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the CZ gate in the KLM scheme and its proposed implementation using N waveguide-coupled cavities, detailing coupling rates and decay parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical setup and the required gate operation. It then derives the necessary scattering matrices using quantum optics formalisms (input-output and SLH) to calculate the gate fidelity based on input wave packet optimization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we analyze the performance of photonic CZ gates based on N waveguide-coupled cavities containing two-level emitters. We derive single- and two-photon scattering matrices for one cavity using the input-output formalism of quantum optics and for N>1 cavities using the SLH formalism, allowing us to compute the CZ gate fidelity. We consider two input Gaussian wave packets that are centered on a two-photon transition of the emitter-cavity system, and find a trade-off between optimizing the single-photon and two-photon wave packet shape. While the single-photon wave packet is preserved for large emitter-cavity coupling rates, where there is little overlap with the single-photon transitions, the two-photon wave packet acquires the necessary $π$ phase shift for smaller coupling rates, comparable to the cavity decay rate multiplied by the number of cavities. We find that this trade-off limits the CZ gate fidelity to approximately 60% when averaging over all input states.

</details>
