**Summary.** This work develops a Berry-Landau Fermi-liquid theory to incorporate quantum geometry effects into the description of interacting metals. It derives that the anomalous Hall conductivity is determined by the Berry curvature integral, while the total charge current and Drude weight are modified by quantum-geometric contributions. This establishes quasiparticle occupation, Landau interactions, and quantum geometry as the fundamental low-energy ingredients.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit derivation of quantum-geometric corrections to transport coefficients, such as the anomalous Hall effect, provides a rigorous theoretical framework applicable to topological materials and strongly correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of incorporating non-trivial quantum geometry, encoded by Berry phases and the quantum metric, into the established framework of Landau Fermi-liquid theory to describe transport in interacting metals.

**Main result.** The intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity is fixed by the Berry-curvature integral of the dressed quasiparticle band, and the Drude weight receives both conventional and quantum-geometric contributions.

**Method.** The authors formulate a Berry-Landau Fermi-liquid theory, adapting the Nozières--Luttinger construction and using path integral methods to analyze the system's response to external perturbations.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to spinless fermions within an isolated band crossing the Fermi surface, treating the system as an interacting metal described by a generalized Landau functional.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{AHE}$), Drude weight ($D_{xx}$), and the interaction-induced quantum-geometric current ($\gamma_{int, \omega}(k)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi temperature ($T_F$), interaction strength ($g$), and the presence of Galilean symmetry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an isolated active band crossing the Fermi energy and utilizes approximations like the Hartree-Fock treatment for interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures numerically compare the AHE, Lorenz ratio, and Drude weight across different regimes (flat, narrow, dispersive), showing the distinct contributions of the quantum geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the BL-FL functional by incorporating quantum geometry effects into the Landau functional, derives the physical currents via Ward identities, and calculates key transport coefficients like $\sigma_{AHE}$ and $D_{xx}$ in various limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Landau Fermi-liquid theory characterizes interacting metals through quasiparticles and their residual interactions. It is a challenge to incorporate non-trivial quantum geometry -- as encoded by Berry phases and the quantum metric -- as a fundamental ingredient. We formulate a Berry-Landau Fermi-liquid theory for spinless fermions within an isolated band crossing the Fermi surface and derive the Landau functional through the Nozières--Luttinger construction. The instantaneous response to a perturbation of the Bloch waves generates both an anomalous Berry-connection potential in the action and an interaction-induced quantum-geometric contribution to the quasiparticle current. The conserved physical charge current is then obtained via the electromagnetic Ward identity as a combinati of this quantum-geometric current and the bare drift current. Therefore, the intrinsic anomalous Hall conductivity is fixed by the Berry-curvature integral of the dressed quasiparticle band while the Drude weight contains both conventional and quantum-geometric contributions. In the presence of Galilean symmetry, the Drude weight is protected against interaction renormalization. In the flat/narrow-band limit, transport is dominantly quantum-geometric and can be thermally enhanced. These results establish quasiparticle occupations, Landau interactions, and the quantum geometry carried by quasiparticles as the fundamental low-energy ingredients of a Berry-Landau Fermi liquid.

</details>

### [Geometric construction of optimal probe states in distributed multiparameter quantum sensing](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26608v1)

**Authors:** Hongzhen Chen, Lingna Wang, Haidong Yuan

**Summary.** This paper develops a geometric method to find optimal probe states for quantum sensing involving multiple parameters. By mapping the problem onto the geometry of shared eigenstates, it shows that the maximum achievable precision (QFI) is determined by the size of the minimum enclosing ball of the eigenvalue spectrum. This offers a systematic tool for designing high-precision quantum sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, general geometric framework for optimizing quantum measurements in complex, multi-parameter sensing scenarios, directly linking parameter structure to fundamental precision limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing optimal probe states for achieving ultimate precision in multiparameter quantum sensing.

**Main result.** The maximum total Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) is determined by the radius of the Minimum Enclosing Ball (MEB) of the joint eigenvalue vectors, providing a systematic design principle for optimal probes.

**Method.** The methodology involves framing the problem geometrically by analyzing the convex hull of the joint spectrum of commuting generators, leading to bounds defined by the MEB and minimum enclosing ellipsoid.

**Model / system.** The model is distributed quantum sensing involving $M$ spatially separated sensors, where the system evolution is governed by commuting local generators whose joint spectrum defines the problem's geometry.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) and the minimum weighted covariance ($	ext{C}_{	ext{W}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure and eigenvalues of the commuting parameter generators ($\vec{g}_i$), and the number of parameters ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The generators commute and share a common eigenbasis, allowing the problem to be mapped onto the geometry of the joint eigenvalue vectors.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference geometric concepts like the Minimum Enclosing Ball (MEB) and Minimum Enclosing Ellipsoid.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theory by first establishing the single-parameter case, then generalizing to the multiparameter case using geometric tools (MEB/Ellipsoid), and finally deriving the optimal state structure based on the boundary of these convex bodies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The choice of probe state is essential to achieving ultimate precision in quantum metrology. For a single parameter, an equal superposition of eigenstates associated with the largest and smallest generator eigenvalues is optimal. This simple construction does not directly extend to multiple parameters, since different parameters can favor different probes. We address this difficulty through a geometric construction of optimal probes for multiparameter estimation in distributed quantum sensing. In this setting, the generators commute and share a common eigenbasis, so each common eigenstate defines a vector of generator eigenvalues. We show that the minimal enclosing balls and ellipsoids of these vectors determine the maximal total quantum Fisher information and the minimum weighted covariance, respectively. In both cases, the boundary of the convex body identifies the common eigenstates that can contribute to an optimal probe, and its center and shape specify the conditions that their populations must satisfy. Our results provide a systematic approach to designing optimal probe states, linking the structure of the parameter generators directly to the attainable precision in multiparameter quantum metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a systematic method to construct exact parent Hamiltonians for Fractional Quantum Hall states on lattices. By comparing these exact models to those derived from simple onsite interactions, the authors demonstrate that while Laughlin states are robustly stabilized, the Moore-Read state is more susceptible to spectral mixing. This framework offers a theoretical roadmap for realizing complex topological phases in engineered quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, systematic theoretical tool to bridge the gap between idealized continuum FQH physics and the constraints of realistic, lattice-based experimental platforms, which is crucial for guiding experimental realization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a unified theoretical framework connecting lattice Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) model states, their exact parent Hamiltonians, and experimentally accessible interactions.

**Main result.** The study successfully shows that short-range density interactions stabilize Laughlin states at $
u=1/3$ and $1/4$, while the Moore-Read triplet at $
u=1$ is more sensitive to interband mixing than the Laughlin states.

**Method.** The authors systematically construct Hermitian parent Hamiltonians by sampling continuum Lowest Landau Level (LLL) model states onto a lattice, using biorthogonal duals to ensure the resulting lattice operators are Hermitian.

**Model / system.** The system models FQH phases (Laughlin and Moore-Read states) realized on lattices, using parent Hamiltonians derived from the continuum LLL structure. The analysis compares these exact parent Hamiltonians to those generated by simple projected onsite interactions.

**Key observables.** Overlap measures ($O_{regular}, O_{min}$), spectral isolation ratio ($r$), and the gap structure ($\Delta_{iso}, \delta_{low}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions ($
u=1/3, 1/4, 1$), flux densities ($\phi$), and interaction strengths ($U/J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the lattice model states can be accurately represented by sampling the continuum LLL, and the use of the Hermitian $\mu$-based Hamiltonian is preferred over the $	au$-based one.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of parent Hamiltonians and show the spectra for various FQH states ($
u=1/3, 1/4, 1$) under different interaction regimes, demonstrating spectral robustness for Laughlin states versus the sensitivity of the Moore-Read state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework for constructing lattice parent Hamiltonians using continuum LLL zero modes. It then applies this framework to benchmark Laughlin and Moore-Read states, comparing the results from the exact parent Hamiltonian against those derived from simple projected onsite interactions across varying interaction strengths and flux densities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in engineered quantum platforms have enabled the realization of bosonic Laughlin states at $ν=1/2$ and brought non-Abelian topological phases within experimental reach. A central theoretical challenge is to develop a unified framework connecting lattice fractional quantum Hall (FQH) model states, exact parent Hamiltonians, and experimentally accessible interactions. We systematically construct Hermitian parent Hamiltonians for which continuum lowest Landau level (LLL) model states sampled on lattice sites are exact zero modes. Using these model manifolds as quantitative references, we find that, within the projected lattice LLL at the flux densities studied, short-range density interactions stabilize Laughlin ground-state manifolds at $ν=1/3$ and $1/4$, while two-body onsite repulsion supports a Moore--Read triplet at $ν=1$. Full multiband calculations reveal a sharp contrast: the Laughlin manifolds remain robust against interband mixing on the studied tori, whereas the Moore--Read triplet becomes less spectrally isolated and eventually undergoes a level crossing with competing states near the interband scale. Our framework provides a theoretical foundation for using lattice model states to guide the search for experimentally accessible Abelian and non-Abelian FQH states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes the computational complexity of estimating the Berry phase for parameterized quantum systems, proving it is BQP-complete for 2-local interactions. By developing advanced simulation techniques, the authors also show a critical complexity transition, proving that 1-local Hamiltonians allow for efficient, polynomial-time computation of the Berry phase.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous complexity results for physically relevant quantities (Berry phases) in quantum simulation, directly impacting the theoretical understanding of what quantum computers can efficiently calculate regarding geometric phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the computational complexity of deciding the Berry phase for parameterized local qubit Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** It proves that this problem is BQP-complete for 2-local Hamiltonians, while establishing that the problem for 1-local Hamiltonians can be solved in polynomial time (P).

**Method.** The techniques involve extending the Schrieffer-Wolff transformation to parameterized families and utilizing the perturbative gadget reduction framework to control simulation errors.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on parameterised 2-local qubit Hamiltonians, specifically on square or triangular lattices, and extends to weighted Heisenberg interactions.

**Key observables.** The Berry phase ($\vartheta$), which is the primary observable whose computational complexity is analyzed.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The locality of interactions (1-local vs. 2-local), the required precision ($\epsilon_{	ext{alg}}$), and the initial overlap ($\delta_0$) of the guiding state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing simulator Hamiltonians that accurately approximate the target Hamiltonian up to second order, and the hardness proof requires the existence of a guiding state with sufficient overlap.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the locality of the Hamiltonian relative to known complexity results, and schematics detail the logical flow connecting uniform simulation, Schrieffer-Wolff analysis, and gadget reductions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing uniform simulation techniques for parameterized Hamiltonians, extending these to the Schrieffer-Wolff analysis, applying gadget reductions to control errors, and culminating in the hardness proof (BQP-completeness) and the polynomial-time algorithm for 1-local cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that deciding the Berry phase for parameterised 2-local qubit Hamiltonians is BQP-complete when presented with a classical description of a guiding state, promised to overlap with the ground state of the system. Our results extend to systems with weighted Heisenberg interactions and when restricted to a 2D square or triangular lattice geometry. The techniques we develop leverage the Schrieffer--Wolff transformation, typically used in the construction of perturbative gadget reductions for local Hamiltonian problems, extending it to parameterised families of Hamiltonians. We demonstrate that there exists a choice of parameterised simulator Hamiltonians whose Berry phase well-approximates that of a parameterised target family. Using the perturbative gadget reduction framework of Oliveira and Terhal and of Schuch and Verstraete, we adapt the arguments to parameterised interactions and demonstrate the error bounds in the resulting simulation can be controlled. Additionally, we provide an explicit proof that families of 1-local Hamiltonians have a Berry phase that can be efficiently computed to inverse-polynomial precision. This establishes a complexity transition between 1-local and 2-local Hamiltonian families.

</details>

### [Order-parameter-dependent mobility in the Convective-Viscous Cahn-Hilliard equation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25263v1)

**Authors:** P. O. Mchedlov-Petrosyan, L. N. Davydov, O. A. Osmaev  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25263v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figure class="paper-figure-card">

</figure>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the Convective-Viscous Cahn-Hilliard equation, focusing on the challenging case where the mobility depends on the order parameter. By restricting the mobility dependence to specific reciprocal forms, the authors successfully derive exact analytical solutions for the traveling wave fronts. This provides a significant theoretical advance over previous approximations, offering deeper insight into non-equilibrium phase separation dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical phase transitions, the mathematical techniques for solving nonlinear PDEs with complex, state-dependent coefficients are highly relevant to modeling non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems, such as domain wall motion or quench dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the complex dynamics of phase transitions modeled by the Cahn-Hilliard equation when the mobility coefficient depends non-trivially on the order parameter.

**Main result.** Exact traveling wave solutions are obtained for specific reciprocal linear and quadratic dependencies of the mobility on the order parameter, providing analytical insights where numerical methods previously were required.

**Method.** The authors employ the traveling wave ansatz ($z = x - vt$) and solve the resulting nonlinear partial differential equation by assuming a specific functional form for the derivative ($\partial u / \partial z$).

**Model / system.** The system is governed by the Convective-Viscous Cahn-Hilliard equation, which models phase separation and transitions using an order parameter $w$. The complexity arises from the mobility term $ar{M}(w)$ depending on $w$.

**Key observables.** Front velocity ($v$), the solution profile $u(x-vt)$, and the constraints on system parameters ($\kappa, v$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mobility dependence parameters ($	heta, \xi$) and the ratio of convective to diffusive terms ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to specific functional forms for mobility (reciprocal dependencies) to achieve exact analytical solutions, and it assumes the solution approaches constant values at infinity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the dependence of two specific mobility forms ($M_c$ and $M_s$) on a parameter $	au$ within the physically relevant range of the order parameter $u$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the governing equation and the difficulty of non-constant mobility. It then focuses on deriving and solving the traveling wave equation using specific mobility forms, leading to detailed analysis in appendices regarding parameter constraints and solution forms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generally the mobility in the Cahn-Hilliard equation may depend on the order parameter; this was evident already from the original derivation. However, explicit introduction of such dependence results in tremendous calculation difficulties, so the constant mobility approximation was commonly used. On the other hand, in several cases this dependence appeared to be crucial, so more realistic expressions were applied, usually positive powers or polynomials; still, for such dependencies only approximate and/or numerical solution exist. We consider 'reciprocal' linear and quadratic dependencies of mobility on the order parameter; for these dependencies exact traveling wave solutions are obtained. Even more, in a limited interval of the parameters reciprocal quadratic mobilities are rather good approximations to polynomial mobilities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models a quantum battery using a Micromaser system coupled to a memory ancilla. By analyzing the non-Markovian dynamics introduced by this memory, the authors demonstrate that memory effects significantly boost both the charging capacity and the operational stability of the device. The findings suggest that such schemes are viable for implementation in modern solid-state circuit quantum electrodynamics platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-Markovian dynamics in a cavity QED setting (Micromaser), showing how memory effects can be harnessed as a resource to improve quantum device performance.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates how non-Markovian memory effects influence the charging process of a quantum battery.

**Main result.** Non-Markovian memory effects are shown to enhance both the charging capacity and the stability of the quantum battery compared to its Markovian counterpart.

**Method.** The dynamics are analyzed using quantum evolution operators derived from sequential interactions between the battery mode, a memory ancilla, and external chargers.

**Model / system.** The system is a Micromaser Quantum Battery (QB) where energy is stored in a resonant cavity mode, coupled to a memory ancilla (TLS) which interacts sequentially with external two-level chargers.

**Key observables.** Charging capacity, stability, and energy fluctuations of the quantum battery.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength $g$, cavity frequency $\omega$, and the memory control parameter $p$ (which interpolates between Markovian and coherent regimes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Jaynes-Cummings limit/RWA, and the memory ancilla introduces the non-Markovian character.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the two sequential collisional steps: the interaction between the QB and the memory, and the interaction between the memory and the external charger.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the Hamiltonian and interaction terms for the QB-Memory and Memory-Charger steps, derives the corresponding unitary operators, and then analyzes the resulting density matrix evolution to determine the steady state and performance metrics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate the role played by non-Markovianity in the charging of a quantum battery. We focus on the case of a Micromaser, where the energy is stored in a resonant cavity mode and memory effects are introduced and controlled by coupling this mode to a memory ancilla, described as a two-level system, that in turn sequentially interacts with a stream of two-level chargers through pairwise exchange interactions leading to a partial swap. Through numerical analysis, we show that non-Markovian memory effects act as a resource that enhances both the charging capacity and the stability of this device with respect to the Markovian counterpart. We conclude our study showing that the proposed scheme can be implemented in state-of-the-art solid-state platforms for circuit quantum electrodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a statistical method to analyze diffusion dynamics on large networks by studying the statistical properties of the network Laplacian's eigenspectrum, bypassing the need to calculate all eigenvalues. Using advanced inequalities and asymptotic analysis, the authors derive how network structure changes affect the probability distribution of eigenvalues. This provides a powerful, computationally efficient diagnostic tool for characterizing transport processes in complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical diffusion, the mathematical techniques—analyzing spectral properties statistically in the large $N$ limit—are highly relevant to understanding spectral gaps and transport in complex quantum systems or quantum networks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to analyze the statistical properties of the eigenspectrum of the network Laplacian to understand diffusion dynamics efficiently, especially for very large networks.

**Main result.** The analysis derives scaled formulas for the derivatives of the maximum probability of finding eigenvalues above or below a threshold, showing how network weights influence the diffusion modes.

**Method.** The authors use statistical tools like the Gershgorin circle theorem, Cantelli’s inequality, and asymptotic analysis ($N 	o \infty$) to characterize the spectrum without computing all eigenvalues.

**Model / system.** The model is linear diffusion on a network, mathematically described by the evolution equation d(x)/dt = -D L x, where L is the network Laplacian matrix.

**Key observables.** Mean ($\mu_\lambda$) and variance ($\sigma^2_\lambda$) of the eigenspectrum; $P_{rmax}(\lambda \ge k)$ and $P_{rmax}(\lambda \le k)$ (maximum probabilities of finding eigenvalues above or below a threshold k).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network weights ($w_{ij}$); the number of nodes ($N$) in the limit $N 	o \infty$; the condition $\mu = 0.2929 a_{max}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes non-negative, constant edge weights ($w_{ij} \ge 0$) and relies on approximations derived from inequalities (Cantelli's) and limits ($N 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Gershgorin discs, the probability bound condition, and the scaled derivatives of $P_{rmax}$ with respect to edge weights ($w_{ij}$) and the threshold ($k$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the statistical framework using Gershgorin discs, then applying Cantelli's inequality to bound probabilities, and finally deriving the scaled derivatives of these probabilities with respect to network parameters in the large $N$ limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Network diffusion is a well-known model for studying how a quantity in a network propagates to reach a steady state, where its value becomes uniformly distributed across all nodes. Previous studies of network diffusion have mainly focused on the second-smallest eigenvalue of the network Laplacian to determine the timescale associated with the slowest decaying mode. However, this study investigates the eigenspectrum of the network Laplacian in terms of its statistical properties. This method is useful for studying the statistical properties of network diffusion without calculating all eigenvalues, which can be useful for large networks. Additionally, this study introduces a condition under which the system is less diffusive and investigates how changes in the network structure influence the overall diffusion dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper calculates the intersection exponents ($\xi$) that describe how the probability of multiple independent random walks avoiding each other decays in 2D and 3D. Using Monte Carlo simulations, the authors extend the analysis to complex group configurations and continuous parameters. The results provide crucial numerical data for theoretical studies of non-intersecting paths in statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical random walks, the mathematical techniques used to determine scaling exponents and analyze multi-particle avoidance probabilities are foundational to understanding critical phenomena and correlation decay in complex many-body quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the intersection exponents ($\xi$) governing the decay of the probability that several independent random walks avoid one another in two and three dimensions.

**Main result.** The authors provide numerical values for $\xi$ in 3D for various group configurations and extend the analysis to continuous moment orders ($\lambda$), confirming theoretical predictions in 2D.

**Method.** The primary method involves Monte Carlo (MC) simulations to estimate the non-intersection probability ($P_N$) and fitting this probability to a scaling ansatz $P_N \sim N^{-\xi/2}$.

**Model / system.** The system models simple random walks on a lattice in $d=2$ and $d=3$. The analysis generalizes to considering multiple groups of walks where intersections are forbidden between groups but allowed within them.

**Key observables.** The non-intersection probability $P_N$ and the associated intersection exponent $\xi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of random walks in different groups ($k, m$), the continuous moment parameter $\lambda$, and the spatial dimension ($d=2, 3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the non-intersection probability decays algebraically, $P_N \sim N^{-\xi/2}$, and involves finite-M bias corrections when estimating continuous parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the calculated non-intersection probability $P_N$ for various group sizes and the resulting exponents $\xi(k, \lambda)/2$ as a function of $\lambda$ for $k=1, 2, 3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the random-walk model and numerical methods (Section II), followed by the presentation of results for 2D and 3D intersection exponents (Section III).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The probability that several independent random walks avoid one another decays algebraically with the number of steps $N\to\infty$ in two and three dimensions, $P_N\sim N^{-ξ/2}$, where the intersection exponent $ξ$ depends on the number of random walks. More generally, one may consider several groups of independent random walks, with intersections allowed within each group and forbidden between different groups. We first study intersection exponents in two dimensions, where our results agree with the known exact formulas and test the numerical approach. In three dimensions, where no general exact expression is known, we determine $ξ$ for a range of cases. For two groups containing $k$ and $m$ random walks, respectively, we obtain the exponents $ξ(k,m)$ for $k=1,2$ and $m=1,2,\ldots,7$. We further extend $m$ to a continuous parameter $λ$, allowing us to obtain numerical values for $ξ(k,λ)$ for $k=1,2,3$ and $0.25\leqλ\leq3$. Away from the smallest moment orders, these functions increase with $λ$ with decreasing slopes, as expected from the strict concavity of Brownian intersection exponents. We also investigate three-group configurations for several representative cases. The resulting integer and continuous exponents supply numerical values for analytical studies of non-intersecting random paths.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work critiques experimental claims of observing momentum-dependent spin polarization in CrSb using Andreev reflection. By applying symmetry arguments, the authors show that the measured ASP is forbidden by symmetry in the diffusive regime and for most measured directions in the ballistic regime. This suggests the original measurements may stem from flawed definitions or experimental artifacts rather than genuine physical effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of symmetry principles to interpret complex transport measurements in magnetic topological materials is highly relevant for understanding spin-charge coupling in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper challenges claims regarding the observation of momentum-dependent spin polarization (Andreev Spin Polarization, ASP) in the altermagnet CrSb using point-contact Andreev reflection (PCAR).

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the claimed ASP is forbidden by symmetry in the diffusive regime for all measured directions and is forbidden in the ballistic regime for two of the three measured orientations.

**Method.** The analysis uses symmetry constraints and theoretical derivations for both the diffusive and ballistic regimes of Andreev reflection to calculate the expected ASP.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the altermagnet CrSb, analyzing spin transport properties across a point contact interface.

**Key observables.** Andreev Spin Polarization (ASP) measured via point-contact Andreev reflection (PCAR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Crystallographic orientations ((0001), (-1-120), (10-10)), diffusive vs. ballistic regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on symmetry properties (e.g., C2^perp||Mn) and the theoretical framework of Andreev reflection in magnetic materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing the theoretical constraints imposed by symmetry on the ASP in both the diffusive and ballistic limits, contrasting these findings with the original claims.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Zhang et al. [Phys. Rev. Lett. 137, 016701 (2026)] report momentum-dependent spin polarization in the altermagnet CrSb from point-contact Andreev reflection along the (0001), (-1-120), and (10-10) directions. We show that the Andreev spin polarization they claim to measure is forbidden by symmetry: it vanishes identically in the diffusive regime for all directions in CrSb, and in the ballistic regime for two of the three orientations measured. It is allowed only along one direction, where the calculated band structure gives ~50%. The large, nearly isotropic values reported follow from an incorrect definition of ballistic Andreev spin polarization that evaluates the spin asymmetry at fixed three-dimensional momentum; the Andreev selection rule is local only in the momentum component parallel to the interface. Moreover, the ballistic regime is not demonstrated, and the fitting procedure is flawed.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines the electrostatic pressure between two charged walls containing counterions, focusing on how modulating the surface charge density affects the force. Using the Poisson-Boltzmann framework and exact solutions to the 2D Liouville equation, the authors demonstrate that modulation can cause the pressure to increase or decrease depending on the separation distance. This provides detailed insights into the non-uniform effects of surface chemistry on confined plasmas.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical electrostatics, the use of advanced mathematical techniques like the 2D Liouville equation and the analysis of non-uniform boundary conditions are relevant to understanding boundary effects in quantum field theories or confined quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the thermal equilibrium and effective force (pressure) between two parallel walls containing counterions, specifically analyzing the effect of surface charge modulations.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that surface charge modulation can both increase (at small distances) and decrease (at large distances) the pressure between the walls compared to uniformly charged surfaces.

**Method.** The authors derive exact solutions for the electrostatic potential using the general solution of the two-dimensional Liouville equation, analyzing the system within the Poisson-Boltzmann mean-field theory.

**Model / system.** The system models a classical plasma of identical point charges (counterions) confined between two parallel walls separated by distance d. The walls possess position-dependent surface charge densities, leading to a 2D electrostatic problem.

**Key observables.** Effective force (pressure) between the walls, counterion density profile, and the electrostatic potential $\psi(x, y)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bjerrum length ($\ell_B$), Gouy-Chapman length ($\mu$), and the coupling constant ($\Xi$). The analysis focuses on the weak coupling regime ($\Xi \ll 1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The exact solutions are restricted to models where the surface charge density is generated inversely from the calculated potential, and the analysis assumes the dielectric constant of the walls matches the medium.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometry of two parallel walls at $x=\pm d/2$ with a surface charge density $\sigma(y)$ dependent on the transverse coordinate $y$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon previous work by extending exact solutions for a single EDL to two parallel EDLs, utilizing the general solution of the 2D Liouville equation to derive expressions for the pressure and charge density.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the thermal equilibrium of a classical system of identical point charges moving between two walls with parallel surfaces at a distance $d$, charged symmetrically with position-dependent surface charge densities. Specifically, we study the effect of surface charge modulation on the effective force (pressure) between the walls in the Poisson-Boltzmann limit. Based on Monte Carlo simulations at high temperatures, it is predicted that surface charge modulation reduces the pressure between the walls compared to uniformly charged wall surfaces with the same average surface charge density. We restrict ourselves to surface charge modulations in only one direction and, using a general solution of the two-dimensional Liouville equation, we construct exact solutions for the electrostatic potential. The surface charge density on the walls is generated inversely from this potential, which means that our exact results apply to a limited set of models with a specific variation of the surface charge density with distance $d$. Explicit analytical results show that surface charge modulation can both increase (small distances $d$) and decrease (large $d$) the pressure between the walls.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the critical preprocessing step for applying DMRG to 2D/3D lattices: finding the optimal site ordering. The authors demonstrate that using advanced SAT/CP solvers to optimize for cutwidth ($C$) and interaction range ($R$) is superior to minimizing graph bandwidth ($B$). This methodology provides a powerful, systematic tool for simulating complex magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on lattice ordering, the underlying techniques—using advanced constraint solvers (SAT/CP) to solve hard combinatorial optimization problems—are highly relevant for mapping complex quantum Hamiltonians onto tractable computational structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of finding an optimal linear ordering of sites on complex 2D or 3D lattices to efficiently apply the Density-Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) algorithm.

**Main result.** Optimizing the ordering by targeting the cutwidth ($C$) and then the average interaction range ($R$) lexicographically yields the best ground-state energies, significantly outperforming bandwidth-only optimizations.

**Method.** The authors propose a systematic, staged optimization approach utilizing Boolean Satisfiability (SAT) and Constraint-Programming (CP) solvers (specifically CP-SAT) to solve the NP-hard lattice compilation problem.

**Model / system.** The work applies to lattice geometries (e.g., pyrochlore, fullerene, kagome) relevant to frustrated magnetism, modeled using Hamiltonians like the SU(2) Heisenberg model.

**Key observables.** Graph Bandwidth ($B$), Cutwidth ($C$), Average Interaction Range ($R$), and the resulting ground-state energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimal ordering is determined by minimizing $(C, R)$ lexicographically, with $B$ being less critical.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Heuristic algorithms (like RCM) are unreliable for achieving true optimal orderings; the SAT/CP approach provides rigorous bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare optimal vs. heuristic orderings for rings, showing $C$ vs. $B$ planes, and comparing $R$ values derived from cutwidth-optimal versus bandwidth-optimal orderings.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, analyzes proxy loss functions ($B, C, R$), compares optimization techniques (heuristics vs. QUBO vs. SAT/CP), and systematically presents results for various complex lattice geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The density-matrix renormalization group (DMRG) is a one-dimensional tensor-network technique, but it is not limited to one-dimensional systems: it can be applied to periodic 2D and 3D clusters and molecules, provided their sites are first enumerated along a line; a step one may call "lattice compilation".   This paper discusses three proxy loss functions for finding this optimal enumeration: the graph bandwidth $B$ (maximum interaction range), the cutwidth $C$ (maximum number of bonds crossing a cut), and the average interaction range $R$. Constructing the Hamiltonian MPO (matrix-product operator) for a large set of clusters that are of interest in frustrated magnetism, I find that $C$ determines the peak SU(2) Heisenberg MPO bond dimension and $R$ the average one. Targeting $(C,R)$ lexicographically yields the best energies. Targeting $B$ indirectly reduces $C$, but not as efficiently as targeting $C$ directly. Otherwise, the value of $B$ itself is largely irrelevant in the sense that good DMRG energies can have large $B$.   To perform the optimization, classic heuristics (e.g. reverse Cuthill--McKee) prove unreliable even for small clusters, and I find that a QUBO formulation improves them only marginally. Instead I propose a staged optimization built on Boolean satisfiability (SAT) and constraint-programming (CP) solvers, chiefly CP-SAT of Google's OR-Tools, a hybrid of CP propagation and SAT clause learning. This approach yields significantly better orderings together with rigorous bounds.   The corresponding Lattice in Line code is available at https://github.com/spinflip/lattice_in_line and was designed with extensive use of the Fable 5 large language model.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes the charge transport and collective excitations in a 3D tilted nodal-line semimetal. It finds that the longitudinal Drude weight shows a logarithmic enhancement due to the tilt, and furthermore, it predicts the existence of a low-frequency, damping-free acoustic-like plasmon branch when the tilt exceeds a critical value. The methodology involves advanced calculations using the Kubo formula and RPA for the dielectric function.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of collective excitations and the emergence of gapless modes in topological semimetals provides theoretical insights into screening and low-energy dynamics, which are foundational concepts in many-body physics relevant to AMO and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the anisotropic charge response (Drude and interband polarization) in a three-dimensional tilted nodal-line semimetal.

**Main result.** The longitudinal Drude weight exhibits a logarithmic enhancement, while the system predicts a low-frequency, damping-free acoustic-like plasmon branch in the type-II tilt regime.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Kubo formula with line-integral vertices to calculate the charge response tensor, followed by a self-consistent solution for the plasmon dispersion relation.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D tilted nodal-line semimetal modeled by the Parallel-Tilted Nodal-Ring (PTNR) Hamiltonian, which includes a tilt parameter ($\eta z$) acting as a control parameter.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal Drude weight ($D_{zz}$), transverse Drude weight ($D_{\perp}$), interband polarization ($P_2$), and the plasmon dispersion $\omega_p(\mathbf{q})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tilt parameter ($\eta z$), doping ($\mu$), and the momentum transfer ($\mathbf{q}$); the analysis distinguishes between type-I and type-II tilt regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations rely on the Kubo formula, use a matched-asymptotics scheme for the long-tail integration, and the type-II results are stated within a specific Brillouin-zone regularization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Fermi-surface geometry, showing the tilted ring and the asymmetric integration interval used for the Drude weight calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the anisotropic Drude response (longitudinal vs. transverse) using the Kubo formula, then incorporates the interband polarization, and finally solves the RPA condition to identify the plasmon dispersion and the resulting damping-free window.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the anisotropic charge response of a three-dimensional tilted nodal-line semimetal in which the tilt enters the spectrum through an identity term and acts as the model control parameter. Using the Kubo formula with line-integral vertices, we show that the tilt-induced deformation of the low-energy phase space produces parametrically different longitudinal and transverse Drude responses: the longitudinal weight grows logarithmically as $D_{zz}=B\ln(1/δ)$ with the analytically determined coefficient $B=g/(2π)^2$---within the window in which the Fermi-surface tail lies inside the ultraviolet cutoff, while at a fixed physical cutoff the weight saturates instead at a cutoff-set plateau---whereas the transverse weight follows a stronger algebraic scaling $D_\perp\simδ^{-1}$; the leading $\sqrtδ$ corrections to both asymptotic forms are obtained analytically. The interband polarization retains the $q^2$ long-wavelength structure, with a coefficient that is infrared-integrable through Pauli blocking at finite doping yet carries a slow ultraviolet logarithm; it renormalizes the collective mode multiplicatively and sets the interband absorption threshold $ω_c=2μ/(1+|η_z|)$. A self-consistent solution of the plasmon dispersion identifies a tilt-dependent damping-free window bounded by the interband continuum. We predict that the type-II extension of this window, within the stated bandwidth (Brillouin-zone-like) cutoff regularization, contains a candidate low-frequency acoustic-like branch outside the particle--hole continua for $η_z\gtrsim1.2$ over a finite window in momentum transfer.

</details>

**Authors:** Swarup Sangiri, A. Taraphder  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25278v1>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel geometric framework using Bargmann invariants to characterize topological properties in condensed matter models like the Weyl and Kitaev systems. By analyzing finite-state overlaps rather than continuous geometry, the authors successfully extract chirality and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariants. This offers a robust, state-based alternative for probing complex topological physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful, purely state-based geometric tool to characterize topological phases in condensed matter systems, which is valuable for understanding boundary physics and topological invariants without relying on momentum-space continuum assumptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a finite-state geometric formulation using Bargmann invariants to probe chirality-sensitive and topological information in quantum systems, bypassing continuous state-space descriptions.

**Main result.** The method successfully translates topological invariants (like chirality and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ distinction) into rephasing-invariant, finite-state overlap formulations for both Weyl and Kitaev models.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing and analyzing higher-order Bargmann invariants (third- and fourth-order) derived from overlaps between selected quantum states.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to the two-band Weyl Hamiltonian and the one-dimensional Kitaev model of a p-wave superconductor, extending to open boundary conditions.

**Key observables.** Chirality-sensitive component of the local pseudospin geometry, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ distinction between gapped phases, and higher-order geometric relations involving Majorana edge modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The third-order invariant is key for chirality; the ratio of third-order to second-order invariants distinguishes the Kitaev phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the relevant topological and geometric information can be captured by finite-order, state-overlap invariants rather than continuous derivatives.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first applying the third-order invariant to the Weyl system to isolate chirality, then applying it to the Kitaev chain to find the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant, and finally extending the framework to construct fourth-order loops connecting edge modes and bulk states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We apply a finite-state geometric formulation based on Bargmann invariants to a two-band Weyl Hamiltonian and the one-dimensional Kitaev model of a p-wave superconductor. For the Weyl system, a symmetry-antisymmetrized third-order Bargmann combination selects the chirality-sensitive component of the local pseudospin geometry and extends to a two-band lattice Weyl model. For the Kitaev chain, a normalized ratio involving a third-order Bargmann invariant and a second-order overlap factor removes the dependence on an intermediate state and reproduces the $Z_2$ distinction between its two gapped phases. We further construct a fourth-order loop connecting localized Majorana edge-mode profiles with finite-energy BdG states and an alternating fourth-order loop involving opposite Weyl pseudospin sectors. These constructions illustrate how suitable Bargmann combinations can probe chirality-sensitive, topological, and higher-order geometric information directly through quantum-state overlaps, providing an alternative state-based route without requiring a continuous description of the underlying state-space geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a Nullspace-guided Adaptive Bootstrap (NGA) method to calculate highly accurate lower bounds on the ground-state energy of quantum many-body systems. By iteratively refining the operator basis using the nullspace of the moment matrix, it systematically improves upon existing state-of-the-art bounds for models like the Hubbard chain. This provides a scalable, rigorous theoretical tool for studying strongly correlated quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on strongly correlated systems, the underlying mathematical framework—using nullspace analysis and symmetry constraints to constrain high-dimensional SDPs—is a powerful, generalizable technique relevant to finding ground states in complex quantum models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is determining the ground-state energy of interacting quantum many-body systems, which is computationally intractable due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space.

**Main result.** The proposed Nullspace-guided Adaptive Bootstrap (NGA) method achieves significantly improved energy lower bounds, reaching errors down to $10^{-5}$ for the Hubbard chain, and automatically discovering exact structures for the Ising chain.

**Method.** The method uses an iterative, adaptive bootstrap approach that refines the basis for a Semidefinite Program (SDP) by analyzing and growing along the nullspace of the optimized moment matrix.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to model quantum many-body systems, specifically tested on the transverse-field Ising chain and the Hubbard chain, utilizing symmetry constraints (like particle-hole and $\eta$-pairing) to simplify the SDP formulation.

**Key observables.** Energy lower bound ($E_{	ext{SDP}}$), Moment Matrix ($M$), Nullspace structure, and the error gap relative to the true ground state energy ($E_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U$), hopping parameter ($t$), transverse field ($h$), and the system size ($L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the assumption that the optimal moment functional admits a normalized PSD realization, and its complexity is limited by the size of the operator basis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the nesting of variational states and the schematic workflow of the NGA bootstrap, showing the iterative process of solving the SDP, extracting the nullspace, pruning, and growing the basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the NGA bootstrap, details the mathematical machinery for enforcing symmetries (Ward identities, PH symmetry, etc.) to simplify the moment matrix constraints, and then applies this machinery to the Ising and Hubbard models to demonstrate convergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a nullspace-guided adaptive (NGA) bootstrap method that improves the energy lower bounds of quantum many-body ground states by refining the bootstrap basis in a dynamic and incremental way. At each iteration, the optimized moment matrix reveals a nullspace of saturated positivity directions, which is intuitively interpreted as annihilators of the approximate ground-state subspace. The NGA bootstrap then prunes operators with small nullspace leverage and grows the basis along descendants of these null directions. By applying the NGA bootstrap to the transverse-field Ising chain, we obtain nearly exact energy lower bounds because the algorithm automatically discovers the eigenoperator structure in terms of Jordan-Wigner fermions from a minimal local bootstrap basis. For the Hubbard chain, it improves upon state-of-the-art energy lower bounds by up to two orders of magnitude, reaching errors ranging from $10^{-3}$ down to $10^{-5}$ in the strongly correlated regimes. We further show that the NGA framework can be used to improve the certified two-sided bounds on general observables. In addition, the bootstrap error decreases approximately as a power law with increasing computational resources. These results suggest that our method provides a practical and scalable route toward accurate bootstrap of general quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a sophisticated Poincaré duality framework for analyzing defects in quantum lattice models described by Laurent polynomial rings. By constructing canonical pairings and quadratic refinements, the authors extend key topological invariants, such as the topological spin, to higher dimensions. This work provides powerful mathematical machinery for understanding the robustness of topological order in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between abstract algebraic topology (Poincaré duality) and concrete physical models like Pauli stabilizer codes provides deep mathematical tools for understanding topological phases of matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a Poincaré duality theory for defects of nondegenerate sesquilinear pairings over Laurent polynomial rings.

**Main result.** It establishes canonical pairings and constructs distinguished quadratic refinements, extending the 2D T-junction formula for topological spin to higher dimensions.

**Method.** The approach combines advanced algebraic topology (Poincaré duality, flat resolutions, equivariant cohomology) with module theory over Laurent rings.

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to translation-invariant Pauli stabilizer codes, which model quantum lattice systems and are relevant to topological order and quantum error correction.

**Key observables.** Duality defects, canonical sesquilinear pairings (Br_p), and quadratic refinements (q or q_L).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the system, and conditions related to the signature $\epsilon(-1)^{d/2}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that certain charge modules have finite length, and the construction of quadratic refinements requires specific algebraic conditions.

**Figures summary.** The text references theorems (A, B, C) and equations detailing the construction of resolutions, pairings, and quadratic forms, particularly relating to the T-junction formula.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing a flat resolution of the character module, then defining the duality defects and pairings, and finally constructing the explicit quadratic refinements using geometric methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a Poincaré duality theory for defects of nondegenerate sesquilinear pairings over Laurent polynomial rings. A key ingredient is a novel flat resolution of the character module, constructed from a triangulation of the sphere at infinity associated with a fan. The cup product on this resolution turns Poincaré duality on the sphere into canonical pairings between the resulting defect modules. In middle degrees, we construct distinguished quadratic refinements using equivariant cohomology of the sphere with the antipodal action. The effective replacement of the sphere with a projective space provides a geometric substitute for division by two. Applied to translation-invariant Pauli stabilizer codes, our results establish the nondegeneracy of higher-dimensional braiding pairings. They extend the two-dimensional T-junction formula for topological spin to higher dimensions, while giving it a geometric interpretation.

</details>

Black solid curves are the symmetric resonant reference; green dashed curves use detuned cells ωi = (0.92, 1.00, 1.08)ω0 at uniform coupling; red dotted curves use resonant cells with γi = (0.4, 1.0, 1.6)γ. The identity WB = ∆EB −ℏωminν−holds pointwise in all three cases; the coincidence of extrema is not asserted away from the symmetric protocol.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates the relationship between extractable work (ergotropy) and quantum correlations in a multi-cell bosonic quantum battery. The authors derive an exact connection showing that work loss is governed by entanglement, and they decompose the charging power into spectral and transfer components. The findings clarify the role of coherence in local work extraction, providing a detailed resource theory analysis for bosonic quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep insights into the fundamental thermodynamic resources (work vs. entanglement) in open quantum systems, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information processing using bosonic modes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a direct dynamical connection between extractable work (ergotropy) and quantum correlations (like entanglement) in complex, multipartite bosonic quantum systems.

**Main result.** It shows that the loss of extractable work is directly determined by the charger-battery entanglement, and that local work extraction is entirely supported by energetic coherence in the considered regime.

**Method.** The analysis uses exact solutions derived from the quadratic nature of the Hamiltonian, allowing the reduction of the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space to a rank-two support spanned by two coherent-state branches.

**Model / system.** The system is a bosonic quantum battery consisting of a harmonic-oscillator charger coupled to three oscillator cells, modeled by a number-conserving Hamiltonian. The dynamics are analyzed under coherent-state superpositions.

**Key observables.** Ergotropy (extractable work), charger-battery entanglement (concurrence), and multipartite correlations (three-tangle).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Symmetric resonant regime ($\omega_c = \omega_i = \omega$), single-photon loss, and the charging parameter $y$ (or time $t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the system dynamics retaining a simple, two-branch coherent-state structure, and the initial state being a coherent product state.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general work-correlation relation, then analyzing the dynamics in the symmetric resonant regime, decomposing the work into coherence and population contributions, and finally examining the behavior under environmental loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ergotropic-gap relations provide thermodynamic witnesses of quantum correlations, but a direct dynamical connection between extractable work and entanglement remains largely unexplored in multipartite non-Gaussian bosonic systems. Here, we establish such a connection for a bosonic quantum battery driven by a coherent-state superposition. Despite the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, the dynamics retains a simple structure that allows the relevant work and correlation measures to be obtained exactly. We show that the loss of extractable work associated with the passive part of the battery energy is directly determined by charger-battery entanglement. This relation also separates the charging power into contributions from energy transfer and from changes in the battery spectrum. At the same time, multipartite correlations are progressively redistributed from charger-involving correlations to correlations internal to the battery. For a symmetric resonant protocol, complete energy transfer coincides with charger-battery disentanglement and fully extractable stored energy, while multipartite correlations within the battery are maximal. Individual cells, however, remain mixed, revealing a distinction between global and local work extraction. We further show that, in the regime considered, locally extractable work is entirely supported by energetic coherence. Under single-photon loss, the analytical structure survives, but environmental correlations separate charger disentanglement from maximal work extraction.

</details>

**Summary.** This study investigates how intrinsic biaxial stress affects the superconducting properties of niobium thin films by comparing DC transport measurements with high-precision microwave resonator measurements. The authors find that while DC measurements suggest a stable superconducting state, the microwave response reveals a strong, stress-dependent loss mechanism. They conclude this anomalous behavior is due to a secondary, low-gap superconducting component, demonstrating that microwave techniques are necessary to detect subtle, stress-induced low-energy excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics because it demonstrates how subtle, low-energy excitations (the low-gap component) can dominate macroscopic measurements (microwave response) even when the primary superconducting gap remains robust, providing a model for hidden degrees of freedom in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the discrepancy between superconducting properties measured by DC transport versus microwave response in niobium thin films, particularly focusing on the influence of intrinsic biaxial film stress.

**Main result.** The anomalous microwave response, characterized by enhanced loss and frequency shifts in highly compressive films, is attributed to an additional, stress-dependent low-gap superconducting component, which is missed by conventional transport measurements.

**Method.** The research uses microwave resonators (CPW) to measure loss and frequency shifts across varying film stresses, complementing DC transport measurements and employing two-component superconducting models for fitting.

**Model / system.** The system is niobium (Nb) thin films sputtered under controlled biaxial stress. The analysis uses a two-component model to fit the microwave response, separating the contribution of the main superconducting phase from a secondary, low-gap component.

**Key observables.** Microwave loss (1/Q_i), relative frequency shift (Δf/f), superconducting transition temperature (Tc), and residual resistance ratio (RRR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Biaxial in-plane stress (up to 1 GPa), low-gap critical temperature (Tc_2nd ≈ 2.4 K), and cryogenic temperatures (down to 70 mK).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the Mattis–Bardeen basis functions are valid approximations and that the anomalous response is dominated by the secondary component, ruling out vortex motion as the primary cause of the thermal loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the stability of transport properties (Tc, RRR) versus stress, contrasted with the strong stress dependence of microwave loss and frequency shift. Later figures confirm the fit to a two-component model and rule out vortex contributions by comparing zero-field and field-cooled measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the discrepancy between transport and microwave measurements under stress. It then develops a two-component model to fit the microwave data, identifying the low-gap component. Finally, it rigorously tests alternative sources of anomalous loss, such as trapped vortices, and rules them out.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the microwave response of sputtered Nb films as a function of biaxial film stress. Although transport measurements show nearly identical superconducting transition temperatures ($9.0\pm 0.2$K) and residual resistance ratios across the stress series, microwave resonators fabricated from highly compressive films exhibit enhanced temperature-dependent loss and larger frequency shifts. A conventional single-phase Mattis--Bardeen description yields an effective critical temperature of $2.4\pm0.2$K, approximately a factor of four below the value obtained from transport measurements. We show that the data are instead consistent with the presence of an additional low-gap component whose contribution increases with compressive stress. These results show that conventional transport measurements can overlook stress-dependent low-energy excitations that strongly influence microwave performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the excitonic insulator phase in $	ext{Ta}_2	ext{Pd}_3	ext{Te}_5$ by studying its coherent phonon modes. The key finding is an anomalous, strong lifetime enhancement of these phonons below the transition temperature, which is proposed to be a robust fingerprint of exciton condensation. The enhancement is quantitatively explained by modeling the phonon as an 'order-parameter polaron' coupled to the excitonic condensate fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of lifetime enhancement due to coupling with a quantum condensate (exciton) provides a clean, measurable signature of an electronic phase transition, analogous to superconducting gap signatures in tunneling spectroscopy, making it relevant to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To identify a robust, purely electronic fingerprint for the excitonic insulator (EI) phase in $	ext{Ta}_2	ext{Pd}_3	ext{Te}_5$, especially since the transition occurs without a detectable structural change.

**Main result.** The hallmark of exciton condensation is an extraordinarily strong, non-anharmonic lifetime enhancement observed in the coherent phonon-amplitude modes below the transition temperature ($T_C \approx 100 	ext{ K}$).

**Method.** The study combines time-resolved pump-probe spectroscopy to measure phonon lifetimes and amplitudes with theoretical modeling using exciton-polaron and coupled phonon-exciton models.

**Model / system.** The system is $	ext{Ta}_2	ext{Pd}_3	ext{Te}_5$, which enters an EI phase. The theoretical framework involves modeling the coherent phonon mode as coupling to the fluctuations of the excitonic order parameter, leading to an 'order-parameter polaron' description.

**Key observables.** Coherent phonon lifetimes ($	au$), phonon frequencies ($
u$), and the temperature/fluence dependence of the lifetime enhancement factor ($\eta(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transition temperature ($T_C \approx 100 	ext{ K}$), excitonic gap ($\Delta \approx 80 	ext{ meV}$), and the coupling strength ($\lambda$) derived from the polaron model.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement is attributed to the coherent phonon being 'dressed' by the excitonic condensate fluctuations, and the analysis relies on fitting the data to specific polaron or coupled decay models.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the temperature dependence of phonon lifetimes, clearly illustrating a deviation from expected anharmonic decay below $T_C$. Theoretical fits using the exciton-polaron model successfully reproduce this anomalous lifetime enhancement.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the EI phase via transport/spectroscopy, then analyzes the temperature dependence of the phonon response, contrasting anharmonic predictions with the observed anomaly, and finally models this anomaly using the exciton-polaron framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The excitonic insulator (EI) is an electronic phase of condensed excitons. However, in many prototypical materials the presence of a concurrent structural transition complicates the identification of a purely electronic origin of the ordered state. Here, we investigate Ta2Pd3Te5, in which an EI phase develops below TC ~ 100 K in the absence of a detectable structural transition. We characterize the excitonic condensate via its coherent phonon-amplitude response. Most strikingly, an extraordinarily strong lifetime enhancement of these modes sets in below TC which we establish as a new and robust fingerprint linked to exciton condensation. We discuss possibilities to capture the enhancement when taking into account a coupling of excitonic and lattice effects in line with the coupled phonon-amplitude response. Within an order-parameter polaron picture coupling to the excitonic state may dress the phonon mode and thereby suppress its relaxation and a phenomenological model combines the anharmonic phonon response and a reduced electronic scattering due to the gap opening in the EI phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes Anderson localization in a non-Hermitian quasiperiodic lattice, extending previous models to the non-Hermitian domain. The authors introduce 'mobility arcs' as a novel class of mobility edges, showing that delocalized states occupy only zero-measure sets. The findings reveal a deep mathematical connection between these arcs, closed rings, and lines through a generalized Joukowski transformation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding of measure-zero delocalization is a profound result in spectral theory, providing a precise mathematical characterization of extended states in complex, non-Hermitian, and quasiperiodic settings, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Anderson localization in a one-dimensional lattice subjected to non-Hermitian off-diagonal quasiperiodic disorder.

**Main result.** It discovers 'mobility arcs' as a new class of measure-zero delocalization sets, which coexist with closed mobility rings, demonstrating that delocalized states occupy only zero-measure sets in the complex energy plane.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes Avila's global theory to derive the exact Lyapunov exponent and the complete phase diagram in the complex energy plane.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1D lattice governed by a non-Hermitian Hamiltonian with quasiperiodic off-diagonal disorder, parameterized by hopping constants and modulation parameters.

**Key observables.** Lyapunov exponent, mobility edges (arcs, rings, lines), and the average growth-decay ratio (f).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quasiperiodicity parameters ($\lambda, 	au, eta$), the modulation period ($\kappa$), and the energy $E$ in the complex plane.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending known results from Hermitian to non-Hermitian regimes and assumes ergodicity for the map used in the theory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the mosaic modulated lattice for $\kappa=2$ and $\kappa=3$. Other figures compare transfer matrix calculations with analytical formulas for the Lyapunov exponent.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by setting up the non-Hermitian quasiperiodic model, applying Avila's theory to calculate the Lyapunov exponent, and then deriving the topological structure of the mobility edges, showing the common mathematical origin of rings, arcs, and lines via the generalized Joukowski transformation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate Anderson localization in a one-dimensional lattice with non-Hermitian off-diagonal quasiperiodic disorder, extending a recently studied Hermitian mosaic model to the non-Hermitian regime. Using Avila's global theory, we derive the exact Lyapunov exponent and the complete phase diagram in the complex energy plane. This work contains two central findings. First, we discover mobility arcs---open curved segments in the complex plane---as a new class of mobility edges and the generic form of open mobility edges, which coexist with closed mobility rings in a complementary parameter regime. These arcs share the same localization physics as the previously reported mobility lines: eigenstates are delocalized if and only if their energies lie exactly on these sets; any deviation yields localized states. This constitutes a striking measure-zero delocalization phenomenon: delocalized states occupy only zero-measure sets (arcs or lines) in the complex plane, in sharp contrast to the mobility rings, which enclose a finite-area region of delocalized states. Second, we reveal that mobility rings, arcs, and lines all share a common mathematical origin in the generalized Joukowski transformation $P(E) = \frac{1}{2}(u - w^2/u)$, rooted in the algebraic structure of the underlying polynomial: the preimage of the boundary of an elliptical region under the polynomial map $P(E)$ gives the rings, while the branch cut inside this ellipse gives rise to the mobility arcs and lines in the complementary parameter regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that contrary to prior assumptions, node heterogeneity can promote stability in complex network systems, especially when the underlying dynamics are high-dimensional. This stabilizing effect is mathematically rooted in the non-Hermiticity of the system's Jacobian matrix. The paper establishes disorder not as a flaw, but as a general resource for enhancing the robustness of complex systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide a general principle—that disorder can be a resource—which is highly relevant for designing robust quantum or classical networks where parameter variability is expected.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper challenges the conventional view that node heterogeneity inhibits stability in network dynamics, particularly when simplified models are used.

**Main result.** Nodal heterogeneity can actively enhance stability in complex systems, a mechanism fundamentally linked to the non-Hermiticity of the system's Jacobian matrix.

**Method.** The analysis uses linearization around equilibrium points and investigates the stability via the Jacobian matrix and Lyapunov exponents, comparing 1D vs. higher-dimensional dynamics.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes general network systems, including those with higher-dimensional nodal dynamics (e.g., mechanical metamaterials, power grids), modeled by coupled differential equations.

**Key observables.** Largest transverse Lyapunov exponent ($\Lambda_{\max}$), stability regions (Arnold tongues), and the degree of mode mixing.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nodal heterogeneity parameters ($\mathbf{b}$), network structure ($\mathbf{A}$), and the dimensionality of the nodal dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Previous work often used model reductions that may misrepresent the true stability impact; the analysis focuses on continuous-time systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that second-order models can achieve optimal stability with heterogeneous damping, and that stability enhancement is visible across various network types and disorder strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to show that non-Hermiticity, arising from higher-dimensional dynamics, allows disorder to act as a stabilizing resource, contrasting this with results from simplified 1D models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Previous studies of network dynamics have suggested that heterogeneity among nodes inhibits stability, at odds with the ubiquity of inherently heterogeneous natural and engineered systems. Here, we show that this conclusion arises from model reductions introduced for mathematical tractability and breaks down when nodal dynamics are higher-dimensional, yielding non-Hermitian Jacobians. In such systems, including neural, power-grid, and material networks, nodal heterogeneity can instead enhance stability, even when parameters are randomly disordered. Non-Hermiticity also underlies the stabilizing effects of network heterogeneity, which can arise even in one-dimensional nodal dynamics through nonreciprocal interactions, as shown for ecological networks. Our framework reveals disorder not as a liability but as a general resource for stabilizing complex systems.

</details>
