**Summary.** This paper introduces BOPS, a novel generative model utilizing Schrödinger bridges, to perform quantum circuit optimization. It frames the task as learning a continuous transformation from a complex source circuit to a minimal equivalent circuit. By outperforming established optimizers, it advances the field by integrating deep generative ML methods into quantum compilation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges advanced machine learning techniques (diffusion models, generative modeling) directly into the quantum compilation stack, offering a data-driven approach to quantum circuit optimization that moves beyond fixed algebraic rules.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is quantum circuit optimization: finding an equivalent quantum circuit that has fewer gates and lower depth to reduce computational cost and error rates.

**Main result.** The proposed generative model, BOPS, significantly outperforms nine baseline optimizers, achieving reductions of 2.46x in gate count and 2.45x in depth on tested circuits.

**Method.** The authors propose using a generative model based on Schrödinger bridges to learn the transformation from a source circuit to an optimized equivalent circuit directly from examples.

**Model / system.** The system under study is quantum circuits, represented by sequences of gates using the Clifford+T gate set. The optimization is framed as a continuous transport problem between the source and target circuit distributions.

**Key observables.** Gate count, circuit depth, and the exact equivalence of the resulting unitaries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The circuit size is tested on held-out 8 qubits $	imes$ 64 depth Clifford+T circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that circuit optimization can be modeled as a continuous process bridged by a generative model, and it relies on constructing a paired training dataset by applying rewrite rules backward.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the BOPS architecture and training pipeline, while other figures detail the structure of the circuit representation tensor and the systematic application of SAT-mined rewrite rules for simplification.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the theoretical framework using Schrödinger bridges for circuit-to-circuit transport, presents the BOPS model architecture, trains it on synthetic data, and validates its performance against state-of-the-art hard-coded and search-based optimizers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum circuit optimization replaces a circuit with an equivalent one of fewer gates and lower depth, reducing execution cost and error rate. We ask whether a generative model can learn this transformation directly from examples, rather than selecting from a fixed rewrite library or rigid algebraic routines. We present Bridge of $Ψ$'s (BOPS), a generative model based on Schrödinger bridges, using a custom denoiser architecture, that learns a transformation from a source circuit into an equivalent optimized circuit. We train it on data constructed to be hard for existing optimizers, by applying rewrite rules backwards so that each input has a known lower-cost target. On held-out 8 qubits $\times$ 64 depth Clifford+$T$ circuits, BOPS reduces gate count by $2.46\times$ and depth by $2.45\times$ in geometric mean, outperforming all nine baseline optimizers. This constitutes the first generative model bridging quantum circuits and frontier machine learning methods, opening up the quantum compilation stack to learned optimization along multiple axes.

</details>

### [Bulk OPE Coefficients of the $E$-Series Virasoro Minimal Models](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25215v1)

**Authors:** Amaury Lhoste, Jiaxin Qiao, Masahito Yamazaki  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25215v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.25215_figures/2609.25215_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Analytic continuation of the q-numbers for q+ (orange) and q−(green). For n &gt; 1, we have log([n]q) &gt; 0 when q &gt; 0 and arg(q) = 0. The red zigzag line is the branch cut.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper calculates the full set of bulk OPE coefficients for Virasoro minimal models associated with $E_6, E_7,$ and $E_8$ symmetries. By leveraging the conformal bootstrap and reducing the problem to algebraic constraints involving quantum-group symbols, the authors provide explicit, systematic results for these coefficients. This work is crucial for understanding the structure of highly symmetric, exactly solvable quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous determination of OPE coefficients in highly constrained CFTs provides deep insights into the structure of quantum symmetries, which underpins many modern approaches to strongly correlated systems and quantum field theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To explicitly calculate the bulk Operator Product Expansion (OPE) coefficients for all Virasoro minimal models possessing an exceptional modular invariant ($E_6, E_7, E_8$).

**Main result.** The authors provide explicit coefficient tables and reconstruction conventions for these coefficients, reducing the problem to a finite algebraic structure governed by quantum-group 6j symbols.

**Method.** The calculation uses the conformal bootstrap approach, assuming Virasoro symmetry, bulk locality, and OPE associativity, and reducing the problem by dividing by universal chiral coefficients.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Rational Conformal Field Theories (CFTs), specifically the $E$-series Virasoro minimal models, which are characterized by their modular invariance related to the exceptional Lie groups $E_6, E_7, E_8$.

**Key observables.** Bulk-primary OPE coefficients ($C_{IJK}$), which are required in specific numbers (18 for $E_6$, 32 for $E_7$, 107 for $E_8$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The central charge $c$ and the Kac indices $(r, s)$ defining the primary operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation assumes only Virasoro symmetry, bulk locality (permutation symmetry), and OPE associativity (crossing equations); the initial seed step remains uncertified.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the paper provides coefficient tables and reconstruction conventions.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the problem, details the reduction to an algebraic problem, presents the results for the three exceptional families, and discusses the implications for existence and uniqueness across all Kac labels.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present bulk-primary operator-product coefficients for every Virasoro minimal model with an $E_6$, $E_7$, or $E_8$ modular invariant. Dividing by a universal product of chiral generalized-minimal-model OPE coefficients reduces the conformal bootstrap problem to a finite algebraic problem governed by root-of-unity quantum-group $6j$ symbols. The exceptional factors of the OPE coefficients can be chosen independent of the second Kac indices $s$, and their dependence on the central charge reduces to finitely many reference cases and explicit phases. A finite computer-assisted calculation yields candidate algebraic $s=1$ seed data and high-precision numerical evidence for existence and uniqueness modulo primary-field signs. We prove that exact existence and uniqueness for these seeds imply the corresponding result for all Kac labels within the genus-zero bulk locality and crossing equations; the finite seed step remains uncertified. The three families require $18$, $32$, and $107$ representative reduced OPE coefficients for $E_6$, $E_7$, and $E_8$, respectively. We provide reconstruction conventions and coefficient tables for both unitary and nonunitary models. Our calculation assumes only Virasoro symmetry, bulk locality (permutation symmetry) and OPE associativity (crossing equations).

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously compares the subcritical mass gap and the Lee-Yang gap for the planar Ising model. Using sophisticated tools like random-current moment bounds and correlation inequalities, the authors establish a precise power-law relationship between these two fundamental spectral quantities near the critical point. This work represents a significant mathematical advance in the understanding of phase transitions in lattice models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous comparison of spectral gaps (mass gap vs. Lee-Yang gap) provides deep mathematical tools applicable to understanding spectral properties in quantum many-body systems, especially near critical points.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously compare the subcritical mass gap ($m_eta$) with the Lee-Yang gap ($L_eta$) in the planar Ising model.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the mass gap and Lee-Yang gap are comparable near criticality, specifically showing $c L^{8/15}/eta \le m_eta \le C L^{8/15}/eta$ for the subcritical regime.

**Method.** The proof combines advanced techniques including random-current moment bounds, correlation inequalities, finite block expansions, and analysis of the Lee-Yang product zeros.

**Model / system.** The system is the planar, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ Ising model, studied in the sub-critical regime ($0 < eta < eta_c$). The analysis extends to the Random Field Ising Model (RFIM) in some parts.

**Key observables.** Mass gap ($m_eta$), Lee-Yang gap ($L_eta$), infinite-volume susceptibility ($\chi_eta$), and the partition function $Z_{eta,\Lambda}(h)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical inverse temperature $eta_c$, the deviation from criticality $t = eta_c - eta$, and the coupling strength $eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on several advanced inequalities (e.g., correlation inequalities, Griffiths–Kelly–Sherman inequalities) and involves conjectures regarding the RFIM limit.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops bounds on correlation functions and partition functions using various inequalities (e.g., Lemma 5.1, Lemma 5.2) to ultimately establish the quantitative relationship between the two gaps.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the subcritical mass gap is comparable to the Lee-Yang gap over free-boundary square boxes raised to the power $8/15$. Random-current moment bounds, a correlation inequality and a finite block expansion give the upper bound. The lower bound follows from the Lee-Yang product, the susceptibility and the critical magnetization. We also obtain uniform factorial bounds on the field derivatives of the pressure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive study of the magnetic and transport properties of the ferromagnetic material CeRu2Ge2. By applying Crystal Electric Field (CEF) theory to experimental data from magnetization, resistivity, and specific heat, the authors successfully explain the observed magnetic anisotropy. The work highlights that the magnetic easy axis is dictated by the CEF ground state, providing a detailed picture of local moment magnetism in this correlated material.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of magnetic anisotropy and the successful application of CEF theory to explain the magnetic ground state in a heavy fermion ferromagnet provides insights into how crystal symmetry dictates magnetic ordering in correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To conduct a detailed anisotropic study on the magnetic and transport properties of the ferromagnetic single crystal CeRu2Ge2 and explain the observed phenomena using the Crystal Electric Field (CEF) framework.

**Main result.** The magnetic anisotropy and overall magnetic behavior are quantitatively explained by the CEF model, showing the easy axis aligns along the [001] direction, consistent with the CEF ground state.

**Method.** The study combines multiple experimental techniques (magnetization, resistivity, specific heat) with theoretical analysis using the point-charge Crystal Electric Field (CEF) model.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the CeRu2Ge2 single crystal, which is analyzed using the CEF Hamiltonian, which is fitted to experimental data to determine the electronic energy level scheme.

**Key observables.** Ferromagnetic transition temperature (Tc = 7.5 K), positive magnetoresistance, magnon excitation gap, magnetic susceptibility anisotropy ([100] vs [001]), and CEF energy levels (three doublets).

**Important parameters / regimes.** CEF parameters (e.g., B2^0 = -25 K), molecular field constants (lambda_x, lambda_z), and the magnon gap energy (Delta).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the point-charge CEF model, and the interpretation of large negative molecular field constants as anisotropic exchange rather than direct antiferromagnetic exchange.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD patterns, magnetization vs. T/B curves along different axes, resistivity vs. T curves with magnon-gap fits, and MR vs. B plots.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from structural characterization and basic magnetic/transport measurements to detailed CEF analysis using specific heat (Schottky effect) and susceptibility data, culminating in a full explanation of the magnetic anisotropy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a detailed anisotropic studies on the magnetic and transport properties of a CeRu$_2$Ge$_2$ single crystal. A clear ferromagnetic transition is observed at $T_{\rm C} = 7.5$~K in the magnetic susceptibility, electrical resistivity and heat capacity measurements. The magnetoresistance (MR) is positive at low temperature within the magnetically ordered state. We confirmed the presence of a magnon excitation gap in the resistivity, MR and the low temperature heat capacity data. The pronounced anisotropy observed in the magnetic susceptibility and magnetization along the [100] and [001] directions is quantitatively explained within the crystal electric field (CEF) framework using the point-charge model. The analysis reveals a CEF level scheme comprising three doublets, with a ground state and two excited states at 491 and 671~K, that was further confirmed from the Schottky effect in the magnetic part of the heat capacity data. Unlike most Kondo lattice ferromagnets of Ce compounds, where the magnetic easy axis deviates from the expected CEF ground state, CeRu$_2$Ge$_2$ exhibits magnetization, which is fully consistent with the CEF derived ground state wave function, with the easy axis of magnetization aligned along the [001] direction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates why diffusion models exhibit 'malign overfitting'—a failure mode worse than expected—despite being fundamentally regression-like. By analyzing the bias and variance of the score estimator, the authors show that the model memorizes training data rather than learning the true underlying distribution. They conclude that while overparameterization is powerful, it requires explicit regularization to prevent catastrophic generalization failure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a deep theoretical understanding of generalization failure in complex generative models, drawing parallels between statistical physics concepts (like phase transitions or critical points) and machine learning generalization boundaries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the paradox of 'malign overfitting' observed in diffusion models, contrasting it with the expected 'benign overfitting' from standard deep learning regression.

**Main result.** Overfitting in diffusion models is malignant because the implicit regularization drives the model to memorize the empirical score rather than the true underlying score, which is mitigated only by explicit regularization.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical derivations using random-features models (Gaussian Equivalence Principle) with empirical studies on U-Nets, employing bias-variance decomposition to pinpoint the failure mechanism.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the training of diffusion models (like DDPM-style U-Nets) to estimate the score function of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. The theory is rigorously tested using Random Feature Neural Networks (RFNNs).

**Key observables.** Test loss ($L_{	ext{test}}$), Empirical test loss ($L_{	ext{emp}}^{	ext{test}}$), Training loss ($L_{n,m}^{	ext{train}}$), Bias, and Variance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model size ($p$), sample size ($n$), noise realizations ($m$), and regularization strength ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical analysis often relies on the high-dimensional limit ($p, n, d \gg 1$) and specific asymptotic approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the double descent curve, showing the divergence between $L_{	ext{test}}$ and $L_{	ext{emp}}^{	ext{test}}$ in the overparameterized regime, and demonstrating that regularization restores optimal performance.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the paradox, develops theoretical tools (RFNN analysis), diagnoses the mechanism via bias/variance decomposition, and concludes by showing that regularization is necessary to restore beneficial overparameterization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional wisdom in deep learning holds that overparameterization---having more parameters $p$ than training samples $n$---is benign: larger models generalize better and, even without regularization, interpolating models generalize well, the test error following a double-descent curve. One might expect the same benign overfitting for diffusion models, whose training reduces to regression, i.e. to minimizing a quadratic score-matching loss. Yet the opposite is observed: overfitting here is catastrophic, driving the model into a memorization regime. We resolve this paradox by combining experiments on U-Nets trained on CelebA with a random-features model for which we derive closed-form learning curves. We show that with a fixed number $m$ of noise realizations per training sample, an interpolation peak does occur, but at $p\sim nm$ rather than at $p\sim n$ as in standard regression. The rise of the test loss, however, sets in much earlier, at $p\sim n$, independently of $m$. This overfitting is malign because, although the implicit regularization of training is fully at work, it drives the model toward the empirical score, which memorizes the training set, rather than toward the true score. A bias-variance decomposition pinpoints the mechanism: the bias of the score estimator starts to grow at $p\sim n$; past the peak the variance decays, as in regression, whereas the bias keeps growing and both saturate at a large value. Since diffusion models are trained with $m\gg1$, the peak is pushed to very large model sizes, and therefore sit on the rising branch that precedes it, where malign overfitting is already in play. Nevertheless, overparameterization remains beneficial when paired with regularization: in the random-features theory and in U-Net experiments, optimally regularized large models---via a ridge penalty or early stopping, respectively---outperform any unregularized models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous, representation-theoretic framework to analyze bosonic quantum error-correcting codes. By viewing the QEC matrix as a group representation morphism, the authors systematically analyze how these codes protect against noise like photon loss. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for designing hardware-efficient fault-tolerant quantum memories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, abstract mathematical tool (representation theory) to analyze physical error correction codes in bosonic systems, which is highly relevant for quantum optics and superconducting circuit implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a general framework for constructing and analyzing the error-correction properties of covariant bosonic codes.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that the Quantum Error Correction (QEC) matrix can be viewed as a morphism of group representations, allowing for the systematic study of error protection for various bosonic codes.

**Method.** The authors employ a representation-theoretic framework, defining codewords via a covariant encoding that acts as a morphism between logical and physical group representations.

**Model / system.** The system involves bosonic modes, where logical information is encoded in the infinite-dimensional Hilbert space. The analysis focuses on error protection against amplitude damping channels (photon loss) using finite subgroups of SU(2).

**Key observables.** The QEC matrix ($Q_{kl}$) and the near-optimal fidelity ($	ilde{F}$) are key diagnostics used to quantify the code's robustness.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves parameters related to the size of the group representations ($d_L, N$) and the order of the error operators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the applicability of the representation-theoretic view of QEC, and the analysis relies on using the computationally tractable near-optimal fidelity ($	ilde{F}$) instead of the optimal fidelity ($F_{	ext{opt}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from preliminaries on Representation Theory and Covariant Encoding (Section II) to establish the general framework connecting the QEC matrix to group representation morphisms (Section III). Appendices detail proofs regarding the action of group elements on single-photon states and the structure of the QEC matrix.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bosonic codes offer a promising approach towards hardware-efficient fault tolerance, with many leading examples such as the cat-code and the GKP code organized by an underlying symmetry. We build on a representation-theoretic framework of quantum error correction to formalize the construction and analysis of multimode symmetric bosonic codes, focusing on finite groups. Here, a physical, logical representation and an initial seed state together define the codewords via a covariant encoding. A central observation is that QEC matrices can be viewed as morphisms of group representations, so that Schur's lemma forces entire irreducible representations blocks to vanish. We recover several known single- and two-mode bosonic codes and build and analyze the error protection capabilities of three codes based on finite subgroups of SU(2). We analyze the impact of reducibility of the logical representation and optimize the seed state using the near-optimal fidelity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a resource-efficient method called heterogeneous-distance lattice surgery for fault-tolerant quantum computing across noisy links. By selectively enlarging the ancilla at the inter-QPU boundary while keeping data patches smaller, the authors significantly reduce physical qubit overhead compared to standard methods. Simulations show this approach maintains excellent error suppression even as link noise increases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the scaling bottleneck in quantum computing by providing a resource-efficient error correction scheme for noisy, modular interconnects, which is crucial for realizing large-scale quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving fault-tolerant quantum operations across inter-QPU links, which are significantly noisier than local gates in distributed quantum computing architectures.

**Main result.** The proposed heterogeneous-distance lattice surgery (8-DAM) architecture maintains low logical error rates at high link noise while significantly reducing the physical qubit overhead compared to uniform-distance implementations.

**Method.** The authors introduce a novel lattice surgery protocol using an enlarged central ancilla and traveling stabilizers to suppress hook errors during merge/split operations between unequal-distance data patches.

**Model / system.** The model uses the rotated surface code implemented across modular Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) connected by noisy inter-QPU links. The core technique is the 8-DAM architecture, where the central ancilla is enlarged while data patches retain a smaller, fixed distance $d$.

**Key observables.** Logical-readout error rates, physical qubit overhead (qubit count), and the comparison of error suppression factors against uniform-distance lattice surgery.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local depolarizing noise ($	ext{p}_{	ext{local}}$), inter-QPU link noise ($	ext{p}_{	ext{link}}$), and the specific distance parameters used in the heterogeneous construction (e.g., $3	ext{--}7	ext{--}3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that local QPU operations are fault-tolerant if the distance is high enough, and it specifically excludes modeling link-generation failures, leakage, or correlated link noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of the rotated surface code patches, the merge configurations for lattice surgery, and the resulting 8-DAM patch architecture, along with error rate comparisons for single and dual logical CNOT operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews background on rotated surface codes and lattice surgery, presents the heterogeneous 8-DAM constructions and simulation results, and concludes by discussing the resource-efficient implications for fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum computing requires fault-tolerant operations across inter-QPU links that can be substantially noisier than local gates. Uniformly increasing code distance provides additional protection but also enlarges data patches used for local storage and computation. Here, we introduce distributed heterogeneous-distance lattice surgery using an eight-data-patch ancilla-mediated (8-DAM) architecture, which will be useful for near-term quantum devices with less qubit overhead. In this architecture, the central ancilla spanning the inter-QPU boundary is enlarged while the data patches retain distance $d$. The protocol uses traveling stabilizers to suppress hook errors during merge and split operations between these unequal-distance patches. Circuit-level simulations of rotated surface codes at fixed local depolarizing noise show that logical-readout error rates depend only weakly on link noise. The resulting advantage over conventional lattice surgery grows as link errors increase. Comparisons with uniform distance implementations demonstrate comparable logical error suppression with reduced physical-qubit overhead. We also demonstrate how 8-DAM layouts support two simultaneous distributed logical CNOT operations between four logical data qubits using a single enlarged ancilla. At higher link noise, this construction yields lower logical error rates and more stability than two independent distributed logical CNOTs. These results support selective ancilla enlargement as a resource-efficient approach to fault-tolerant distributed quantum computing.

</details>

**Authors:** Sayam Sethi, Maxwell Poster, Aditi Awasthi, Willers Yang, Joshua Viszlai, Jonathan Mark Baker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25251v1>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how to improve concatenated quantum error-correcting codes by mitigating the inherent asymmetry between X and Z error channels. The authors propose and test six specific 'deformation' strategies applied to the building block code. They demonstrate that careful deformation can balance the X and Z logical error rates while improving the overall error suppression capability.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses resource optimization in quantum error correction by providing concrete, structured methods (deformations) to mitigate systematic biases in logical error rates, which is crucial for scaling quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Concatenated Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECC) suffer from logical error rate (LER) skewness between X and Z observables due to the inherent asymmetry of the building block code.

**Main result.** One proposed deformation strategy successfully achieves near-identical X and Z LERs while simultaneously reducing the total LER in a logical-memory experiment.

**Method.** The authors formalize the space of Clifford-deformations (Hadamard deformations) and compare six concrete strategies using a concatenated maximum-likelihood soft-information decoder.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on concatenating the [[4, 1, 2]] Iceberg code. The analysis uses a phenomenological noise model encompassing both unbiased (depolarizing) and biased noise channels.

**Key observables.** Logical Error Rates ($	ext{LER}_X$ and $	ext{LER}_Z$), Total LER, and Code Capacity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Concatenation level ($l$), physical error rate ($p$), and the specific deformation strategy employed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the maximum-likelihood decoder applied level-by-level, and the search space for optimal deformations grows doubly exponentially.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Iceberg code structure, the six deformation strategies, and the resulting LER performance under unbiased and biased noise across multiple concatenation levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of X/Z LER imbalance in concatenated codes, formalizes the deformation space, proposes and compares six specific deformation strategies, and evaluates their performance using simulation under various noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Concatenated quantum error-correcting codes have recently gained popularity because they improve the effective distance of a joint code without requiring the discovery of new codes with desirable parameters. A common building block for such studies is the $[[4,1,2]]$ Iceberg code, the smallest error-detecting code and therefore the one with the lowest resource overhead. However, it has an asymmetric number of $X$ and $Z$ checks, and concatenating it produces skewed logical error rates (LERs) between the $X$ and $Z$ observables. This asymmetry can be mitigated by Clifford-deforming the individual blocks of the concatenation, but the space of possible Hadamard deformations grows doubly exponentially with the number of concatenation levels. We formalize this deformation space and investigate it by proposing six concrete deformation strategies, which we compare under a code capacity (with and without bias) noise model using a concatenated maximum-likelihood soft-information decoder. Among the strategies we study, one one of them achieves near-identical $X$ and $Z$ LERs in a logical-memory experiment while simultaneously reducing the total LER (the sum of the $X$ and $Z$ error rates).

</details>

### [Good Quantum Locally Testable Codes from Product Expansion](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26735v1)

**Authors:** Mitali Bafna, Anqi Li, Quynh T. Nguyen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26735v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to construct high-quality Quantum Locally Testable Codes (qLTCs) by combining concepts from algebraic topology and coding theory. It overcomes previous limitations by employing non-Abelian cubical complexes within a high-dimensional expansion framework. The result provides a theoretical blueprint for building quantum codes with excellent, constant performance guarantees.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for constructing robust quantum error-correcting codes, which are foundational for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to construct quantum locally testable codes (qLTCs) that possess desirable constant parameters (rate, distance, soundness, locality).

**Main result.** The authors construct an explicit family of good qLTCs by leveraging a variant of the product expansion conjecture and utilizing non-Abelian cubical complexes.

**Method.** The construction method involves equipping high-dimensional non-Abelian cubical complexes with carefully chosen Reed-Solomon local codes whose symmetries are compatible with the complex structure.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Locally Testable Codes (qLTCs), which are defined using CSS codes derived from underlying graph/complex structures, generalizing from 2D to higher dimensions.

**Key observables.** Constant rate, quantum distance, soundness, and locality of the constructed code.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The construction requires the local codes to satisfy a $ho$-product-expanding property, and the rate must be non-zero.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction critically relies on assuming a variant of the product expansion conjecture of Bafna and Vyas.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first reviewing classical LTCs on Cayley Complexes, then generalizing to the high-dimensional expansion framework, and finally improving the construction by incorporating non-Abelian cubical complexes and RS codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct quantum locally testable codes (LTCs) with constant rate, distance, soundness and locality under a variant of a product expansion conjecture of Bafna and Vyas about Reed-Solomon codes. In particular, we use the high-dimensional expansion framework of Dinur, Lin and Vidick for constructing quantum LTCs, instantiated with the non-Abelian cubical complexes of Rungtanapirom, Stix and Vdovina. Our code is obtained by equipping the complex with carefully chosen Reed-Solomon local codes whose symmetries are compatible with those of the complex.

</details>

**Authors:** Sujan Shrestha, Matthew Coile, Menglin Zhu, Maryam Souri, Jiwoong Kim, Rina Pandey, Joseph Brill, Jinwoo Hwang, Jong-Woo Kim, Ambrose Seo  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25328v1>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the successful fabrication of freestanding, high-quality $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{IrO}_4$ thin-films using a water-soluble buffer layer. By analyzing structural, optical, and magnetic properties, the authors show that these freestanding membranes retain the characteristic signatures of the bulk material. This technique opens a promising avenue for developing flexible electronic devices based on strongly correlated layered oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work demonstrates a novel, scalable method (freestanding films) to study strongly correlated materials like $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{IrO}_4$ in a mechanically flexible format, allowing for tuning of electronic properties beyond traditional epitaxial constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing techniques to create high-quality, freestanding, layered oxide thin-films for flexible electronics, overcoming substrate-related limitations.

**Main result.** The freestanding $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{IrO}_4$ nanomembranes maintain structural and optical properties analogous to single crystals, suggesting a viable pathway for flexible layered-oxide electronics.

**Method.** Fabrication involves growing $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{IrO}_4$ on a $	ext{Sr}_3	ext{Al}_2	ext{O}_6$ buffer layer, followed by leaching to create freestanding films, followed by structural and spectroscopic characterization.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Sr}_2	ext{IrO}_4$, a layered Mott insulator with $J_{	ext{eff}} = 1/2$, studied in freestanding thin-film form. The structure is stabilized by interfacial layers formed during the fabrication process.

**Key observables.** Phonon modes, two-magnon Raman scattering peak ($\omega_{2M}$), optical absorption spectra, and lattice constants (in-plane and out-of-plane).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange interaction ($J$) derived from $\omega_{2M}$ ($\approx 60 	ext{ meV}$); tensile strain; and the presence of interfacial $	ext{SrIrO}_3$ layers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The structural integrity and electronic properties of the freestanding film are comparable to their epitaxial counterparts, despite the removal of the substrate.

**Figures summary.** Figures show cross-sectional STEM images detailing interfacial layers, XRD/RSM data comparing as-grown vs. freestanding films, and Raman spectra illustrating phonon modes and temperature-dependent two-magnon scattering.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the fabrication process using a soluble buffer layer, followed by comprehensive characterization using XRD, STEM, and various spectroscopies (Raman, optical absorption) to confirm structural coherence and measure key physical properties.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the structural and optical properties of nanoscale Sr2IrO4 freestanding thin-films fabricated using a water-soluble Sr3Al2O6 layer. The coherent lattice structure, phonon modes, two-magnon Raman scattering, and optical absorption spectra of the Sr2IrO4 nanomembrane are analogous to those of the layered iridate epitaxial thin-films and single crystals. Remarkably, the formation of 3-unit-cell-thick SrIrO3 and interfacial composite layers alleviates antiphase boundaries at the Sr2IrO4/Sr3Al2O6 interface, resulting in structurally-robust nanomembranes. Our experimental results show that this freestanding thin-film approach of layered oxides can provide techniques for tuning or realizing unprecedented states beyond conventional thin-film methods, suggesting a pathway in achieving flexible layered-oxide electronics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a quantum algorithm achieving a significant speedup for solving graph problems like coloring and finding independent sets in the distributed quantum setting. By showing that certain problems solvable in polylogarithmic classical rounds can be solved in constant quantum rounds, it establishes the first natural examples of quantum advantage for the LOCAL model. The methodology involves advanced quantum information theory applied to graph structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical techniques used to establish quantum advantage in a highly structured computational model like the LOCAL model are relevant for understanding the limits of quantum computation in distributed physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to demonstrate and quantify the quantum advantage for solving natural graph problems in the distributed computing setting, specifically within the LOCAL model.

**Main result.** It shows that any Locally Checkable Labeling Problem (LCL) solvable in $O(\log^* n)$ rounds classically can be solved in $O(1)$ rounds quantum-locally, providing natural examples of quantum advantage.

**Method.** The authors develop quantum algorithms by constructing specific one-way quantum-LOCAL operators and analyzing their convergence properties using operator norms and energy bounds.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is the comparison between the classical LOCAL model and the quantum-LOCAL model for distributed computation on input graphs. The core mechanism involves analyzing the time evolution of quantum operators related to graph colorings.

**Key observables.** Round complexity (e.g., $O(1)$ vs. $\Theta(\log^* n)$), local failure probability ($\le \epsilon$), and the expectation values of time evolution operators $p_n(w)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of colors ($q$), the convergence parameter ($s$), and the maximum degree ($\Delta$) of the graph.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on bounding operator norms (Frobenius norm) and assuming the smallness of error terms ($\eta$) relative to the required precision.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing a core result (1-round quantum coloring of cycles) to generalizing this to all LCLs. The mathematical development involves constructing the necessary quantum operators via iterative methods and proving their convergence and positivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a distributed quantum algorithm that $3$-colors cycles in $O(1)$ rounds, with high probability. It follows that all locally checkable labeling problems (LCLs) that have round complexity $O(\log^* n)$ in the classical LOCAL model can be solved in $O(1)$ rounds in the quantum-LOCAL model, with high probability; this includes problems such as maximal independent set and maximal matching in bounded-degree graphs. This presents the first natural examples of graph problems with an asymptotic distributed quantum advantage for the LOCAL model; all prior examples that separate LOCAL and quantum-LOCAL are artificial problems constructed merely for the sake of demonstrating quantum advantage.

</details>

**Authors:** Paula Belzig, Sukanya Ghosal, Farzin Salek, Graeme Smith  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26750v1>

**Summary.** This work derives the inner bound for the confidential classical capacity of quantum broadcast channels, ensuring that two transmitted messages remain secret from each other. Using advanced information theory, the authors prove that for certain channels, the achievable confidential rates are strictly lower than what standard quantum capacity measures predict. This highlights the crucial role of mutual secrecy constraints in quantum communication theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of confidential capacity regions and the explicit separation from standard quantum capacity bounds provide deep insights into the fundamental security limits of quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the fundamental limits of transmitting two independent, mutually confidential classical messages over a quantum broadcast channel.

**Main result.** It establishes an inner bound on the confidential classical capacity region, and demonstrates that for certain channels, this achievable rate region lies outside the unassisted quantum-capacity region.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced information-theoretic techniques, including establishing Marton-type inner bounds, using likelihood-selected coding schemes, and applying complex multi-letter capacity theorems.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a Quantum Broadcast Channel (CQ channel) designed to transmit two messages ($M_1, M_2$) such that each message remains secret from the other receiver, even when the other receiver knows its own message.

**Key observables.** Achievable rate pairs $(R_1, R_2)$, average error probability ($P_e^{(i)}$), and conditional strong-secrecy parameters ($\Delta_{1,n}, \Delta_{2,n}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the block length $n 	o \infty$ limit, and the specific structure of the quantum channel (e.g., Blackwell or Platypus channels).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of finite auxiliary variables and the use of normalized likelihood weights for encoding.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the confidential-message model for cq channels, showing the two secrecy conditions. Figure 2 illustrates the likelihood selection process for fixed indices.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the inner bound by first establishing the classical Marton-type bound, then providing a concrete classical-quantum realization using likelihood-selected coding, and finally comparing this confidential classical capacity to the unassisted quantum capacity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the transmission of two independent confidential classical messages over a quantum broadcast channel, one for each receiver. Each message must remain secret from the other receiver, including when that receiver knows its own message. For classical inputs and quantum outputs, we establish the classical Marton-type inner bound under average reliability and conditional strong secrecy. The encoder selects pairs of codewords from independently generated codebooks using normalized likelihood weights. We prove reliability through a change-of-distribution argument. Our main technical result is a bipartite classical-quantum resolvability theorem that accounts for the dependence created by pair selection and establishes secrecy for the same encoder. We also obtain a multi-letter capacity characterization and extend it to arbitrary quantum inputs under secrecy against the other receiver, together with the Stinespring environment. We recover confidential capacity regions for deterministic classical and degraded classical-quantum channels, with an explicit evaluation for the classical Blackwell channel. For coherent isometric extensions of injective deterministic classical broadcast channels, we show that the confidential classical capacity region equals that of the corresponding classical channel. We then compare confidential classical communication with quantum transmission. For the coherent isometric extension of the Blackwell channel, we determine the unassisted quantum-capacity region and show that some achievable confidential classical rate pairs lie outside it. The Platypus channel provides another example of this separation.

</details>

<a id="paper-2609.25660"></a>
### [Schrödingerization for quantum linear systems problems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25660v1)

**Authors:** Long Zhang, Yin Yang, Yue Yu  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25660v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an advanced Schrödingerization algorithm to solve quantum linear systems by transforming the problem into an inhomogeneous convection equation. By employing Duhamel's principle and Fourier projection, the authors achieve an optimal query complexity of $O(\kappa_A \log(1/\epsilon))$. This represents a significant algorithmic improvement for quantum computation tasks involving linear system inversion.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of Schrödingerization to solve linear systems is a powerful technique for quantum simulation, offering a potentially superior scaling compared to older methods by avoiding separate LCU steps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a novel, efficient quantum algorithm to solve quantum linear systems of equations (QLSPs), aiming to prepare a state proportional to the solution $x = A^{-1}b$.

**Main result.** The proposed Schrödingerization algorithm achieves a query complexity of $O(\kappa_A \log(1/\epsilon))$ queries, matching the standard worst-case scaling for solving QLSPs.

**Method.** It reformulates the problem using Duhamel's principle to create an inhomogeneous convection equation, which is then solved via a two-dimensional Schrödingerization process involving Fourier projection and an auxiliary variable.

**Model / system.** The core model is the quantum linear system $Ax=b$, where $A$ is an invertible matrix. The method is tested on various systems, including positive-definite and indefinite matrices.

**Key observables.** Solution error ($\ell_2$ state error $\le \epsilon$), recovery probability ($P_p$), and the condition number ($\kappa_A$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Condition number $\kappa_A$, target accuracy $\epsilon$, and the evolution time $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes exact oracle access and requires a valid constant-factor estimate of the solution norm $\xi$ for the block preconditioning step.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate error decay curves showing the benefit of increasing time steps ($m$) and comparing the superior recovery probability achieved using interval recovery versus point recovery.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the convection representation using Duhamel's principle, establishes rigorous discretization and recovery error bounds using spectral theory, details the final algorithm and its complexity analysis, and concludes with numerical simulations on various systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a Schrödingerization algorithm for quantum linear systems problems in two higher dimensions. The earlier LC-Schrödingerization approach represents the solution as a time integral of a homogeneous convection response and implements this integral by a linear combination of unitaries (LCU) over evolution times. We instead use Duhamel's principle to incorporate the integral into an inhomogeneous convection equation with zero initial data. Fourier projection in the convection variable and Schrödingerization in a second auxiliary variable then realize the solution without the separate LCU step. A joint choice of the kernel and recovery procedure gives an evolution time independent of the target accuracy, a uniformly bounded $L^2$ kernel normalization, and accurate recovery on a fixed interval. We establish the periodization and discretization bounds and analyze the interval recovery probability. With block preconditioning, we retain a single logarithmic precision factor and obtain linear condition-number dependence without variable-time amplitude amplification. Under exact oracle access and given a valid constant-factor solution-norm estimate, the algorithm uses $\mathcal O(κ_A\log(1/\varepsilon))$ queries to each original input oracle, with constant success probability and $\ell^2$ state error at most $\varepsilon$. The matrix-query bound matches the standard worst-case scaling when the supplied norm bounds are tight. UnitaryLab simulations on positive-definite and indefinite systems demonstrate the solution accuracy and probability gain from interval recovery.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous mathematical improvement on the bounds for the complex Grothendieck constant using advanced techniques from functional analysis and complex Gaussian theory. It establishes a new, tighter upper bound by combining ideas from real and noncommutative complex cases. The work is significant for pure mathematics, though its underlying tools touch upon areas relevant to quantum correlation bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques used—involving Gaussian processes, kernel methods, and bounding complex correlation structures—have deep mathematical parallels to quantum information theory and quantum channel analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine sharper mathematical bounds for the complex Grothendieck constant, $K_G^{\mathbb{C}}$, a constant arising from complex matrix inequalities.

**Main result.** The authors establish a new, tighter upper bound for the constant: $1.4 < K_G^{\mathbb{C}} < 1.404898554746$, improving previous bounds from Haagerup and Davie.

**Method.** The methods involve advanced functional analysis techniques, including constructing and analyzing 'valid rounding kernels' using jointly proper standard complex Gaussians and optimizing complex quadratic forms.

**Model / system.** The context is abstract functional analysis, specifically dealing with matrix norms and inequalities related to complex correlation structures, rather than a physical Hamiltonian or lattice model.

**Key observables.** The complex Grothendieck constant ($K_G^{\mathbb{C}}$), and related quantities like $\kappa_r$ (cumulant expressions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bounds themselves (e.g., $1.404898554746$), and parameters derived from Gaussian moments and polynomial approximations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing the validity of correlation kernels via Gaussian projections and solving complex optimization problems over parameter spaces.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the text references theorems and equations detailing the derivation of the bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the constant and its associated inequalities, to developing the machinery of valid rounding kernels using Gaussian projections, and finally culminating in the rigorous derivation and comparison of the improved upper and lower bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that $1.4<K_G^{\mathbb{C}}<1.404898554746$, where $K_G^{\mathbb{C}}$ is the complex Grothendieck constant. The upper bound improves on Haagerup's bound of $1.40490913\ldots$ from 1987, and the lower bound improves on Davie's bound of $1.33807$ from 1984. The upper bound combines ideas from the real and noncommutative complex cases of Grothendieck's inequality.

</details>

### [Strong matchgate designs in nearly optimal depth](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26677v1)

**Authors:** Maxwell West, M. Cerezo, Martin Larocca  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26677v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.26677_figures/2609.26677_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1. Our construction proceeds in three steps: (1) Randomly sample m ∈O(k2n log(n/ε)) pairs 1 ⩽ai &lt; bi ⩽2n, and m angles ϑi; apply the corresponding Majorana rotations {Rai,bi(ϑi)}m i=1. (2) Check if those rotations can be parallelised into O(k2 log(n/ε)) layers of commuting gates; if not, go back to step one. (3) Compile each parallel layer of Majorana rotations into two qubit gates of depth O(rt(G) log n) via a fermionic router. Concatenate two such independently accepted circuits; the total depth is then given by (# Layers) × O(rt(G) log n) = O(k2rt(G) log(n/ε) log n).</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops methods to construct high-quality quantum states (k-designs) over the matchgate group, which is relevant for quantum simulation and tomography. It achieves this by analyzing the depth required based on the underlying quantum hardware's connectivity graph. The key finding is that the required depth scales polynomially with $k$ and the graph's routing number, offering significant speedups over previous theoretical limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The results provide concrete, resource-aware estimates for implementing complex quantum tasks (like tomography) on near-term hardware architectures by optimizing the required circuit depth based on physical connectivity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the resource cost (circuit depth) required to generate approximately random unitaries, specifically $k$-designs, over the matchgate group on quantum hardware.

**Main result.** They provide a construction achieving strong $\epsilon$-approximate relative error matchgate $k$-designs in depth $O(k^2 r_t(G) \log n \log(n/\epsilon))$ for general connectivity, and show optimal depth for $k=3$ in the all-to-all case.

**Method.** The construction uses a random walk on the matchgate group, followed by compiling the resulting operations into quantum circuits using techniques related to fermionic routing and Clifford group gates.

**Model / system.** The system involves $n$ qubits and the matchgate group $MG(n)$, which is generated by 2-Majorana rotations. The complexity is analyzed based on the connectivity graph $G$ and its routing number $r_t(G)$.

**Key observables.** Design quality ($\epsilon$-approximate relative error $k$-design), circuit depth ($D$), and the routing number $r_t(G)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits ($n$), design order ($k$), approximation error ($\epsilon$), and graph connectivity ($r_t(G)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on spectral bounds derived from the random walk operator and the ability to compile permutations using Clifford gates, with the general case overcoming limitations of nearest-neighbor connectivity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three-step construction process: sampling rotations, checking for parallelization into layers, and compiling layers into two-qubit gates. Figure (A) shows the structure of a binary tree for $n=3$ qubits.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general depth bound for $k$-designs using random walks. It then details the compilation of permutations of Majoranas into depth $O(r_t(G) \log n)$ using Clifford gates. Finally, it provides lower bounds on the required depth for $k$-designs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the resources required to generate approximately random unitaries over various groups is a natural goal of quantum information theory. With respect to one notion of approximation, that of a design, it is known that the full unitary group can be approximated in logarithmic depth by one-dimensional circuits of nearest-neighbour 2-local gates. On the other hand, remarkably, circuits with this connectivity cannot form designs over the matchgate group in sublinear depth. Here we show that this dramatic slowdown can disappear when using a general qubit connectivity graph $\mathsf{G}$ of routing number ${\rm rt}(\mathsf{G})$. Indeed, in this setting one can obtain (strong) $\varepsilon$-approximate relative error matchgate $k$-designs in depth $\mathcal{O}(k^2{\rm rt}(\mathsf{G})\log n\log(n/\varepsilon))$. For all-to-all connectivity, ${\rm rt}(\mathsf{G})=2$. Our construction is conceptually simple, involving a random walk on the matchgate group, and no ancillae. As a technical byproduct, we improve upon the state of the art for fermionic routing, obtaining an $\mathcal{O}({\rm rt}(\mathsf{G})\log n)$ depth router. Additionally, for $k=3$, we obtain an exact strong matchgate design in $\mathcal{O}({\rm rt}(\mathsf{G})\log n)$ depth, again without ancillae. Under all-to-all connectivity, our fermionic router and 3-designs are optimal. Notably, our results imply that quantum algorithms for fermionic tomography which require drawing from a matchgate 3-design may be exponentially sped up on quantum computers with all-to-all connectivity, relative to their strictly one-dimensional counterparts.

</details>

**Authors:** Paul Beame, Niels Kornerup, Michael Whitmeyer  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26678v1>

**Summary.** This paper advances the theory of quantum query complexity by proving strong direct-product theorems for selective and list-decoding settings. It achieves this by introducing a generalized multiplicative adversary method capable of handling relations. These results provide tighter lower bounds on the resources needed for quantum computation compared to previous methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the development of generalized adversary methods for quantum query complexity provides powerful new tools for establishing lower bounds on quantum algorithms, which is fundamental to quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes strong direct-product theorems to cover quantum query complexity for selective and list-decoding scenarios, extending classical results.

**Main result.** It proves quantum strong selective and list-decoding direct-product theorems for all partial Boolean functions, establishing new lower bounds for quantum query complexity.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced adversary methods, specifically developing a new multiplicative adversary formulation for relations that captures lower bounds previously established by negative-weights adversaries.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum query complexity, analyzing the computational power required to compute functions or relations $R$ using quantum algorithms.

**Key observables.** Quantum query complexity, success probability, and the structure of the adversary method bounds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of inputs ($m$), the number of required answers ($k$), and the structure of the relation $R$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proofs rely on extending multiplicative adversary methods to handle relational structures and generalizing techniques from classical randomized complexity.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but they detail the mathematical structure of proofs involving projectors and norms.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops results in stages: first, establishing selective direct-product theorems using a new adversary method; second, proving the quantum analogue of list-decoding direct-product theorems; and third, providing detailed technical lemmas (e.g., Lemma D.4) to support the general bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum strong direct-product theorems for specific functions have been known for nearly two decades. These have been extended to general results for function computation and state generation. The proofs of these results use a version of the multiplicative adversary method that does not naturally extend to relations. Standard strong direct-product theorems apply when algorithms must correctly answer every given question. Prior work extended them to equivalent threshold direct-product theorems, which require answers to all questions but only require that most answers are correct.   We focus on two further generalizations. Strong selective direct-products apply to algorithms that adaptively choose, based on what they learn from queries, which questions from a large list to answer. This generalization is relational and useful for proving time-space tradeoffs. We prove a quantum strong selective direct-product theorem for all functions using a new multiplicative adversary formulation for relations that satisfies a strong selective direct product property while being strong enough to capture any query lower bound for functions proven by negative-weights adversaries. This was not previously known even without selectivity.   The second generalization is list-decoding direct product problems introduced by Ben-David and Blais for classical randomized query complexity. These allow an algorithm to produce a large list of possible output vectors such that one of them is fully correct. They proved that such theorems hold for classical randomized complexity of all Boolean functions. We prove a quantum analogue of this theorem for all partial Boolean functions. We show that strong list-decoding direct-product theorems are implied by a special case of multiplicative adversaries which we show, via a new reduction, can be obtained from negative-weights adversaries for any Boolean-valued function.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves the failure of simultaneous quantum typicality for multipartite quantum states, establishing precise thresholds for when this failure occurs. By constructing explicit counterexamples, the authors disprove several major conjectures in quantum information theory. The work relies on advanced mathematical techniques connecting entropy rates to polynomial concentration bounds.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental, quantitative limits on how well local measurements can predict global quantum correlations, which is crucial for understanding resource theories and entanglement measures in quantum information.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the failure of 'simultaneous quantum typicality,' which concerns whether local marginal subsystems of a quantum state $ho^{\otimes n}$ retain the expected entropy rate when the state is perturbed.

**Main result.** The authors establish exact thresholds showing that simultaneous quantum typicality fails for three parties in mixed states and four parties in pure states, disproving major conjectures in the field.

**Method.** The proof connects the typicality failure to polynomial concentration on $\mathbb{CP}^1$, utilizing Bernstein-Markov estimates to show an exponential obstruction to simultaneous typicality.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses quantum states on tensor products of local systems, exemplified by four-qubit states (e.g., the Higuchi--Sudbery state) and mixed tripartite states.

**Key observables.** Trace distance between the actual state and the tensor power of the input, and the purity bounds on overlapping marginals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties (3 or 4), the fixed positive entropy slack ($\delta$), and the number of copies ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The failure is shown to be robust, persisting in an open neighborhood of the counterexample state, and holds for both purity bounds and operator norms.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of simultaneous quantum typicality and reviews related conjectures. It then constructs a specific counterexample state (e.g., for four qubits) and uses mathematical tools like polynomial concentration and entropy deficit to prove the asymptotic distinguishability from the tensor product state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish exact thresholds for the failure of simultaneous quantum typicality: three parties for mixed states and four for pure states. For the four-qubit Higuchi--Sudbery state, every sequence satisfying the expected purity bounds on three overlapping marginals, with sufficiently small fixed positive entropy slack, is asymptotically perfectly distinguishable from the tensor powers of the input: its trace distance from them tends to the maximum value $2$. This maximal separation persists, with a common positive slack, throughout an open neighborhood in the full four-qubit state space. Tracing out one party yields a mixed tripartite example.   The proof connects simultaneous quantum smoothing with polynomial concentration on $\CP^1$. Symmetry reduces the problem to a frame inequality on $\Sym^n(\mathbb C^2)$, where a linear entropy deficit competes with a quadratic cost of polynomial concentration. Bernstein--Markov estimates and a subharmonic barrier control this cost, yielding an exponential obstruction to simultaneous typicality.   These results disprove Dutil's multiparty quantum typicality conjecture, formulated in 2011, and the one-shot simultaneous min-entropy-smoothing conjectures of Drescher--Fawzi and Colomer--Winter. Together with the positive results for smaller systems, they determine the smallest numbers of parties at which simultaneous typicality can fail.

</details>

**Summary.** This study uses large-scale Monte Carlo simulations to map the complex phase diagram of $5d^1$ double perovskites, which feature coupled spin, orbital, and quadrupolar degrees of freedom. The research distinguishes between phases stable under classical thermal fluctuations and those that require quantum mechanical treatment. It establishes that the FM[110] state is intrinsically quantum, while the dominant magnetic phases are robustly classical.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed, fluctuation-driven phase diagram for strongly correlated $5d$ systems, directly addressing the boundary between classical and quantum stability in multiferroic materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between spin, orbital, and quadrupolar degrees of freedom in $5d^1$ double perovskites and determines the stability of exotic magnetic and multipolar phases against thermal fluctuations.

**Main result.** The simulations show that while the AFM and FM 4-sublattices are robustly classical, the coplanar canted FM[110] state destabilizes, identifying it as an intrinsically quantum state.

**Method.** The research employs large-scale classical Monte Carlo simulations on the projected $j=3/2$ manifold of the FCC lattice to map the finite-temperature phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is $5d^1$ double perovskites, modeled with an effective Hamiltonian incorporating nearest-neighbor AFM/FM exchange and electric quadrupole-quadrupole interactions, projected onto the $j=3/2$ basis.

**Key observables.** Orbital occupations ($\langle	ilde{n}_{\alphaeta}angle$), staggered magnetization ($m_{xy}^{	ext{stg}}$), and various susceptibilities ($\chi^{-1}_{	ext{tot}}$, $\chi^{-1}_{\parallel}$, $\chi^{-1}_{\perp}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of exchange couplings ($J_2/J_1$) and the quadrupolar interaction strength ($V/J_1$) govern the transitions between different ordered phases.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the system's essential physics can be captured by treating the effective moment classically, thereby surpassing mean-field limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate orbital occupations and magnetization across different coupling regimes, while correlation functions (FIG. S2-S4) diagnose the nature of order (e.g., long-range FM vs. short-range AFM).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the phase diagram by analyzing transitions (PM $	o$ Quad $	o$ FM or PM $	o$ AFM) as a function of coupling constants, using MC simulations to track order parameters and correlation functions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-orbit-coupled $d^1$ double perovskites exhibit a rich interplay of spin, orbital, and quadrupolar degrees of freedom (DOF), giving rise to competing magnetic and multipolar phases. Although quantum mean-field theories predict many exotic phases, their stability against thermal fluctuations and reproducibility within a classical framework remain an open question. Here, we surpass the mean-field limitations by deploying large-scale classical Monte Carlo simulations on the projected $j=3/2$ manifold of the FCC lattice, allowing complex ordering patterns to emerge spontaneously without preassigned magnetic symmetries. Our thermodynamic mapping reveals that thermal fluctuations melt the intermediate-temperature quadrupolar phase over part of the phase diagram, while it survives intact elsewhere. Crucially, by systematically isolating the boundary between classical and quantum stability, we demonstrate that while the four-sublattice antiferromagnetic and ferromagnetic (FM) phases are robustly classical, the coplanar canted FM[110] state completely destabilizes. This identifies the FM[110] phase as an intrinsically quantum state born out of quantum fluctuations. Our results demonstrate that the dominant magnetic phases are robust within a classical description, where the essential physics of the system is captured by weakly entangled, short-range correlated DOF and highlight the role of thermal fluctuations in determining the stability of different types of magnetic and quadrupolar order.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes topological phase transitions in electronic systems subjected to structural disorder, modeling the transition from a perfect crystal to an amorphous state using defect introduction. By employing the eigenstate spillage indicator, the authors demonstrate that structural defects induce pseudo-band inversions, leading to a quantifiable reversal of the system's topological invariant, the Chern number.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of using local structural metrics (like defect density) to predict global topological invariants (like the Chern number) is highly relevant for understanding disorder effects in quantum materials, bridging condensed matter theory with structural physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how structural defects, such as Stone-Wales defects, drive topological phase transitions in electronic systems, moving from ordered crystalline states to amorphous ones.

**Main result.** The introduction of structural disorder causes pseudo-band inversions and a measurable reversal of the Chern number, allowing the prediction of the amorphous topological phase diagram from the crystal.

**Method.** The authors employ adiabatic arguments, using the Symmetry Indicator (SI) and a novel 'eigenstate spillage' indicator ($\gamma$) to track topological invariants across the disorder parameter space.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using electrons on a decorated honeycomb lattice described by a Weaire-Thorpe Hamiltonian. The disorder is introduced by continuously generating Stone-Wales defects, parameterizing the transition from crystalline ($\alpha=0$) to amorphous ($\alpha=1$) states.

**Key observables.** Chern number, pseudo-band inversion, eigenstate spillage indicator ($\gamma$), and the topological phase diagram.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder parameter ($\alpha$), magnetic flux ($\phi$), and hopping parameters ($W$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The electronic properties of amorphous systems can be inferred from crystalline systems with identical local environments, and the analysis relies on disorder averaging techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural change via SW defects, the evolution of the energy spectrum across the transition, and phase diagrams mapping topological invariants against the disorder strength $\alpha$ using the spillage indicator.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the Hamiltonian and the amorphization procedure, then introducing diagnostic tools like the spillage indicator, and finally applying these tools to map out the full topological phase diagram connecting the crystalline and amorphous regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structural defects, such as disclinations and dislocations, destroy the long-range crystalline order as they proliferate. In this work, we continuously drive a system from a crystalline state on a decorated honeycomb lattice to a hyperuniform amorphous state, by consecutively introducing Stone-Wales (SW) defects, which can be viewed as dipoles of dislocations. Using a topological Weaire-Thorpe Hamiltonian model to describe electrons in this system, we demonstrate that SW defects can cause pseudo-band inversions and, correspondingly, a reversal of the Chern number. Using adiabatic arguments and a computationally efficient spillage indicator, we predict the topological phase diagram of the amorphous state from the crystalline one. We explain the nature of the pseudo-band inversion through the renormalization of the hopping in an effective Hamiltonian based on a single SW defect.

</details>
