**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous mathematical framework to construct quantum error-correcting codes, specifically qLDPC and qLTCs, capable of supporting transversal non-Clifford gates. By employing advanced tools from algebraic topology, such as cup product pairings on cubical complexes, the authors prove the existence of such codes with optimal parameters. This is a major theoretical advance for fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the fundamental challenge of fault-tolerant quantum computation by providing explicit constructions for implementing non-Clifford gates on highly structured, good quantum codes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is constructing quantum error-correcting codes that can support nontrivial transversal logical non-Clifford gates while maintaining optimal error-correction parameters.

**Main result.** The authors prove the existence of asymptotically good quantum LDPC and qLTC codes that support nontrivial transversal logical multi-controlled-Z gates for any required gate depth.

**Method.** The method combines advanced algebraic topology (covering space method, cup product pairing on cubical complexes) with coding theory techniques to establish the necessary non-zero cohomology invariants.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum error-correcting codes, specifically quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes and quantum locally testable codes (qLTCs). These codes are analyzed using algebraic structures derived from decorated Cayley cubical complexes.

**Key observables.** Nontrivial transversal logical multi-controlled-Z gates ($	ext{Cr}^{-1}	ext{Z}$), code parameters like constant rate, linear distance, and constant soundness.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The construction requires the existence of non-zero cup product pairings, which are proven by analyzing polynomial nonvanishing via bipartite multigraph specializations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work builds upon previous constructions by applying a specific gate framework to recent good qLTC constructions, addressing compatibility issues arising from arithmetic identifications.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the necessary algebraic machinery (covering space method, cup product pairing) to guarantee the existence of non-zero invariants. It then applies this machinery to the specific structures of qLDPC and qLTCs to construct the required transversal gates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We achieve nontrivial transversal logical multi-controlled-$Z$ gates with asymptotically optimal parameters simultaneously on quantum low-density parity check codes and quantum locally testable codes, by applying the gate framework of [arXiv:2604.01874] to the recent good qLTC construction of [arXiv:2609.20780]. To this end, we use the covering space method to construct a nonzero cup product pairing on a finite arithmetic cubical complex. This differs from the previous construction of almost-good codes, whose base space is a hypergraph product. We express the pairing as a coefficient in a product of Moore determinants and prove polynomial nonvanishing by a bipartite multigraph specialization. We then construct covering spaces to obtain an asymptotic family of good qLTCs on which the pulled-back pairing induces the desired nontrivial transversal logical action.

</details>

**Summary.** This work compares three methods—full KBE, GKBA, and second-order AE—for simulating time-dependent quantum transport in spintronic junctions. The second-order AE proves to be a highly accurate and computationally efficient alternative to the full KBE for describing spin pumping and STT. However, the authors note that this approximation fails to capture the fast oscillations associated with magnetic inertia.

**Why it may be interesting.** The comparison of approximations (GKBA vs. AE) for non-equilibrium dynamics in coupled quantum-classical systems is highly relevant for developing accurate, yet computationally tractable, models for open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks different theoretical approximations for calculating time-dependent quantum transport in spintronic junctions, specifically comparing the Generalized Kadanoff-Baym ansatz (GKBA) against the second-order Adiabatic Expansion (AE).

**Main result.** The second-order AE accurately describes spin pumping and Spin-Transfer Torque (STT) effects, suggesting a nonadiabatic nature, while the GKBA fails to capture these dynamics. However, AE is limited in describing fast magnetic inertia oscillations.

**Method.** The study uses the Nonequilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) formalism, comparing the full Kadanoff-Baym equations (KBE) against the GKBA and the computationally cheaper second-order AE.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-terminal junction where quantum electrons interact with classical Localized Magnetic Moments (LMMs) in the central region, attached to semi-infinite normal metal leads.

**Key observables.** Spin current pumping, Spin-Transfer Torque (STT) vectors, and LMM dynamics governed by the Landau–Lifshitz-Gilbert (LLG) equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time-dependent coupling strengths, the order of the adiabatic expansion (second-order), and the inclusion of magnetic inertia terms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The GKBA neglects certain memory effects, and the AE assumes the separation of fast quantum and slow classical degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the computational scaling of KBE vs. GKBA. Figure 2 schematically shows three 1D toy models demonstrating spin pumping, voltage-driven STT, and magnetic inertia.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical setup and the computational challenges of KBE, then proceeds to compare the accuracy and efficiency of GKBA and second-order AE against numerically exact benchmarks for key spintronic phenomena.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The generalized Kadanoff-Baym ansatz (GKBA) [P. Lipavský {\em et al.}, Phys. Rev. B {\bf 34}, 6933 (1986)] has emerged as a popular and numerically efficient algorithm for simplification of nonequilibrium Green's function (NEGF)-based calculations of time-dependent quantum transport. For systems that can be split into classical and quantum degrees of freedom, another popular simplifying strategy is adiabatic expansion (AE) of NEGF [N. Bode {\em et al.}, Phys. Rev. Lett. {\bf 107}, 036804 (2011); S. Deghi {\em et al.}, Phys. Rev. B {\bf 110}, 115409 (2024)] in terms of the velocity of classical degrees of freedom, such as localized magnetic moments (LMMs) in spintronics or coordinates of nuclei in nanoelectronics. Here we compare GKBA and second-order AE with numerically exact benchmarks for two-terminal junctions whose central region hosting quantum electrons and classical LMMs is attached to two semi-infinite normal metal leads. Three simple models are employed to exhibit cornerstone time-dependent effects in spintronics---spin pumping and spin-transfer torque (STT), as well as magnetic inertia as a recently explored phenomenon. We find that GKBA fails to describe pumping of spin current by precessing LMMs, or STT vectors, and thereby induced LMM dynamics. Conversely, the second-order AE matches numerically exact benchmarks for both effects remarkably well, thereby also revealing the essentially {\em nonadiabatic} nature of spin pumping. Thus, AE opens a path toward an accurate description of STT-driven magnetization dynamics, including combination with first-principles Hamiltonians, while incurring a fraction of the cost of time evolution of full NEGF. However, despite including terms up to the second time derivatives of LMMs into AE, this approach fails to capture fast nutational oscillations on top of the precessional motion of LMMs as the hallmark of magnetic inertia.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to detect single microwave photons using Josephson junctions by analyzing changes in their switching time statistics. It models the junction dynamics using the RCSJ equation and calculates escape rates via the Kramers approximation. The detection performance is quantified using statistical measures like the KC index, providing a pathway to estimate quantum efficiency at finite temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics, as it applies non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (Kramers escape rates) to characterize the detection of single quanta (photons) using a superconducting circuit element.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates an optimal statistical scheme for detecting the presence of single microwave photons using the switching time distribution of a Josephson junction.

**Main result.** Detection efficiency can be quantified by analyzing the unbalance in switching events, leading to a relationship between photon field properties, quantum efficiency, and junction switching features at finite temperatures.

**Method.** The analysis compares the switching probabilities of the Josephson junction under two conditions: exposed to a periodic train of current pulses (simulating photons) versus no pulses, utilizing statistical tests like the Kumar-Caroll index.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Josephson Junction modeled by the RCSJ equation, whose dynamics are analyzed via a Langevin equation. The switching process is treated as a noise-induced escape from a metastable washboard potential, governed by the Kramers approximation.

**Key observables.** Switching time distribution, the Kumar-Caroll (KC) index ($d_{KC}$), and the ratio of switching rates ($r_1/r_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias current ($I_b$), noise intensity ($D$), observation time ($P$), and the photon arrival rate ($r_A$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes ideal cavity/transmission line, periodic photon source, and utilizes the Kramers model for escape rates; statistical analysis often relies on sample mean approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electrical model (RCSJ), the pulse train mimicking photon absorption, and the tilted washboard potential showing the trapping and switching dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model (RCSJ, Langevin equation) to deriving the escape rates (Kramers theory). It then develops statistical diagnostics (KC index, dichotomous classification) to quantify detection performance by comparing noise-only vs. signal+noise switching statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate an optimal scheme for the detection of single microwave photons by a Josephson junction through the analysis of its switching times distribution. The proposed analysis is of support for the decision about the existence of the photon field, which is important in the case of rare events. We assume that the cavity and the transmission line are ideal (each photon absorbed to the cavity gives a current pulse as the output of the transmission line) and the photon source is periodic. The employed methodology consists in comparing the switching probabilities of a Josephson junction exposed to a train of current pulses, simulating a weak photon field, with that of the same device in absence of pulses. In both cases, thermal noise can induce thermal activated switchings. The investigation of the unbalance in the number of switching events in the two cases, gives an estimate of the efficiency of the detection. Furthermore, in the assumption of escapes described by Kramers model, it is possible to provide a relationship between the properties of the photons field, the quantum efficiency of the detection process, and the Josephson junctions switching features at finite temperatures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically examines how electron-electron and electron-phonon interactions modify charge and spin transport in a quantum ring junction. By employing advanced Green's function techniques, the authors predict unique bias-driven circular currents and demonstrate the potential for highly spin-polarized transport in these interacting nanoscale systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The combination of electron-phonon coupling and strong correlations (Hubbard model) in a mesoscopic ring geometry is highly relevant for understanding energy dissipation and spin-charge conversion in nanoscale electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates bias-driven circular charge and spin currents in a quantum ring, specifically analyzing the influence of electron-electron (e-e) and electron-phonon (e-ph) interactions.

**Main result.** The interplay between e-e and e-ph interactions leads to rich transport physics, enabling selective spin transport and generating novel circular current features not previously reported.

**Method.** The theoretical framework employs the non-equilibrium Green's function formalism, utilizing the Lang-Firsov transformation followed by the Hartree-Fock mean-field approximation.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional quantum ring coupled to source and drain electrodes, modeled using a tight-binding framework incorporating the Hubbard-Holstein model Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Bias-driven circular currents ($I_{cir}$), junction charge and spin transport currents ($I_{ch}^{tr}, I_{sp}^{tr}$), and the spin polarization coefficient.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron-electron interaction strength ($u$), e-ph coupling ($g$), phonon energy ($\hbar\omega_0$), and applied bias voltage ($V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Lang-Firsov transformation and the Hartree-Fock mean-field approximation, assuming zero-temperature conditions and neglecting acoustic phonon contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the circular current density ($J_{\sigma}^{cir}$) dependence on energy for varying interaction strengths and the total spin circular currents ($I_{\sigma}^{cir}$) as a function of bias voltage.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the full Hamiltonian, then applies the Lang-Firsov transformation and zero-phonon averaging to derive effective Hamiltonians for both the ring and the coupling. Subsequently, it uses the NEGF formalism within the HF approximation to calculate the various current components.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The phenomenon of bias-driven circular charge and spin currents in a ring nanojunction is investigated in the presence of electron-electron (e-e) and electron-phonon (e-ph) interactions within a tight-binding framework based on the non-equilibrium Green's function formalism. The Lang-Firsov transformation is employed to map the interacting system onto an effective electronic model, which is subsequently treated within the Hartree-Fock mean-field scheme. By exploring the interplay among e-e interaction, e-ph coupling, and electrode-ring interface sensitivity, several intriguing features emerge in both circular charge and spin currents that, to the best of our knowledge, have not been reported previously. In addition to bias-driven circular currents, charge and spin-dependent junction currents through the nanojunction are also analyzed. Selective spin transport is achieved, leading to a high degree of spin polarization. All four current components, two associated with circular currents and two with transport currents, are systematically inspected over a wide range of parameter regimes to assess the sensitivity of the results to the relevant system parameters. Our findings provide useful insights into charge and spin transport phenomena in interacting nanojunctions with single- and multi-loop geometries.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes the coherence scaling of Majorana qubits built on quantum dot chains by examining Rabi and Ramsey oscillations for varying chain lengths. The authors find that while longer chains might improve quantum memory, the coherence time for qubit manipulation is not universally enhanced by increasing the number of dots due to complex disorder-induced leakage channels. The results highlight that coherence performance is critically dependent on precise parameter tuning rather than just system size.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the fundamental challenge of scaling quantum hardware: demonstrating that increasing physical size (more dots) translates into improved coherence for practical quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the coherence time (Rabi and Ramsey) of Majorana-based qubits scales as the number of quantum dots ($N$) increases in artificial Kitaev chains.

**Main result.** Increasing the chain length ($N$) does not guarantee better qubit performance; while longer chains might be better for quantum memory, the coherence enhancement is highly sensitive to disorder and specific manipulation protocols.

**Method.** The authors employ theoretical modeling, deriving effective Hamiltonians and calculating coherence decay rates by analyzing leakage channels in both the pristine and disordered limits.

**Model / system.** The system is a double $N$-site quantum-dot artificial Kitaev chain, designed to host Majorana zero modes. The physics is governed by a generalized Hamiltonian incorporating site energies, tunneling terms, and disorder.

**Key observables.** Rabi coherence time ($T_{2,	ext{Rabi}}$) and Ramsey coherence time ($T_{2,	ext{Ramsey}}$) oscillations, and the associated leakage probabilities ($P_{	ext{leak}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain length ($N$), superconducting gaps, detuning ($\epsilon$), and the strength of disorder fluctuations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares results for pristine limits versus realistic disorder, and the derivation of dephasing envelopes relies on Gaussian disorder assumptions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Rabi and Ramsey oscillations for $N=2$ and $N=3$ qubits in the pristine limit, comparing numerical and analytical results, and showing leakage probabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and Hamiltonian, deriving the effective $2	imes 2$ block Hamiltonians for parity sectors, calculating the decay mechanisms for Rabi and Ramsey sequences, and finally analyzing the scaling effects of disorder.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theoretical study of the Rabi and Ramsey coherence time of Majorana-based qubits formed by double $N$-site quantum-dot artificial Kitaev chains, following the recent experimental realization arXiv:2607.09511. We generalize the qubit to two chains of arbitrary length and compare the Rabi and Ramsey oscillations of the four-dot and six-dot qubits, simulated with realistic disorder for three sets of parameters: small and large superconducting gaps, and the experimental parameters. We find that, in the pristine limit, the Rabi oscillation is independent of the chain length, whereas the Ramsey oscillation of the six-dot qubit suffers from larger leakage, because the third site suppresses the unwanted energy splitting away from the sweet spot but at the same time makes the deliberate splitting difficult to achieve, which requires a larger detuning that could excite the bulk. In reality with disorder, neither coherence time improves universally with the chain length. The Rabi coherence is set by the competition of three dephasing channels, and the additional dots pay off only if the fluctuation of the interchain tunneling is suppressed; the Ramsey coherence is set by the fluctuation of the energy splitting during the $σ_z$ rotation, which for the four-dot qubit is fixed by the sweet-spot disorder alone and does not depend on the mean splitting, whereas for the six-dot qubit the detuning that generates the splitting adds a fluctuation growing with the splitting itself, so the additional dots pay off only for small splittings. This implies that a longer chain is thus a better quantum memory but not necessarily a better qubit under manipulation. Although topological protection is expected in a longer Kitaev chain qubit, observing it in the current Rabi and Ramsey measurement protocols remains an experimental challenge.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the complex dynamics of a mass-conserving two-species reaction-diffusion model, showing how pattern formation is governed by specific instabilities like the Cahn-Hilliard and Allen-Cahn-Hopf types. Through numerical simulation and stability analysis, the authors demonstrate that even simple systems can exhibit rich spatiotemporal chaos due to nonlinear interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical reaction-diffusion dynamics, the study of pattern formation, symmetry breaking, and the transition to chaos in non-equilibrium systems is highly relevant to understanding complex dynamics in many-body quantum systems or open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the complex routes to chaos and pattern formation in a mass-conserving two-species reaction-diffusion system.

**Main result.** The system can exhibit rich spatiotemporal behavior, including period-doubling cascades and low-dimensional spatiotemporal chaos, even when primary bifurcations are restricted to the Cahn-Hilliard type.

**Method.** The analysis combines linear stability analysis (identifying Cahn-Hilliard and Allen-Cahn-Hopf instabilities) with numerical continuation and direct time simulations to map out bifurcation structures.

**Model / system.** The model is a mass-conserving reaction-diffusion system for two species, governed by coupled partial differential equations with a specific reaction term $f(u, v)$. Mass conservation ($\partial ho / \partial t = 
abla^2 \eta$) is a defining feature.

**Key observables.** Order parameter $M(t)$, Normalized Peak Asymmetry ($\Phi$), Normalized Flip Ratio ($\mathcal{F}$), and the identification of different instability types (CH, ACH).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffusion constants ($d_u, d_v$), reaction constants ($a_1, a_2, \alpha$), and the total mean density $ar{ho}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the case where only a Cahn-Hilliard instability can occur (all primary bifurcations are stationary), and the system is restricted to two species with one conservation law.

**Figures summary.** Stability diagrams map out the conditions for instability based on system invariants, showing regions for Cahn-Hilliard and Allen-Cahn-Hopf instabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper revisits the system by first analyzing linear stability to identify potential instabilities (CH, ACH). It then investigates the nonlinear dynamics, focusing on the case where only CH instabilities occur, leading to the study of secondary bifurcations and the emergence of chaos.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Mass-conserving reaction-diffusion systems with two species correspond to a seemingly simple case where pattern formation occurs under the influence of a conservation law. Here, we first revisit their linear stability behavior and point out that generically two instabilities can occur: a stationary large-scale mass-conserving (Cahn-Hilliard) instability and an instability that combines features of a stationary large-scale non-mass-conserving (Allen-Cahn) instability and a oscillatory large-scale mass-conserving (conserved-Hopf) instability. We term it an Allen-Cahn-Hopf instability. Second, we investigate the nonlinear dynamics for a specific model related to the formation of cell polarization where only a Cahn-Hilliard instability can occur, i.e., all primary bifurcations are stationary. We analyze how secondary and further bifurcations subsequently give rise to various oscillatory states. The emerging rich spectrum of spatiotemporal behavior includes several period-doubling cascades related to different forms of spatial and temporal symmetry breaking. Beside regular states, three types of low-dimensional spatiotemporal chaos occur and involve transitions like fusion and an outer crises. Our results demonstrate the importance of nonlinear interactions in the dynamics of mass-conserving reaction-diffusion systems, and show that even a simple two-species system with primary bifurcations of Cahn-Hilliard type can show complex spatiotemporal behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates whether electron-electron correlations are the primary cause of the Chirality Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect in molecular junctions. Using the NEGF method with Hubbard atoms, the authors found that while correlations are important, they alone cannot account for the large spin polarization factors seen in experiments. The results suggest that other mechanisms, beyond simple correlation effects, must be at play.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses spin transport in nanoscale systems using advanced many-body techniques (NEGF, SBA), which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and molecular spintronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the specific role of electronic correlation effects in explaining the Chirality Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect in molecular junctions.

**Main result.** The calculations found very little spin polarization, suggesting that while electron-electron interactions are important for spin selectivity, they cannot be solely responsible for the high polarization factors observed experimentally.

**Method.** The Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) approach is employed, treating correlation effects using the self-consistent second Born approximation.

**Model / system.** The physical system modeled is a chain of Hubbard atoms arranged in a helical geometry, sandwiched between two leads, one of which is magnetic.

**Key observables.** Spin polarization (SP) factor, total currents for different spin directions, and the distribution of spin populations on the chain atoms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of the Hubbard constant to the hopping parameter ($U/t$), and the strength of spin-orbit coupling (SOC).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interaction with the leads is treated within the Wide Band Approximation, and correlation effects are systematically included up to the second Born approximation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically applies the NEGF formalism to model spin transport through a chiral molecular junction, comparing results for L and D enantiomers while varying the correlation strength ($U/t$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Non-Equilibrium Green's Function (NEGF) approach is applied to a chain of Hubbard atoms in a helical geometry sandwiched between two leads, one of which is magnetic, to assess what role the electronic correlation effects within the molecule may play in explaining the Chirality Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect. The correlation effects are treated at the level of the self-consistent second Born approximation. Two chiralities, Laevorotatory (L) and Dextrorotatory (D), of the helix, are discussed. Further, we account for spin-orbit coupling (SOC) within the chains using a simplified model of Kane and Mele that exploits next-nearest neighbour interactions intrinsic to a curved geometry. The interaction with the leads is considered within the Wide Band Approximation. We consider the ratio, U/t, of the Hubbard constant U to the hopping t between atomic sites along the chain of up to two, but found very little spin polarisation in our calculations. Even though the spin polarisation factor defined as a relative difference between the largest and smallest total current is found for both enantiomers practically the same, the orientation of the tip's polarisation required to reach the largest and smallest currents for the two enantiomers differed. It is also found that the qualitative agreement with observations can only be achieved if we account, self-consistently, for the second order of the perturbation theory. The distribution of the spin populations on the chain atoms for different U/t ratios is also discussed. This study concludes that even though accounting for electron-electron interactions are important in understanding spin selectivity within molecular junctions, they cannot be made solely responsible for the high spin polarisation factors observed in experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an AI-driven framework that automates the entire process of quantum transport measurements on 2D semiconductors like $	ext{MoS}_2$. By autonomously planning, executing, and analyzing multi-day experiments, the agent successfully characterized fundamental properties, such as spin-orbit coupling and layer-resolved phase diagrams, demonstrating a significant step toward self-driving laboratories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the physics is condensed matter, the core contribution is the methodology—using advanced AI agents to automate complex, multi-day experimental procedures, which is a major development in experimental physics infrastructure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need to automate complex, multi-step, multi-day quantum transport measurements in 2D semiconductors to accelerate scientific discovery.

**Main result.** The AI agent successfully performed end-to-end measurements, determining the conduction-band spin-orbit coupling energy in monolayer $	ext{MoS}_2$ and mapping the layer- and valley-resolved phase diagram in bilayer $	ext{MoS}_2$.

**Method.** An AI-driven, hypothesis-guided workflow implemented via the FermiLink agent harness, which autonomously plans, executes, analyzes, and reports on cryogenic quantum transport measurements.

**Model / system.** The experiments are conducted on two-dimensional (2D) semiconductors, specifically $	ext{MoS}_2$ (monolayer and bilayer), utilizing a dual-gated Hall bar device structure encapsulated in hBN.

**Key observables.** Conduction-band spin–orbit coupling (SOC) energy, layer- and valley-resolved phase diagrams, quantum oscillation frequencies derived from FFT analysis, and Hall resistance ($R_{xx}, R_{xy}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltages ($V_{	ext{g}}$), magnetic fields ($B$), and carrier densities ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on the stability of the AI agent harness and requires human experts to define robust 'Agent Skills' through iterative dry runs for new device types.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the multi-step workflow, device setups, and results showing Landau fan measurements, $R_{xx}$ vs. gate voltage maps, and the final phase diagrams detailing layer and valley occupations.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the architecture of the AI workflow (planning $ightarrow$ measurement $ightarrow$ analysis $ightarrow$ reporting), applies this workflow to $	ext{MoS}_2$ to extract SOC energy, and then extends it to map the complex phase diagram of bilayer $	ext{MoS}_2$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial-intelligence (AI) agents are beginning to enter experimental laboratories, automating experiments and accelerating scientific discovery. Herein, we introduce an AI-driven workflow in which an AI agent performs multi-step, multi-day quantum transport measurements end-to-end. Specifically, given brief instructions, the agent starts by planning the multi-step measurements, then safely operates the cryogenic instruments, analyzes the data, and concludes with a final report. We demonstrate this AI workflow on multiple monolayer and bilayer MoS2 devices. Through autonomous measurement campaigns lasting up to six days, the agent determined the conduction-band spin-orbit coupling energy in monolayer MoS2, and mapped a layer- and valley-resolved phase diagram in bilayer MoS2. This experimental workflow is implemented through the FermiLink agent harness, which emphasizes instrumental safety and the reliability of the measurement and analysis. The framework is general and can be readily adapted to other types of experiments, representing a step toward self-driving laboratories.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that in AuPt superlattices, absorbed optical energy rapidly localizes into Pt due to its high electronic heat capacity, creating an interface stress imbalance. By using ultrafast X-ray diffraction, the authors show that the resulting coherent phonon oscillations are identical regardless of whether the initial energy deposition was homogeneous or selectively absorbed in Pt. This reveals a mechanism for femtosecond control over nanoscale energy distribution.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of energy self-restructuring on ultrafast timescales, driven by material properties like electronic heat capacity, provides a model for non-equilibrium energy transport relevant to open quantum systems and energy dissipation in nanostructures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is controlling the spatial distribution of absorbed optical energy in nanoscale materials for applications like plasmonics and photothermal chemistry.

**Main result.** The energy localization dynamics are governed by ultrafast dissipative equilibration, leading to a final energy landscape independent of the initial optical absorption profile.

**Method.** The study combines ultrafast X-ray Diffraction (UXRD) experiments with detailed numerical simulations using a diffusive two-temperature model (d2TM) and linear elastic wave equations.

**Model / system.** The system is a metallic AuPt superlattice (SL) composed of alternating Au and Pt layers. The dynamics are modeled by considering electronic heat capacity, electron-phonon coupling, and resulting stress imbalances.

**Key observables.** Coherent 570 GHz superlattice phonon amplitude, transient strain ($\eta(t)$), and the comparison of dynamics from 400 nm (homogeneous) vs. 800 nm (Pt-selective) excitation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrafast timescales (femtoseconds to picoseconds), the large electronic heat capacity of Pt, and the superlattice periodicity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to calibrate for equal total deposited energy between different excitation wavelengths and that UXRD probes total local energy density.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy flow visualization comparing 400 nm and 800 nm excitation, and show that the measured transient strain and SL phonon dynamics are nearly identical for both excitation sources.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for energy control, presents the AuPt SL system, details the UXRD measurements comparing different excitation wavelengths, and uses simulations to show that the final phonon response is dictated by ultrafast dissipative equilibration rather than initial absorption.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling the spatial distribution of absorbed optical energy is central to nanoscale photothermal chemistry, plasmonics, and ultrafast materials control. Here, we show that a metallic AuPt superlattice concentrates electronic energy in Pt within a few hundred femtoseconds, regardless of the initial energy distribution between the two constituents. Ultrafast X-ray diffraction follows this energy redistribution through the amplitude of a coherent 570 GHz superlattice phonon driven by the stress imbalance at the AuPt interfaces. Despite the nearly homogeneous absorption at 400 nm, the observed lattice motion is identical to that produced by 800 nm excitation, which is absorbed predominantly in Pt. This dissipation driven localization of energy arises from the large electronic heat capacity of Pt and rapid electronic transport through the superlattice, providing a route to femtosecond control of nanoscale energy distributions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work successfully identifies the Magnetic Spin Hall Effect (MSHE) in conventional ferromagnets using a lateral spin-valve geometry. By measuring the spin signal's dependence on magnetization reversal, the authors confirmed the MSHE's unique time-reversal-symmetry-odd character. This establishes the MSHE as a sizable, magnetically switchable spin-charge conversion mechanism comparable to established heavy metal effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the ability to electrically isolate and quantify a symmetry-protected spin-charge conversion mechanism (MSHE) is highly relevant to understanding spin-orbit torque physics and spintronics, which often interfaces with quantum control concepts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to experimentally identify the Magnetic Spin Hall Effect (MSHE) in conventional ferromagnets, as its detection had been previously elusive.

**Main result.** The MSHE was unequivocally identified by observing that the associated spin signal reverses sign upon magnetization reversal, confirming its time-reversal-symmetry-odd character. The measured magnetic spin Hall angle was found to be $(3.8 \pm 0.6)\%$.

**Method.** The study utilized a non-local lateral spin-valve (LSV) geometry to electrically measure spin signals, comparing the response to magnetization reversal against conventional spin Hall effects.

**Model / system.** The experiment uses perpendicularly magnetized (PMA) Co-based multilayer thin films integrated into a lateral spin-valve structure, allowing for precise control and measurement of spin transport phenomena.

**Key observables.** Non-local resistance ($\Delta R_{NL}$), MSHE/MISHE signals, and the magnetic spin Hall angle ($	heta_{MSH}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic spin Hall angle $	heta_{\mathrm{MSH}} = (3.8 \pm 0.6)\%$; spin diffusion length $\lambda_{PMA}^{\perp} = 0.31 \pm 0.06$ nm.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of LSV architectures to isolate the effect and relies on theoretical models to decouple the TRS-odd (MSHE) from the TRS-even (SHE) components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics comparing SHE and MSHE mechanisms, and show non-local spin signal measurements ($\Delta R_{NL}$) demonstrating the sign reversal characteristic of the MSHE upon magnetization reversal.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical difference between TRS-even (SHE) and TRS-odd (MSHE) components, details the fabrication and measurement setup using LSV, and presents the key results showing the distinct magnetic switching behavior of the MSHE signal.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The conventional spin Hall effect generates spin currents whose flow direction, spin polarization, and driving electric field are mutually perpendicular. Magnetic order lifts this symmetry restriction and enables additional time-reversal-symmetry-odd components of the spin-conductivity tensor, giving rise to the magnetic spin Hall effect (MSHE). These components also couple the generated spin polarization to the magnetic order, providing a degree of control absent in the conventional spin Hall effect. Although the MSHE has been observed in antiferromagnets, its experimental identification in conventional ferromagnets has remained elusive. Here, using a non-local lateral spin-valve geometry, we electrically identify the MSHE and its reciprocal effect in a perpendicularly magnetized Co-based multilayer. Reversal of the multilayer magnetization reverses the MSHE and magnetic inverse spin Hall signals, revealing their time-reversal-symmetry-odd character and magnetization control. By contrast, the conventional spin Hall and inverse spin Hall signals measured in the same devices remain unchanged under magnetization reversal, consistent with their time-reversal-symmetry-even character. We obtain a magnetic spin Hall angle of $θ_{\mathrm{MSH}} = (3.8 \pm 0.6)\%$, comparable in magnitude to the spin Hall angle of heavy metals commonly used in spintronic devices, such as Pt. These results establish the MSHE as a sizable, magnetically switchable spin-charge interconversion mechanism in conventional ferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates the magnetoelectric conductivity in nodal semimetals, focusing on the difference between dipole-like and monopole-like Berry curvature sources. It demonstrates that systems with dipolar topology, like VNR and HSM, exhibit unique, symmetry-forbidden magnetoelectric responses compared to standard Weyl semimetals. This highlights the importance of the underlying topological structure in determining measurable transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on geometric properties like Berry curvature dipoles and their direct, measurable impact on transport coefficients (magnetoelectric response) provides a concrete link between advanced topological invariants and observable condensed matter phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper computes the linear magnetoelectric conductivity in planar-Hall geometries for topological semimetals, specifically contrasting systems with dipolar versus monopole-like Berry curvature sources.

**Main result.** The dipolar nature of the Berry curvature and Orbital Magnetic Moment (OMM) generates unique, symmetry-forbidden odd powers of the magnetic field in the in-plane conductivity channels, which is absent in systems with monopole-like sources.

**Method.** The calculations employ the semiclassical Boltzmann formalism within the relaxation-time approximation, evaluating contributions from Drude, BC, OMM, anomalous-Hall, and Lorentz-force terms.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes two primary models: the two-band Vortex Nodal-Ring (VNR) semimetal and the three-band Hopf Semimetal (HSM). Comparisons are drawn with Weyl semimetals (monopole sources) and other nodal systems.

**Key observables.** Linear magnetoelectric conductivity ($\sigma_{lf}$), Berry curvature flux (dipolar vs. monopole), and the presence of odd powers of $|B|$ in in-plane response channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cubic order in the magnetic field ($B^3$), and the specific planar-Hall field configurations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed using the semiclassical Boltzmann formalism and assume static, uniform electric and magnetic fields.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the smoke-ring pattern of the vector field $\mathbf{d}(\mathbf{k})$ near a VNR section. Figure 2 compares the BC field near a TSM (monopole-like) versus an HSM (dipolar, zero net flux).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the conductivity in different planar-Hall setups, comparing the VNR and HSM models. It details the vanishing of certain contributions (e.g., internode scattering in HSM) and contrasts the resulting magnetoelectric response with known monopole-like systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compute the linear magnetoelectric conductivity of a two-band vortex nodal-ring (VNR) semimetal and a three-band Hopf semimetal (HSM) within the semiclassical Boltzmann formalism in the relaxation-time approximation. We consider three inequivalent planar-Hall configurations, in which the electric ($\boldsymbol E$) and magnetic ($\boldsymbol B$) fields are oriented differently with respect to the nodal-ring plane and the BC-dipole axis. Unlike Weyl and multifold semimetals, where Berry-curvature (BC) flux originates from monopole-like sources, both systems host dipole-like BC sources and vanishing Chern numbers. Working consistently to cubic order in the magnetic field, we evaluate the Drude, BC, orbital-magnetic-moment (OMM), anomalous-Hall, and Lorentz-force contributions to the conductivity. In both systems, the dipolar BC and OMM generate odd powers of $|\boldsymbol B|$ in in-plane response channels that are symmetry-forbidden in systems with monopole-like BC sources. For the HSM, the internode-scattering contribution to the conductivity vanishes identically. By comparing the VNR with a $\mathcal{PT}$-broken gapped nodal ring and the HSM with a pseudospin-1 triple-point semimetal, we demonstrate that the dipolar nature of the BC gives rise to distinctive features in magnetoelectric response that are absent in semimetals with monopole-like BC flux.

</details>

### [Nonlinear Spin Polarization Enables Néel Order Switching in Centrosymmetric Altermagnets](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26709v1)

**Authors:** Sunit Das, Sayan Sarkar, Amit Agarwal

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework to electrically switch the Néel order in centrosymmetric altermagnets, overcoming the symmetry-imposed prohibition on linear current effects. By analyzing nonlinear spin polarization, the authors show that the combination of a staggered response and a SOC-induced transverse component generates the necessary torque for deterministic switching. This establishes a novel nonlinear write-read mechanism for these materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter magnetism, the concept of using non-linear, symmetry-protected responses (like the second-order polarization) to control quantum states (magnetic order) is highly relevant to open quantum systems and non-equilibrium dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is achieving deterministic electrical switching of the Néel order in centrosymmetric altermagnets, a process hindered because linear current-induced spin polarization is forbidden by inversion symmetry.

**Main result.** The authors establish a nonlinear electrical write-read scheme by showing that the interplay between the nonrelativistic staggered spin response and a SOC-induced uniform transverse component enables deterministic $180^\circ$ reversal of the Néel order.

**Method.** The study combines symmetry analysis using spin group theory to constrain nonlinear spin polarization, quantum kinetic calculations (density matrix evolution), and macro-spin simulations using the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation.

**Model / system.** The research focuses on centrosymmetric altermagnets, using a minimal tight-binding model for $	ext{FeSb}_2$ as a concrete example. The physics is governed by the interplay between nonrelativistic spin-split bands and finite spin-orbit coupling (SOC).

**Key observables.** Néel vector reversal ($180^\circ$ switching), anomalous Hall response (for state readout), and the coexistence/interaction of uniform and staggered nonlinear spin polarization components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mechanism is critically dependent on the presence of finite SOC, which generates the necessary transverse component, and the electric field orientation, which controls the switching direction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a perturbative expansion in the weak-field regime and assumes the validity of the minimal model for the altermagnet $	ext{FeSb}_2$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the mechanism: the nonrelativistic limit yields a torque-inactive, collinear response, while finite SOC introduces a transverse component, leading to a non-zero torque capable of driving reversal.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the symmetry constraints on nonlinear spin polarization in the nonrelativistic limit. It then incorporates finite SOC to generate a transverse component. Finally, macro-spin simulations demonstrate how the cooperation between the staggered and transverse components enables deterministic switching, forming a write-read scheme.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Deterministic electrical switching of the Néel order remains challenging in centrosymmetric altermagnets, where linear current-induced spin polarization is forbidden by inversion symmetry. Here, we establish a nonlinear route to Néel order control. Spin group symmetry shows that, in the nonrelativistic limit, the nonlinear spin polarization is constrained along the Néel vector, with a staggered response allowed in $8$ of the $10$ nontrivial spin-Laue groups. Although this Néel spin polarization is torque-inactive by itself, finite spin-orbit coupling can generate an additional uniform component transverse to the Néel vector. Using a minimal model for the $d$-wave altermagnet FeSb$_2$, we demonstrate the coexistence of the nonrelativistic staggered response with an SOC-induced uniform transverse component. Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation-based macro-spin simulations reveal that the transverse component alone produces oscillatory dynamics, whereas its cooperative action with the staggered response enables deterministic $180^\circ$ reversal. We further show that changing the electric field orientation reverses the staggered spin response and selects the switching direction, while the opposite Néel states can be distinguished by their anomalous Hall response. Our results establish a nonlinear electrical write-read scheme for centrosymmetric altermagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates using voltage control to manipulate magnetism in SrTiO3/Fe heterostructures for next-generation cryogenic electronics. By demonstrating that an electric field can modify the magnetic domain structure, the authors propose a highly energy-efficient alternative to conventional magnetic writing methods. This research establishes a viable material platform for integrating magnetic functionality into low-power quantum and classical computing circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state spintronics, the underlying mechanism—controlling magnetic order via an electric field coupled to a ferroelectric/dielectric interface—is highly relevant to understanding emergent quantum phenomena in engineered materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop low-power, scalable magnetic memory elements for cryogenic electronics by replacing high-power current-based writing with voltage-controlled magnetization switching.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated and quantified the Voltage Control of Magnetism (VCM) in epitaxial SrTiO3/Fe heterostructures, showing that back-gate voltage can modify the magnetic domain structure.

**Method.** The study combines experimental characterization (MOKE, SQUID-VSM, MFM) with theoretical modeling (DFT, micromagnetic simulations) to analyze the electric field's influence on magnetic anisotropy.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an epitaxial SrTiO3/Fe thin film heterostructure grown on a CMOS-compatible Si substrate, leveraging the ferroelectric/dielectric properties of SrTiO3.

**Key observables.** Magnetization hysteresis loops (MOKE), domain structure visualization (MFM), effective magnetic anisotropy ($K_{	ext{eff}}$), and the VCM coefficient ($\xi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperatures, ultrathin Fe film thickness (near the Spin Reorientation Transition, e.g., 0.7 nm), and applied back-gate voltage (V).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear approximations for the VCM coefficient ($\xi$) and assumes structural continuity and interfacial quality are maintained during growth.

**Figures summary.** Figures show sample stacks, $M(H)$ loops varying with thickness and voltage, domain structure images (MFM) showing voltage-dependent domain changes, and theoretical plots relating anisotropy to thickness.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for low-power cryogenic spintronics to selecting the SrTiO3/Fe system, characterizing the magnetic anisotropy dependence on thickness, and finally demonstrating the voltage-tunable magnetic state using multiple characterization techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cryogenic electronics forms a rapidly emerging research domain for high-performance and power-efficient computing applications. Incorporating nanomagnetic components in cryogenic circuitry adds highly valuable functionality, facilitating downscaling, reducing energy consumption and introducing time-reversal symmetry breaking. Furthermore, low-temperature environments enhance magnetic stability and switching efficiency at nanoscale dimensions, reinforcing the potential of cryogenic nanomagnets. To fully leverage these opportunities, magnetic control schemes require alternative options to current-based writing, which is the main bottleneck regarding power consumption and downscaling. In this regard, voltage-based gating of the magnetic state could drastically enhance operational efficiency and integration density. In this work, we investigate cryogenic Voltage Control of Magnetism (VCM) in epitaxial SrTiO$_3$/Fe thin film heterostructures on a CMOS compatible Si substrate. We demonstrate and quantify voltage-controlled modifications of the magnetic domain structure, consistent with electric field-controlled magnetic anisotropy at the Fe/SrTiO$_3$ interface. These findings provide a viable material system for the development of next-generation magnetic domain-based devices for classical and quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes entanglement entropy by studying the eigenvalues of correlation matrices for 1D quantum models like free fermions and the Ising chain. By approximating the correlation matrices as circulant, the authors show that the bulk eigenvalue scaling directly explains the expected logarithmic divergence of entanglement entropy at quantum critical points. This provides a spectral perspective on a key result from Conformal Field Theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between the spectral properties (eigenvalue scaling) and the universal thermodynamic quantity (entanglement entropy) provides a powerful, spectral tool for analyzing critical phenomena in 1D quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the scaling behavior of eigenvalues of correlation matrices to derive a spectral understanding of entanglement entropy in quantum systems.

**Main result.** The bulk of the eigenspectrum scales as $1/L_A$ for critical systems, which successfully explains the robust $\log L_A$ scaling of entanglement entropy predicted by Conformal Field Theory.

**Method.** The authors employ a circulant matrix approximation to analyze the Toeplitz correlation matrices in the large dimension limit, allowing for analytical calculation of eigenvalue distributions.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes entanglement in one-dimensional quantum systems, specifically free lattice fermions (tight-binding model) and the transverse field Ising chain. The entanglement is quantified via the reduced density matrix derived from the correlation matrix.

**Key observables.** Entanglement Entropy ($S_{vN}$), eigenvalues ($\{p_i\}$) of the correlation matrix, and the correlation matrix itself.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Subsystem size ($L_A$), fermion density ($n_f$), and the magnetic field strength ($h$) for the Ising chain, particularly the critical point ($h=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is replacing the Toeplitz correlation matrix with an asymptotically equivalent circulant matrix to facilitate analytical eigenvalue analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of entanglement entropy ($\sim \log L_A$) for both free fermions and the Ising chain at criticality, comparing exact results with the circulant approximation. Other figures show eigenvalue distributions for different system parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the connection between entanglement entropy and the eigenvalues of the correlation matrix. It then develops the circulant approximation for both free fermions and the Ising chain. Finally, it analyzes the scaling behavior in the critical and non-critical regimes, demonstrating the $\log L_A$ dependence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the scaling behavior of the eigenvalues of correlation matrices, which characterize the entanglement of a subsystem with its complement part of a total pure state. A most distinguishing feature of entanglement entropy is its logarithmic dependence on the subsystem size for the groundstate of one-dimensional critical systems. Despite its robust universal character and relevance to a wide range of topics, a thorough understanding of this result requires sophisticated mathematical physics techniques or conformal field theory. The aim of our work is to shed light on this from the underlying eigenvalue distribution perspective. The central object is the correlation matrix, which takes the form of Toeplitz or block-Toeplitz matrix. We develop a circulant matrix approximation in the large matrix dimension limit, thus allowing for the individual eigenvalues behavior to be analysed analytically. We find that for both free lattice fermions and transverse field Ising chain, eigenvalues in the bulk of the eigenspectrum scales as $1/L_A$ with the subsystem size. Together with the extensivity of the entropy function, it explains the robust $\log_2 L_A$ scaling of entanglement at criticality. Perturbing from the entanglement-free limit of the Ising chain, we find a smooth crossover behavior to `non-critical' scaling that is characterized by a very slow logarithmic dependence rendering it seemingly a constant entanglement value expected of non-critical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a Momentum-Surface Effective Field Theory to handle strong, non-perturbative mixing effects in radiative capture processes. By identifying the breakdown of standard perturbation theory near a finite momentum $k_0$, the authors construct an EFT that systematically restores unitarity. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for describing strongly interacting quantum systems near unitarity limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-perturbative physics near unitarity, a central theme in modern quantum gas research, particularly concerning Feshbach resonances and universal low-energy physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes non-perturbative dynamics in radiative capture processes in non-relativistic quantum systems, where weak underlying interactions can lead to strong mixing effects.

**Main result.** The strong mixing leads to the necessity of a Momentum-Surface Effective Field Theory (EFT), which successfully restores unitarity and provides a systematic expansion for both elastic scattering and radiative capture.

**Method.** The methodology involves systematic power counting, Renormalization Group (RG) evolution, and constructing a Momentum-Surface EFT organized around a finite momentum surface $k_0$.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses a simple quantum-mechanical two-channel toy model involving fermions and a mediating scalar field, describing processes like radiative capture ($\xi + \xi 	o (\xi\eta) + \phi$).

**Key observables.** Radiative capture cross sections ($\sigma_{	ext{cap}}$), scattering amplitudes, and the breakdown of perturbation theory signaled by $\sigma_{	ext{cap}}$ exceeding the unitarity bound.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis focuses on the strong mixing regime characterized by a finite momentum $k_0$, and involves scales like the binding energy ($\Lambda_b$) and the scattering length ($a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The EFT assumes a hierarchy of expansions in kinematic and analytic terms, and the analysis is based on a simplified two-channel toy model.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the potentials and wavefunctions, contrasting the suppressed regime with the enhanced, geometric regime where strong mixing occurs.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the breakdown of perturbation theory in the toy model (Sec. 2), through the calculation of the capture amplitude (Sec. 3), to the systematic resummation using RG flow (Sec. 4), and finally deriving the Momentum-Surface EFT (Sec. 5).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Radiative capture in a non-relativistic system can become non-perturbative even when the underlying transition interaction is weak. We demonstrate this mechanism in a simple quantum-mechanical two-channel model with a systematic power counting, where strong mixing between scattering and bound states develops in a narrow region around a finite momentum $k_0$, leading to a perturbative violation of unitarity. In this regime, the enhanced contributions factorize, reducing the dynamics to a free scattering state interacting through a strong local coupling. Under RG evolution in the factorization scale, this local interaction approaches a unitary fixed point, analogous to that of systems with an anomalously large scattering length. This motivates an EFT organized around the finite-momentum surface $|\mathbf{k}|=k_0$, describing fermions at unitarity with a linear dispersion relation. The resummation resulting from the EFT restores unitarity and provides a systematic expansion for both elastic scattering and radiative capture.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically models superconducting spin-valve heterostructures to understand how magnetic ordering induces spontaneous currents via electromagnetic proximity effects. By self-consistently solving the BdG and Maxwell equations, the authors show that these effects generate long-range magnetic fields and modify exotic odd-frequency triplet pairing. This provides a detailed, microscopic understanding of superconducting spintronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The coupling of superconducting pairing symmetries (triplet correlations) with classical electromagnetism (Maxwell equations) provides a rich platform for studying non-equilibrium quantum dynamics and emergent macroscopic currents.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the complex interplay between electromagnetic proximity effects and conventional superconducting proximity effects when ferromagnets are placed in contact with superconductors.

**Main result.** The study predicts that spontaneous currents can emerge in these heterostructures, and the resulting electromagnetic response extends across the entire superconducting layer, unlike simpler proximity models.

**Method.** The research employs a self-consistent numerical solution of the coupled Bogoliubov-de Gennes and Maxwell equations, allowing for a microscopic description.

**Model / system.** The physical system studied is clean ferromagnet/ferromagnet/superconductor spin-valve heterostructures, allowing the relative angle between the two ferromagnetic layers to be tuned.

**Key observables.** Spontaneous currents, oscillatory singlet Cooper-pair amplitudes, long-ranged odd-frequency triplet correlations, and the resulting magnetic field profile.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative angle between the ferromagnetic layers, the strength of the exchange field, and the thickness of the superconducting layer.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes conventional s-wave superconductors and utilizes a semiclassical treatment for electromagnetic quantities.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention a schematic illustration of the F1/F2/S heterostructure showing the magnetization orientation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a microscopic framework by solving coupled BdG and Maxwell equations to analyze how magnetic ordering in F layers generates measurable electromagnetic responses (spontaneous currents) within the S layer.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

When ferromagnets are brought into contact with a superconductor, superconducting proximity effects give rise to a variety of interesting phenomena, including oscillatory singlet Cooper-pair amplitudes and long-ranged odd-frequency triplet correlations induced by the exchange interactions in the ferromagnets. From an electrodynamic perspective, however, it is equally important to understand when and how spontaneous currents can emerge. To investigate the interplay between electromagnetic and conventional superconducting proximity effects, we study clean ferromagnet/ferromagnet/superconductor spin-valve heterostructures in which the relative angle between the two ferromagnetic layers can be tuned. Our approach is based on a self-consistent numerical solution of the coupled Bogoliubov-de Gennes and Maxwell equations, providing a microscopic description capable of resolving physics on atomic length scales. Several notable features emerge. In both the weak- and strong-exchange-field regimes, the central ferromagnetic layer plays a dominant role in generating sizable spontaneous currents when its exchange-field strength is varied. We further find that the resulting electromagnetic response extends across the entire superconducting layer, in sharp contrast to the short-ranged inverse proximity effect. In noncollinear configurations, the electromagnetic proximity effect also reconfigures the local magnetic-field orientation and reduces the angular mismatch between the fields in the two ferromagnetic layers. The long-ranged odd-frequency triplet amplitudes are consequently modified by the orbital response, which alters the underlying quasiparticle states by changing their momentum-space structure. Finally, our framework can be naturally generalized to other superconducting spintronic systems, possibly including Josephson junctions and altermagnet/superconductor heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study calculates the Berry curvature dipole (BCD) at GaAs(110) surfaces, demonstrating that surface-bulk hybridization is crucial for its existence. The authors show that structural relaxation causes surface states to hybridize with the bulk continuum, significantly enhancing the BCD. This work highlights the importance of surface effects in determining quantum geometric responses at material boundaries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state band theory, the concept of Berry curvature and its dipole moment relates to geometric phases and topological responses, which are fundamental concepts in quantum dynamics and can inspire models for engineered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how symmetry breaking at crystalline boundaries, specifically at GaAs(110) surfaces, enables electronic responses like a finite Berry curvature dipole (BCD) that are forbidden in the bulk.

**Main result.** Surface relaxation promotes hybridization between bulk and surface states, which generates pronounced Berry-curvature hot spots and leads to an overall enhancement of the BCD.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, followed by a Wannier projection scheme to calculate the BCD and decompose its contributions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the GaAs(110) surface slab. The electronic structure is modeled using a Wannier Hamiltonian derived from DFT calculations on slabs with a tetragonal unit cell.

**Key observables.** Berry curvature dipole (BCD), surface polarization parameter $\eta(\mathbf{k})$, and the energy dependence of BCD components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to surface relaxation effects and the hybridization strength between surface-localized and bulk-like states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The BCD calculation uses the zero-temperature limit approximation, and the analysis assumes the BCD is highly sensitive to surface relaxation and hybridization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure and symmetry changes upon surface relaxation, the electronic band structure showing gap states, and energy/layer-resolved plots showing the BCD contributions from interband coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the symmetry constraints leading to a zero BCD in the bulk, then details how surface relaxation introduces finite BCD. It proceeds to decompose the BCD contributions (orbital, layer, interband) to show that hybridization is the key mechanism for enhancement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Symmetry breaking at crystalline boundaries can enable electronic responses that are forbidden in the bulk. Here, we investigate the Berry curvature dipole (BCD) at GaAs(110) surfaces using first-principles calculations. While the BCD vanishes in bulk GaAs due to symmetry constraints, the reduced surface symmetry permits a finite dipole. We show that surface relaxation promotes hybridization between bulk and surface states, which strongly influences the surface BCD. Specifically, in unrelaxed slabs, the BCD arises predominantly from interband coupling among exponentially localized surface states lying within the projected bulk band gap. Surface relaxation shifts these states into the energy range of the bulk continuum, transforming them into surface resonances and promoting hybridization with bulk-like states. This hybridization generates pronounced Berry-curvature hot spots, resulting in an overall enhancement of the BCD. Our results highlight the importance of surface relaxation for accurately describing surface--bulk hybridization, which in turn plays a key role in Berry-curvature-driven responses at crystalline boundaries.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the first observation of superconductivity in compressed TaRhTe4, comparing its pathway to a known material, TaIrTe4. By analyzing transport and structural data under high pressure, the authors conclude that superconductivity in this family of Weyl semimetals is not governed by a single critical point but emerges through distinct, material-specific electronic reconstructions into a multiband metallic state.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the detailed study of pressure-induced topological transitions and the emergence of superconductivity from a reconstructed metallic state offers insights into non-equilibrium many-body physics relevant to strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how external pressure drives Weyl semimetals toward superconductivity by inducing distinct reconstructions in their lattice and electronic structures.

**Main result.** Superconductivity in the TaXTe4 family emerges through distinct, material-specific reconstruction pathways into a multiband metallic regime, rather than requiring a unique critical pressure or single Fermi-surface evolution.

**Method.** The research combines high-pressure transport measurements (resistance, Hall effect, magnetoresistance) with structural characterization (synchrotron X-ray diffraction) and first-principles electronic structure calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves the TaRhTe4 and TaIrTe4 compounds, which are type-II Weyl semimetals belonging to the TaXTe4 family. The study examines the electronic and structural evolution under extreme compression.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature (Tc), zero resistance, Hall coefficient (Rh), magnetoresistance (MR), lattice distortion, and the density of states at the Fermi level (N(EF)).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (up to 66 GPa), temperature (down to 4 K), and the material identity (Rh vs. Ir).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed superconducting behavior is intrinsically linked to the structural and electronic reconstruction induced by pressure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature and magnetic-field dependence of resistance showing superconducting transitions in TaRhTe4. Other figures detail the pressure evolution of Hall resistance, magnetoresistance, and structural parameters, mapping out the crossover regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements on TaRhTe4, detailing the superconducting onset coinciding with lattice distortion and electronic changes. It then contrasts this behavior with TaIrTe4, culminating in a general conclusion about the mechanism of superconductivity in the TaXTe4 family.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pressure can drive Weyl semimetals toward superconductivity through qualitatively distinct reconstructions of their lattices and normal-state electronic structures. Here, we report the first observation of superconductivity in compressed TaRhTe4. This finding enables a direct comparison of the distinct reconstruction pathways leading to superconductivity in TaRhTe4 and the previously studied TaIrTe4, both of which belong to the TaXTe4 (X = Rh, Ir) family of type-II Weyl semimetals. For TaRhTe4, high-pressure electrical-resistance, Hall effect, and magnetoresistance measurements, together with synchrotron X-ray diffraction and first-principles calculations, reveal a superconducting transition that emerges near 20 GPa, with onset Tc increasing to approximately 2.6 K at 65.2 GPa and zero resistance achieved above 63 GPa. The onset of superconductivity coincides with a progressive lattice distortion, a strong suppression of the positive magnetoresistance, and a continuous decrease of the Hall coefficient toward zero. Calculations further show that additional electron-like bands cross the Fermi level (EF) and that N(EF) increases upon compression. This evolution contrasts with TaIrTe4, where the Hall coefficient initially increases before reversing its pressure dependence near the superconducting threshold, while the structural anomaly is confined to a narrower pressure interval. This comparison indicates that superconductivity in the TaXTe4 family is not tied to a unique critical pressure or a single Fermi-surface reconstruction, but can emerge through distinct material-specific pathways once pressure sufficiently reconstructs the low-carrier Weyl-semimetal-derived state into a multiband metallic regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study models the proximity spin-orbit coupling in graphene placed on TMDs using a tight-binding approach. By systematically varying the twist angle and incorporating the effects of charge-transfer dipoles and band alignment, the authors demonstrate that these electrostatic factors critically govern the resulting SOC parameters. The work provides a framework to reconcile discrepancies in the literature regarding these crucial quantum material properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit theoretical modeling of proximity effects in 2D heterostructures, particularly linking band alignment and interface dipoles to fundamental symmetries like the Rashba phase, is highly relevant for designing quantum materials for spintronics and topological quantum computing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the local electrostatic environment influences the proximity-induced spin-orbit coupling (SOC) in graphene when placed on transition-metal dichalcogenide (TMD) heterostructures.

**Main result.** The SOC parameters, including spin-valley and Rashba terms, are strongly dependent on the twist angle and electrostatic tuning, suggesting that band alignment variations are key contributors to discrepancies in existing literature.

**Method.** The authors employ a tight-binding (TB) approach using an effective 4-band model for graphene coupled to a full TMD model, systematically varying parameters like twist angle and charge-transfer dipoles.

**Model / system.** The system is a graphene/TMD heterostructure (e.g., MoS2, WSe2). The model uses a full TB Hamiltonian incorporating graphene, TMD, and interlayer coupling terms, which is then reduced to an effective model to capture proximity-induced SOC effects.

**Key observables.** Spin-valley (SV) and Rashba (R) SOC energies, the Rashba phase ($\varphi_R$), and the spin-orbit gap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), material composition ($	ext{MoS}_2, 	ext{MoSe}_2$, etc.), band alignment ($E_C, E_V$), and charge-transfer dipole strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis isolates the effects of the charge-transfer dipole and band alignment, treating the dipole as a minimal interface on-site correction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the supercell structure for a twisted graphene/MoS2 configuration. Other figures likely compare band structures derived from the full and effective TB models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically constructs and analyzes a full TB model for the heterostructure, derives an effective low-energy model for graphene, and then analyzes the dependence of key SOC parameters on external electrostatic tuning and structural parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the proximity-induced spin-orbit coupling (SOC) in graphene on transition-metal dichalcogenide (TMD) heterostructures using a tight-binding approach. The tight-binding parameters of an effective 4-band model describing the low-energy physics of the graphene layer are extracted as a function of twist angle, material composition (MoS${}_2$, MoSe${}_2$, WS${}_2$, WSe${}_2$), band alignment, and charge-transfer dipole. We investigate the effect of a charge-transfer dipole induced on the chalcogen layer closest to graphene, which is typically neglected in tight-binding models but included in ab initio calculations through charge redistribution. In addition, we explore the influence of variations in the band alignment. We find that the spin-valley and Rashba spin-orbit coupling energies as well as the so-called Rashba phase, governing the orientation of momentum-space spin textures, show a strong dependence on the twist angle and electrostatic tuning. Our results suggest that variations in band alignment and charge redistribution contribute to the spread of spin-orbit parameters reported in the literature.

</details>
