**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the existence of self-accelerating, nonspreading wave packets in the Schrödinger equation modified by higher even-order dispersion terms. The authors prove that the characteristic self-acceleration observed in the standard quadratic case (Airy wave packet) does not generalize to the pure quartic case. Instead, the wave packet forms an accelerating caustic but always disperses over time.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical constraints on the types of localized, non-dispersing wave dynamics possible in systems with higher-order dispersion, which is crucial for understanding wave propagation in nonlinear optics or quantum field theory models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether an accelerating, nonspreading wave packet solution exists for the Schrödinger equation when higher even-order dispersion (like quartic dispersion) is present, contrasting this with the known quadratic case.

**Main result.** The self-accelerating Airy phenomenon is specific to quadratic dispersion; for pure quartic or higher even-order dispersion, the wave packet exhibits an accelerating caustic but always disperses, contradicting the 'nonspreading' requirement.

**Method.** The analysis uses ansatz solutions, Fourier transforms, and the method of numerical steepest descent to analyze the asymptotic behavior of the wave packet.

**Model / system.** The model is the time-dependent Schrödinger equation with higher even-order dispersion, specifically $i\partial_t u = (\partial_x^{2n} + V(x, t))u$, analyzed for the free case ($V=0$).

**Key observables.** Caustic formation location ($x \sim t^{2n}$), wave packet amplitude decay rate ($\sim t^{-1/2n}$ for the principal lobe), and the existence of a nonspreading profile.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the dispersion operator ($2n$), the initial data (e.g., $	ext{Ai}_{2n}(x)$), and the time $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a general shape-preserving ansatz and relies on asymptotic expansions near caustics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the propagation of the wave packet for quartic and sixth-order dispersion, showing the accelerating caustic structure alongside the predicted dispersive decay of the amplitude.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the known quadratic case, then analyzes the general ansatz for higher even-order dispersion, deriving conditions that rule out rigidly accelerating solutions, and finally confirms dispersive decay rates for the free quartic case.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Probably not. For Airy-type initial data, although nearly all lobes accelerate, the principal lobe remains essentially stationary. Moreover, the overall wave packet gradually disperses.

</details>

### [On Energy References when a Magnetic Field is Applied. A Practical Guide to Reference Energies with and without a Magnetic Field](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25473v1)

**Authors:** Pedro Pereyra  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.25473v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2609.25473_figures/2609.25473_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Schematic representation of reference energies. In a) the reservoir and electronic Fermi energy alignment. In b) the red square in a) enlarged to show the local representation of the parabolic magnetic confinement, Landau energy levels, the bottom energy E0 and the minimum energy Emin of the parabolic well. We also indicate the energy spacings that are fixed (independent of the magnetic field) and the field dependent spacing between the minimum and the bottom energy.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed theoretical analysis to resolve the ambiguity of energy references when studying Landau quantization in a magnetic field. It demonstrates that while the confining potential minimum varies with the magnetic field, the Fermi level and the bottom energy of the Landau spectrum remain fixed references. This clarification offers a simpler, more consistent picture for interpreting quantum Hall measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework for interpreting experimental measurements in quantum Hall systems, clarifying how to correctly set the energy zero point when analyzing field-dependent spectra.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the ambiguity in defining the appropriate energy reference point when analyzing Landau quantization in a 2D electron system subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field.

**Main result.** It shows that while the Landau level spacing and the parabolic well minimum depend explicitly on the magnetic field, the Fermi level ($E_F$) and the bottom energy ($E_0$) can be shown to remain fixed references for a given system.

**Method.** The analysis involves comparing the magnetic field dependence of various energy levels ($E_n$, $E_0$, $E_{\min}$) using theoretical derivations based on the physical constraints of the system.

**Model / system.** The model is a charged particle confined in a two-dimensional system (like graphene) subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field, leading to the formation of discrete Landau energy levels.

**Key observables.** Landau energy levels ($E_n$), bottom energy ($E_0$), confining potential minimum ($E_{\min}$), and the Fermi level ($E_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field strength ($B$), cyclotron frequency ($\omega_c$), and the system-dependent field $B_1$ defined by $E_1(B_1) = E_F$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard Landau quantization framework is assumed, and the Fermi level is fixed by an external reservoir in thermal equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the energy reference alignment in two parts: (a) the general setup, and (b) the local representation showing the relationship between $E_0$, $E_{\min}$, and the Landau levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing the dependence of the key energy components ($E_n$, $E_0$, $E_{\min}$) on the magnetic field $B$, ultimately establishing which references remain constant and which vary.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a simple analysis of the energy references and of the magnetic-field-dependent and system-dependent features of the Landau energy levels. The energy reference used in describing Landau quantization is usually taken as given, with the Fermi levels of the electron system and the coupled reservoir aligned in thermal equilibrium. Nevertheless, the dependence of the Landau-level spectrum on magnetic field can make the relation between these energy references less transparent. In this work, we show that when a magnetic field is applied and the confined Landau levels $E_n$ are established, both the Fermi level $E_F$ and the bottom energy $E_0$ remain fixed when the magnetic field varies. We show that the bottom energy $E_0$ is independent of $B$, and determined by a system-dependent field $B_1$, defined by the condition that the first Landau level equals the Fermi energy. In contrast, the minimum of the parabolic well depends explicitly on the magnetic field. The resulting picture provides a simple way of displaying the relative motion of the Landau levels and the fixed Fermi level without introducing a magnetic-field-dependent energy zero.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically investigates the high-pressure behavior of ReO3, revealing that bulk superconductivity is confined to a specific rhombohedral crystal structure. By combining high-pressure X-ray diffraction with DFT calculations, the authors demonstrate that the structural stability and the resulting strong electron-phonon coupling are the key factors determining the superconducting critical temperature.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on conventional BCS superconductivity, the detailed coupling between structural phase transitions (lattice dynamics) and the superconducting pairing mechanism provides insights into how geometric constraints can stabilize exotic quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is understanding how the lattice geometry dictates the emergence and suppression of superconductivity in oxides like ReO3 under extreme pressure.

**Main result.** Bulk superconductivity is robustly stabilized within the rhombohedral R-3c phase, which is linked to strong electron-phonon coupling; this superconductivity is lost when the material undergoes a structural reconstruction to a lower-symmetry phase at higher pressures.

**Method.** The study combines high-pressure experimental techniques (X-ray diffraction, transport, susceptibility) with advanced theoretical calculations (DFT, DFPT) to map the structural and electronic phase diagram.

**Model / system.** The system is Rhenium trioxide (ReO3), an oxide perovskite, whose structural evolution (Pm-3m $ightarrow$ Im-3 $ightarrow$ R-3c $ightarrow$ mP16-P2/c) and superconducting properties are tracked as pressure increases up to 80 GPa.

**Key observables.** Superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$), structural phases (e.g., $Rar{3}c$), electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda$), and lattice parameters/angles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pressure (up to 80 GPa), $T_c$ (max $\sim 17.5$ K), and the octahedral rotation angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting state is primarily governed by electron-phonon coupling, and structural transitions dictate the electronic properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the pressure dependence of $T_c$, resistance ratio, unit cell volume, and Raman modes across the different structural phases of ReO3.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the structural phase transitions of ReO3 under pressure, identifying the stable superconducting phase ($Rar{3}c$) and using DFT calculations to explain the enhancement of electron-phonon coupling within this specific lattice geometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how lattice geometry enables superconductivity in oxides remains a central challenge. Here, we report a systematic study of ReO3 up to 80 GPa. Synchrotron X-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy, electrical transport, dc magnetic susceptibility, and first-principles calculations establish a sequence of pressure-induced structural transitions, from cubic Pm-3m to Im-3 followed by the emergence of a rhombohedral R-3c phase accompanied by bulk superconductivity with a maximum Tc, onset ~17.5 K. DC magnetic susceptibility and trapped-flux magnetization measurements demonstrate that bulk superconductivity is confined to the pressure range where R-3c phase is dominant. Density functional theory calculations show strong electron-phonon coupling in the hR24-R-3c structure, with substantial contributions from both low-frequency Re vibrations and high-frequency oxygen-related phonon modes, yielding a calculated Tc comparable with the experiment. Upon further compression above ~35-40 GPa, powder X-ray diffraction results indicate a symmetry-lowering structural transition. Whereas the experimental diffraction patterns can be best described by a rhombohedral-derived R32-like average distortion with effective enlargement of the crystallographic unit cell, enthalpy calculations identify a lower-symmetry mP16-P2/c structure driven by phonon instability of the R-3c phase. This reconstructed higher-coordination phase has a reduced density of states at the Fermi level, weaker electron-phonon coupling, and a much lower calculated Tc, providing a microscopic explanation for the loss of bulk superconductivity in the higher-pressure phase. These results show that bulk superconductivity is stabilized within the rhombohedral structure, where pressure-induced lattice reconstruction supports enhanced electron-phonon coupling through cooperative Re-O lattice dynamics.

</details>
